# Rulings owed

Questions a fix ran into and could not answer alone, for which no other paper
had room. Each one is filed first with `fli file --sev decision --blocks
<row>`, which writes a row in `ISSUES.md` § Needs a decision and sets the fix's
row aside in `fli next`. Its options then go here, in a bullet whose lead names
that row's id. `fli decide` answers the bullet under the row's own id and closes
the row, so the held row comes back naming its ruling.

A question an existing paper argues belongs in that paper's `## Open questions`,
not here.

## Open questions

- ~~**FJS-D1965 — How does `fli deploy` with `BASECAMP_APP_ID` reach the dev, stage and production App?**~~ **Answered 2026-11-06 (`FJS-D1965`): A — one key, resolved: `BASECAMP_APP_ID` names any App of the project, and the target is the App with the same slug in that project's environment of the target's tier. One resolver, beside `resolveTarget`, that every `BASECAMP_APP_ID` reader calls; a tier with no environment or no same-slug App is refused by name.** Over ssh, `resolveTarget` picks `--production`, then `--stage` or a `stage` branch, else dev; with `BASECAMP_APP_ID` set, `fli deploy` refuses those flags because one id names one App. Basecamp models project → environment (`tier`: development, staging, production) → App (`slug`), and `/apps/<id>` answers `environment.projectId` and `environment.tier`.
  - **A** — one key, resolved: `BASECAMP_APP_ID` names any App of the project, and the target is the App with the same slug in that project's environment of the target's tier. One resolver, beside `resolveTarget`, that every `BASECAMP_APP_ID` reader calls; a tier with no environment or no same-slug App is refused by name.
  - **B** — one key per target: `BASECAMP_APP_ID` for dev, `BASECAMP_STAGE_APP_ID`, `BASECAMP_PRODUCTION_APP_ID`. Cost: three keys to keep right, and a stale one releases to the wrong App.
  - **C** — `BASECAMP_PROJECT_ID`, the App found by kind. Cost: an environment holds a static sibling and can hold several containers, so kind is no join key.
  - **Recommend A** — it adds no key and derives the App from what Basecamp already holds; `FJS-D1717` and `FJS-3938` refuse rather than fall back, and so does this. Cost: bare `fli deploy` on an App made before this targets development and refuses until that environment exists.

- ~~**FJS-D1966 — Does `fli basecamp:app` take the deploy target flags and add an environment to an existing project?**~~ **Answered 2026-11-06 (`FJS-D1966`): A — the same flags as `fli deploy`: bare makes development, `--stage` staging, `--production` production. With the project already there (named by `BASECAMP_APP_ID`) it adds the environment and a same-slug App placed on a server, and refuses a tier that already holds one. `fli deploy`'s refusal names this command.** It makes a project, a `production` environment and an App, and refuses when `BASECAMP_APP_ID` is set or the slug is taken, so no command makes a second environment.
  - **A** — the same flags as `fli deploy`: bare makes development, `--stage` staging, `--production` production. With the project already there (named by `BASECAMP_APP_ID`) it adds the environment and a same-slug App placed on a server, and refuses a tier that already holds one. `fli deploy`'s refusal names this command.
  - **B** — keep it production-only; another environment is made by hand in Basecamp.
  - **Recommend A** — making an environment needs a placement, which is this command's job; one target rule across both commands.

- **FJS-D1090 — Does Button's `size` prop scale its padding, or only its font?** `size` sets an inline `font-size` (`xs` 0.75rem, `sm` 0.8125rem, `md` none, `lg` 1rem), but `.btn` pads with `var(--control-padding-block, var(--space-sm)) var(--space-xl)` and `line-height: 1.25rem`. `--space-*` is `calc(<n>rem * var(--density))` and the line height is rem, so a smaller button gets smaller text in the same 8px/14px box and the same 20px line. `Button.mesa`'s header comment says the padding scales because `.btn` is em-sized; only `.btn.square` (`0.45em`) is. The rem padding is deliberate: `tokens.css` § Control height shares `--control-padding-block` with `.field` so an input and the button beside it are the same height.
  - **A** — em padding for the non-`md` sizes only: `.btn` reads `--btn-pad-block` / `--btn-pad-inline` with the current rem tokens as the fallback, and `Button` sets them in em for `xs`, `sm` and `lg`. `md` is unchanged, so it still matches `.field` and still follows `.dense`/`.roomy`. Cost: two knobs, and `xs`/`sm`/`lg` stop following density.
  - **B** — `size` also sets `--density` on the button (`xs` ≈ 0.7, `sm` ≈ 0.85, `lg` ≈ 1.15), so the existing axis does the work and no token is added. Cost: `--density` inherits, so it also shrinks anything inside the button (an icon, a nested badge), and the sizes become magic multipliers.
  - **C** — keep the rem box and fix only the comment: a size is a font size and the box is the control height. Cost: `xs` is not a compact button, and the workbench Layout button needs `.dense` or a `ghost` treatment to look small.
  - **Recommend A** — it keeps the one thing the shared padding token exists for (an `md` button level with an input) and makes the other three sizes mean what the name says. B reuses an axis that was designed for a region, not for one element, and leaks into the button's children. Whichever is ruled, the `Button.mesa` comment is wrong today and changes with it.

- **FJS-D871 — Is a non-numeric count (`?$limit=abc`) refused by name rather than dropped?** The toolbelt spec pins *dropped* (`toolbelt/test/specs/directives.spec.js` L67-69); `FJS-D237`'s reasoning, that an unknown `$`-key is refused by name because silence is not what this codebase does elsewhere, says refuse. `IDEAS/spec-directives.md` refuses and marks it. Fable audit run 4.2.
  - **A** — refuse, 400 by name (`$limit must be digits`), the same owner and shape as the unknown-key refusal; the spec vector changes.
  - **B** — drop to the default, as pinned, and the spec says so; a typo in a count is a default page, not an error.
  - **C** — drop on a URL (a bookmark should not 400) and refuse on a typed request body or socket bag, the split `FJS-D237` already makes for unknown keys.
  - **Recommend A** — C is the one that matches D237's letter, but D237's split exists because a URL cannot carry a refusal to a browser tab, and a count is typed by a program, not a person; one answer predicts more than two.

- **FJS-D870 — Does a legacy port get a data-move primitive, or is a mover an app script by `FJS-D518`'s reasoning?** `litestone import` converts the schema and nothing moves the rows, so remnant (`db/corpus/`), ela (Phase 3) and maidtech (`db/move/`, `bun run db:move`) each hand-rolled the same mover: `createMany` as the system principal, 1,000 a batch, epoch-ms → ISO, blank → null, a refused batch retried row by row, `litestone validate` after. `FJS-2037`, `2038`, `2039`, `2043` are what the ela move found on the way. Fable audit run 5.1.
  - **A** — an app script, as D518 ruled a data migration: the framework documents the shape once (a `docs/` page with the six steps and their reasons) and ships nothing.
  - **B** — a `fli db:move` that takes a source (SQLite file, CSV directory, or a `.lite` plus rows) and a field map, and runs the six steps as the system principal with the validate pass built in.
  - **C** — a litestone `seeder.move(source, map)` primitive the app script calls, so the steps are owned once and the per-port map stays the app's.
  - **Recommend C** — three ports proved the steps are the same and the map is not; A restates the steps in every port, B owns the map the framework cannot know.

- **FJS-D864 — What severity does `doc-examples-run` carry while 19 packages have no doc-example runner?** The rule (checks.js, repo scope, Invariant 16) grades WIRING: a package whose docs fence an unmarked runnable block must reach a `test/doc-examples.test.*` from its test script. It landed as `warn` on 2026-10-10 (Fable audit run 2.4) and fires 19 times; litestone's runner is the enforcer row. Refines `FJS-D860`: the extraction that moved to `fli check` is the wiring check, the runner stays per package.
  - **A** — `warn` now; `FJS-2331` is the ratchet, and the rule flips to `error` the day the count is 0.
  - **B** — `error` plus 19 named `structure` allowances in `scripts/ci-allowances.json`, each *runner not yet written*, removed as runners land; a stale one is reported by the engine.
  - **C** — `error` plus a per-rule baseline for the repo scope; `check-baseline.json` is app-scoped today, so this adds a mechanism.
  - **Recommend A** — B's 19 allowances each restate the row; C adds a mechanism to answer a count a row already holds. A's cost is the `FJS-D859` argument (a warn is read by whoever already knows), bounded because `fli next` ranks `FJS-2331` and the structure phase prints the 19 lines on every run.

- **FJS-D863 — Does the register re-file the rows run 3.2 names at the severity the legend gives them?** The S1 table is empty while the legend (Conventions, *S1 blocker — data loss, a security hole, or cannot ship*) describes rows sitting in S2–S4, so `fli next` ranks two confirmed tooling defects (`FJS-2366`, `FJS-2368`) above every access hole. Fable audit run 3.2, 2026-10-10. Each is one row and one decision; the list is the audit's proposal, not consent.
  - **Up to S1** — `FJS-2232`, `FJS-1725`, `FJS-1503`, `FJS-1743`, `FJS-1799`, `FJS-1660` (each a security hole by the legend; each waits on a ruling, which argues for filing them where the owner sees them).
  - **Up to S2** — `FJS-2334`, `FJS-2335` (a host page reaches a service call with the user's token) · `FJS-1656` (`@guarded` plaintext through a view) · `FJS-1438` (write escalation by foreign-key move, measured twice) · `FJS-1838` (TOTP oracle, no ceiling) · `FJS-1513`, `FJS-1490` (silently wrong on day one, every app pays) · `FJS-2140`, `FJS-1271` (stored-shape corruption that compounds).
  - **Up to S3** — `FJS-2228` (`'false'` grades 7) · `FJS-1847` (its own test is named oauth-link-hijack) · `FJS-1592` (address included, installation-wide) · `FJS-1297` (the audit trail committed on first `git add`) · `FJS-1853` (verify-request mail bomb from a public route).
  - **Down to S3** — `FJS-2084` (its own text: no user is broken today) · `FJS-2366`, `FJS-2368` (a missed drive, not a wrong answer to a user) · `FJS-1927` (reasoned from the code, not probed) until probed.
  - **Re-probe before ranking** — `FJS-053`, `FJS-266`, `FJS-268`, `FJS-274`, `FJS-373`, `FJS-503`, `FJS-555`, `FJS-610`, and `FJS-009`'s own body, all last verified in August.
  - **A** — re-file the ups and downs as listed, one `fli amend` each.
  - **B** — add a `fli register:check` rule that refuses a row whose title names a hole (a token list) below S2, and let the rule drive the re-file.
  - **C** — leave the severities; change `fli next` to weigh a blocked row's severity and keep blocked rows ranked (`FJS-2409`, `FJS-2410`), which is what put the tooling rows on top.
  - **Recommend A then C** — the legend is the rule and the rows break it; C is owed anyway and does not fix a mis-filed row.

- **FJS-D861 — Does `FJS-D291` still hold once the in-app row is weighed?** D291 lets an activated flow notify any recipient of any notification the app opts in through `orion({ notifications })`, weighing mail: the flow sends nothing the app did not write. The `notify` node also writes an in-app row (`orion/src/plugin.ts` L169-179 → `notifications/drivers/inapp.ts` L59, as the system) whose `type` picks the browser's renderer and whose `data` is the flow's payload passed through the definition untyped (`notifications/types.ts` L28, `data?: Record<string, unknown>`) — so a flow activated at 5 puts a `PaymentReceived` with its own amount into any user's bell. Fable audit run 1.5. The owner of what a payload may be is `defineNotification`, not orion (D290's shape: the handler grades its own input).
  - **A** — D291 holds: activation at 5 is the line, and a 5 that lies in a bell is a 5 that lies in mail.
  - **B** — a definition declares its payload shape and `define()` refuses a payload off it; orion compiles a `notify` node's payload against the same declaration. Owned by notifications, derived by orion.
  - **C** — a flow-authored row is marked (`source: flow`, rendered as such), so a bell entry says who wrote it. A new column and a new noun.
  - **Recommend B** — one owner, derived not restated, and it is the sibling of D290 with the grading where that ruling put it.

- **FJS-D572 — What does `verifyGateLadder` return when some rows are mismatches and some are gates it could not ask: a `{ mismatches, ungraded }` object, an `ungraded: 'throw' | 'return'` option, or a filtered array with the rest read from the env?** `FJS-1213`. Red test, measured: a two-model schema — `Slot` with `@@check("startTime < endTime")` and `@@gate("1.4.4.5")`, `Note` with `@@gate("1.2.2.5")` and `@@allow('create', ownerId == auth().id)` — makes `await env.verifyGateLadder()` return 29 rows (`Slot:error` 23, `Note:skipped` 6) and zero gate defects, so the generated `expect(await env.verifyGateLadder()).toEqual([])` is red. `testing.js:610–638` pushes `error` (no fixture) and `skipped` (policy / required `@guarded`) into the same `mismatches` array as a real `allow`/`deny` disagreement. The same mixed shape is in `verifyRowPolicies` and `verifyTenantIsolation` (`got: 'skipped'`), `mutationScore` already sorts the rows apart by `INCONCLUSIVE` (`mutate.js:362`), and every caller (`litestone.test.ts`, `view-access.test.ts`, `basecamp`, `orion`, the `fli new` scaffold, `tutor`) asserts `toEqual([])`.
  - **A** — `{ mismatches, ungraded }`: the ladder returns an object, `mismatches` holds only `allow`/`deny` disagreements and a thrown non-refusal at a level whose fixture DID build, `ungraded` holds `skipped` and *no fixture could be built*. The generated assertion becomes `expect((await env.verifyGateLadder()).mismatches).toEqual([])`, and an app that wants the count holds `ungraded.length` steady in a second assertion. Every caller moves in the same change, and the other two verifiers take the same shape or are left as a follow-up row.
  - **B** — `verifyGateLadder({ ungraded: 'return' })`: the array stays and the default is the strict one, so a bare call returns mismatches only and `'return'` appends the non-gradings. The return type then depends on an option, and the default hides unaskable gates from the one assertion that is the app's whole view of this tool.
  - **C** — the array is mismatches only, and `env.ungradedLadder()` (or `env.ladderReport`) holds the last run's non-gradings. No call site changes, but it is a second call and a hidden dependency on call order, and a mutation run reads a stale report.
  - **Recommend A** — two channels is the row's own diagnosis, and a returned object keeps the non-gradings visible in the same call that produced them. Which rows leave `ungraded` for good (the synthetic-principal FK sentinel as in `FJS-1200`, `@immutable` columns as in `FJS-1487`, a `@@check` the auto-factory cannot satisfy) is separate fixture work each cause owns.

- **FJS-D682 — How does a Resource say a read never changes and may be held for the tab?** `FJS-1891`: remnant memoizes `verses.service.invoke('passage' | 'chapter', key)` in a module `Map` because the corpus ships with the release and nothing writes it; `list-cache.js` answers only after a failed call and only for an `@@sync` model, and `FJS-D625` sends a cache of server reads to the Resource. The Resource already holds `_read` and `_options` and clears both in `resetResourcesForIdentityChange`, so the held reads must join that epoch. The red test: two `service.invoke('passage', 'Gen.1')` calls hit the transport twice today; declared immutable, once.
  - **A** — a Resource option naming the methods: `createResource('Verse', { once: ['passage', 'chapter'] })`. The held answer is keyed by method, id and query, lives in the Resource beside `_options`, and clears on the identity epoch. No Litestone change; the declaration sits with the call it caches (a custom method, which the schema does not describe).
  - **B** — a Model attribute (`@@static`): the schema says no write path exists, `x-static` reaches the Resource, and every read of that Model is held. Access-style facts live in the schema (Invariant 6), but a custom method's answer is not a row, so the attribute would have to say which methods, and a Litestone noun is spent on a client cache.
  - **C** — a per-call flag, `invoke(name, id, data, query, { once: true })`. Smallest, but it is the module `Map` moved one line, and each call site must remember to say it.
  - **D** — the service declares it where the method is declared: junction's `methods:` list already carries `read: true` per custom method (`FJS-D505`, `packages/junction/src/core/service.ts`), and an `immutable: true` beside it would reach the Resource through the service description, so the fact has its origin with the code that knows the data ships with the release. Costs a transport the Resource does not have today (it learns no per-method metadata from the server) and a key on the method declaration.
  - **Recommend A** — the immutable thing here is a method's answer, which only the Resource's author knows; A puts it where `options()` already caches, behind the same identity epoch, without a schema word. D is the better origin and the larger build: take it when a second client (jetty, mcp) wants the same hold, and delete A's option in the same change so the fact keeps one origin.

- **FJS-D683 — What do `backfill` and `split` look like in the operations document, and where in a created file do they run?** `FJS-1787` has `rename` built (`packages/litestone/src/core/operations.js`, `OPERATIONS = { rename }`) and `FJS-D603` rules the family but not the spellings. The file generator has two slots a rename never needed: a rebuild's `INSERT … SELECT` names only columns the old table shares (`generateRebuildSQL`, `migrate.js` ~1215-1250, an added column is OMITTED so its DEFAULT fills it), so a new `NOT NULL` link has no way to take a value; and `first` runs before everything, so a `split` has no point between the new model's `CREATE TABLE` and the old model's rebuild that drops the column. Red test: answer → emit → create → apply over 3 seeded `Job` rows with a required `companyId` added; today apply fails or the column is NULL, and a `Page.body` moved to `Block` reads 0 of 8 bodies back.
  - **A** — Two entries in the one document, each stating its source: `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, value }` (a constant), `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, first: 'Company' }` (the first parent row; refused when the parent is empty), `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, create: { Company: { name: 'Imported' } } }` (one new parent every row links to); and `{ op: 'split', model: 'Page', column: 'body', into: 'Block', field: 'body', link: 'pageId' }`. Litestone adds two slots to `generateMigrationSQL`: a per-column value expression in the rebuild copy, and a `split` slot after new tables, before rebuilds. The shadow's count check is a `-- check` row count after each. Oracle's answer gains `backfill` on an added link and `moves` on a field. Whole of D603's step 2 and 3 in one ruling.
  - **B** — Same document, built in two passes: `backfill` (constant and `first`) now, `split` and `create` parent after. Oracle's answer refuses a move it cannot carry and asks for `risk: migration` with an `escape`, which the contract already has. Smaller, and the row stays open for the second pass.
  - **C** — Oracle-side only: an answer that adds a required link to an entity the app already holds emits it OPTIONAL (`?`) with a `findings` note, and a move is refused. No litestone change; the person tightens it later. The cost is that a model drops a required link the answer meant, and `emit()` still knows nothing of rows, so it must be told which entities are populated.
  - **Recommend B** — `value` and `first` fix the NOT NULL half-apply in both measured cases with one new slot; `split` needs the second slot and the ordering rule, and is better ruled with the first working in front of it.

- **FJS-D684 — What does `SendResult.id` name: the Message-ID on both mailers, the provider's id on both, or a second field for the one it is not?** `FJS-2125`: `createSmtpMailer.send` answers `messageIdOf(msg).id` (`smtp.ts` ~556, minted on the From's domain when no `Message-ID` header is stated), `createResendMailer.send` answers `result.id` from `POST /emails`, which no header carries. An app threading a reply (`In-Reply-To`) off `send().id` works over SMTP and names nothing over Resend. Red test would assert: with the Resend `fetch` stubbed to answer `{ id: 're_123' }`, the id `send()` answers is the `Message-ID` the request body's `headers` carried; today the body carries none and `id` is `re_123`.
  - **A** — `id` is always the Message-ID. The Resend adapter mints one the way `messageIdOf` does (exported from `smtp.ts`, one owner) and sends it in `headers` unless the message states one. Smallest, `SendResult` keeps its shape, and reply threading works under either adapter. It spends Resend's own id, which a delivery webhook (`email.delivered`, `email.bounced`) names the email by, and it relies on Resend honoring a custom `Message-ID` header, which this row did not probe.
  - **B** — `SendResult` gains `messageId`, answered by both adapters (minted and sent by the Resend adapter as in A); `id` stays each provider's own handle, documented as such. Threading and webhook matching both work. Costs one more field across four `IMail`s (the two here, `createTestMailer` in `packages/testing/src/doubles.ts`, Basecamp's `createConduitMailer`), and an app reading `id` still gets two different things by adapter, now named as such.
  - **C** — change nothing in code: document on `SendResult` that `id` is adapter-defined and an app that threads replies states `Message-ID` in `headers` itself. No new noun, but `IMail` stays an interface whose one answer cannot be used without knowing the implementation.
  - **Recommend B** — `id` as the provider handle is what a webhook and a support lookup need, `messageId` is what a reply needs, and an interface that answers one of them under a name that means the other is the defect; A trades one of the two away.

- **FJS-D849 — How does a `@@softDelete` model in a retention database get an index the sweep can use: a spelling for an unfiltered `@@index`, or an index retention derives?** `FJS-2291`: `createIndexes` (`ddl.js`) ANDs `deletedAt IS NULL` into every `@@index` on a `@@softDelete` model, and `FJS-D719` has the sweep run unfiltered (`createdAt < cutoff` over soft-deleted rows too), so no index the model can declare is reachable by the sweep; `retention-without-an-index` fires and names no remedy. Red test: a `@@softDelete` model with `createdAt` in a `retention` database, `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN DELETE … WHERE createdAt < ?` reports `SCAN`, and no declarable `@@index` changes it.
  - **A** — retention derives it: a model with `createdAt` in a database with `retention` earns a FULL (unpartial) `@@index([createdAt])` with `generated: 'retention'`, as a relator earns its reverse index (`parser.js`, `generated: 'relator'`); `ddl.js` skips the soft-delete clause for it, and the advise rule stops asking. No new spelling, and the author never writes the index. Costs a b-tree the author did not ask for, on every model in the window.
  - **B** — a spelling for the unfiltered index: an `@@index` option (e.g. `all: true`) that suppresses the soft-delete AND. A new option for one reader, and the author must still remember to write it, which is the failure the advise rule exists for.
  - **C** — a declared `where` replaces the soft-delete clause when it names `deletedAt` (the author said it, as `@@unique(where:)` already works), and `@@index([createdAt], where: deletedAt == null or deletedAt != null)` is the spelling. No option, but a tautology as the spelling of "full", and the predicate must survive the partial-index reachability rules.
  - **Recommend A** — retention is already declared on the database and the sweep is the framework's own reader, so the framework owns its index; B and C leave the author to spell what the schema already implies.

- **FJS-D897 — Does a `@@transitions` move's follow-up run on every path that makes the move, or stay the commitment fire's only, with a guard on the paths that forget it?** Found fixing FJS-1995. `FJS-D368` ruled *only the fire runs a hook* (`commitments({ hooks })`, run in the fire's `$transaction` by `fireCommitment`) and rejected a litestone `onAfterWrite` reacting on every path (its B, "whether it runs inside the transaction is unmeasured"). The measured cost is chatwoot's `s.conversation.transition(id, 'reopen')` leaving the SLA clock paused, and lago's hand finalize going around `issueInvoice`. No red test exists: the code does what D368 says, so the assertion that would fail is the ruling's own clause.
  - **A** — keep D368; close the row as by design. Add to `api-hazards` that a `transition()` outside the fire owes its own follow-up, and that the app funnels every path through one function the hook also calls (lago's `issueInvoice`). Costs nothing, and the forgetting path stays silent.
  - **B** — a hook keyed by `Model.transition`, not by commitment, on the same `hooks:` option, run inside the move's transaction by every path that makes it (service, receiver, system client, fire). Needs the litestone seam to run a callback in the transition's own transaction, which `onAfterWrite` is not shown to do (measure first); the fire stops being special and `@@commitment` need not exist for a move to owe something.
  - **C** — keep the fire as the only runner and make the other paths loud: `fli check` flags a `.transition(…, '<name>')` call in an app where `commitments({ hooks })` names that model's transition, so the forgetting is caught at the diff rather than at the breach that never fires. Smaller than B; a dynamic transition name is unseen.
  - **Recommend A** — D368's reasoning (the plugin making the move is what keeps the from-state lock) still holds and B reopens a rejected option on two measured hand-path misses that one shared function already fixes in lago; revisit B only when a third stressor needs a hook on a move no commitment ever fires.

- **FJS-D898 — How does an update learn a `@capability` column's stored value, so restating it (null on an already-null row) is not charged the column grant?** Found fixing FJS-2570. `CapabilityPlugin._checkUpdate` runs in `onBeforeUpdate`, before the stored row is read, so presence of the key is the only test; the create half was fixed by comparing to what omission stores (`storesWhatOmissionStores`, `client.js`). Red test: `as(db, 'Offer.update').offer.update({ where: { id }, data: { signedName: null } })` on a row whose `signedName` is already null rejects `/Offer\.signedName/` today.
  - **A** — the plugin reads the stored row itself in `_checkUpdate` through `ctx.tables` and skips a key whose named value equals it. One file, but the read and the UPDATE are two statements outside the unit, so a row changed in between lets a caller overwrite a value they were never granted.
  - **B** — the grant is checked where the stored row is already in hand: `updatePlan` reads the row synchronously with the statement and no await between, so the check is atomic. Needs a plugin seam (a second hook, or `onBeforeUpdate` handed a `stored()` the plan calls), and a read the common path does not pay today.
  - **C** — keep presence as the test, close the row as by design, and say in `data-hazards` that a hand-written update restates only what changed. Sierra's patch already sends only changed keys, so a generated form is unaffected.
  - **Recommend C** — the only caller hurt is a hand-written update restating a value it was not granted, which can name fewer keys; A opens a check-then-write race on an access rule and B adds a plugin seam and a read to every capability update for it.

- **FJS-D900 — How does a client opened on a database that is ahead of its schema find out?** `FJS-754`: `createClient` on a tenant file another process migrated forward says nothing, and the column is invisible to it. The red test is `createClient({ schema: behind, db })` after `autoMigrate` of the ahead schema, with `console.warn` captured: it asserts one announcement naming the extra column, and today there is none (`test/schema-behind.test.ts` `behindBy` is the setup). The hash cannot decide it (`readAutoHash` carries no ordering and differs on every dev edit); `onlyDrops` can, but only after the pristine `:memory:` build the hash exists to skip.
  - **A** — superset test at open: `PRAGMA table_info` per table against the schema's columns; any live column the schema lacks (and nothing the schema adds that the live lacks) announces once per client. No stored-format change. Costs one PRAGMA per table on every `createClient`, which a tenant-per-file app pays per tenant open, and it also fires in dev for a column just removed from the schema and not yet migrated.
  - **B** — record an ordered stamp (a schema generation counter or the migration count) beside the hash in the auto-meta table, and compare at open. Exact ordering, one row read, no false positive on a dev edit. A stored-format change, and an old database carries no stamp until its next migration.
  - **C** — do nothing at open; keep `autoMigrate`'s refusal (`FJS-566`) as the only voice, and document that a process which never calls it never learns. Zero cost, and the gap stays as the row describes it.
  - **Recommend A, run only on the first open of each file and silent when `autoMigrate` follows in the same boot** — it needs no format change and the cost is one PRAGMA per table; measure it on a 100-table schema before building, and fall back to B if a tenant open cannot afford it.

- **FJS-D902 — Does the outbox promise delivery order, and if so per what key — or is unordered at-least-once the documented contract?** `FJS-893`: `deliverOutbox` claims owed rows `createdAt asc` but a failed row waits out its backoff while later rows are dispatched under their own ids and go past it; `ctx.enqueue(job, payload)` carries no ordering key and `OutboxMessage` has no column for one. Neither `example` nor `basecamp` enqueues anything order-sensitive today, so no caller is hurt yet.
  - **A** — unordered is the contract: say it in the `deliverOutbox` comment and in `api-hazards` (*a handler must not assume it sees an aggregate's effects in write order; carry a version or a timestamp in the payload and reconcile*), and close the row as by design. No new noun, no head-of-line blocking, a dead row still stalls only itself.
  - **B** — an optional `ctx.enqueue(job, payload, { orderKey })`: the row stores the key, and the relay delivers a key's rows one at a time, in `createdAt` order, each only once its predecessor is delivered. Ordered where asked and free elsewhere, but it adds an option and a column, a key's dead row stalls the whole key until an operator clears it, and the row must say what `maxAttempts` exhaustion does to the rows behind it.
  - **C** — ordering is the app's own job through a conduit target of kind broker (`FJS-D235`), where a partition key is the broker's native concept; the outbox stays unordered and the broker is where ordered consumers live.
  - **Recommend A** — no app here needs order, a key nobody has declared is a feature built for a guess, and B can be added later beside the unordered path without changing it; revisit when a stressor app names an aggregate whose effects must land in sequence.

- **FJS-D906 — Does FJS-D466's bound hold for an unordered live list whose window already holds the query's end?** FJS-2617 made an ORDERED window that holds the query's last row grow by a created row rather than trim one, since there is no page 2 for the overflow to move to. The unordered branch of `insert` (`packages/junction/src/client/index.ts`) still answers FJS-D466: at its page size it counts the row on `stale` and bumps `total`, so a drained unordered list reports `hasMore()` true while `more()` has no cursor and returns `[]` at once — a `while (list.hasMore) await list.more()` loop spins. `live-order.test.ts` › "an unordered list at its page size COUNTS a new row rather than growing" pins the bound with `total: 2, limit: 2`, which is exactly a window holding the end.
  - **A** — Grow there too: when `total`, or an explicit `hasMore: false`, says the window holds the end, the row is in the query and in the window, so append it. FJS-D466's 3003-row measurement then applies only where the server never stated an end.
  - **B** — Keep the bound, but do not count the row on `total` when the window holds the end: `stale` says *refresh*, and `hasMore()` stays false because `more()` cannot reach the row.
  - **Recommend A** — a window that holds the whole query is the query (FJS-D145), so a row that enters the query enters the window, and growth is bounded by the query, not the push count.

## Shapes — from the 2026-10-06 cross-schema review (not yet filed; `fli file --sev decision` each before building on it)

Source: *The Ten Shapes* (https://claude.ai/artifact/KFxSqaTYsKfZLcVquhfQVt) and `packages/litestone/references/` as of 2026-10-06. Each is a question a reference file answered provisionally in its own prose; the file says which way it leaned.

- **The catalog word for the container people belong to.** Nine instances, four words (`Workspace`, `Account`, `Site`, `Organization`) and none of them Oracle's; `references/Organization.lite` takes `Organization` as the catalog word with the app's word as `@@label`.
  - **A** — `Organization`, as written: matches Oracle, trigger.dev, documenso; no FrontierJS app uses it.
  - **B** — `Workspace`: what basecamp, linear and notion say, and the word `tenancy { claim workspaceId }` already carries in two prototypes.
  - **C** — no catalog word: the shape is `Member`'s relator and the container is always the app's noun.
  - **Recommend A** — a catalog word that no app uses is the point: it names the shape without claiming the label, which is what `@@label` is for.

- **Whether a trait may ship a `claim`.** `Grant` is unusable without `claim xId` + `@@allow(…, id == auth().xId)`, both the host's today because a trait cannot name the host's key.
  - **A** — no; a trait is columns and model attributes, the claim is a schema-level fact, the README says so and `grant.lite` in auth would ship the claim beside the trait.
  - **B** — yes, with a parameter: `@@trait(Grant, claim: bookingId)` expands the policy.
  - **Recommend A** — B is a language feature for one trait; the auth fragment is the shape `user.lite` already has.

- **Whether `Decision` is a trait or an attribute on the transition.** `references/Decision.lite` says it wants to be `approve: requested -> approved @stamps(decidedAt, decidedById)`.
  - **A** — trait now, attribute later; a host that spreads the trait renames nothing when the attribute arrives.
  - **B** — the attribute now, no trait: the stamp is owned by the edge that writes it, which is the one-owner invariant.
  - **Recommend A** — B is right and is litestone work with a catalog entry, a hook, and `x-transitions` to carry it; the trait costs nothing while that waits.

## Open questions — filed since the Shapes review

The Shapes section above is read as prose and holds no question `fli decisions` can see. Every ruling question filed since goes here, at the end of the file.

- **FJS-D945 — How is a relation count (litestone `include._count`, optionally filtered by a `where` on the target) spelled over the wire?** `parsePopulate` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`, 440) spells `rel`, `rel:a+b` and dotted paths and never `_count`, and `$populate` is a CSV on the wire (`write: asCsv`, `packages/toolbelt/src/directives/directives.js`, 126), so a `where` has nowhere to sit. The red test's assertion: `GET /service-areas?$populate=_count.surveys` answers each row with `_count: { surveys: n }`, and a filtered one answers n over the matching surveys only — today the key is read as a relation named `_count` and the count is absent.
  - **A** — extend the `populate` grammar: `_count.surveys` unfiltered, and a filtered count spelled with the target's own filter keys, `_count.surveys?status=open`. Keeps one directive, but it is a mini-grammar inside a CSV, and a `where` in it needs its own escaping and `$`-key refusal (Invariants 8, 10).
  - **B** — a new directive row, `$count`, in the toolbelt table (`FJS-D125`'s one place): `$count=surveys` unfiltered, `$count={"surveys":{"where":{…}}}` structured with `write` as JSON. The filter reuses `parseWhere` for the target, so the `$`-key refusal and name checks have one owner. Costs a noun (§ V.2) and one more reserved key.
  - **C** — the object form of `populate` only, on bodies that can carry one (POST/WS `{ populate: { _count: { surveys: { where } } } }`), with no query-string spelling. Smallest, but forwards a caller-built include tree to litestone and the browser client's GET path cannot ask.
  - **Recommend B** — `where` is a structure and the directive table is where a per-call option with a structured form already lives; A restates `parseWhere` inside a CSV and C leaves the common GET unable to ask. The ELA case (one count per service area, filtered by date range) needs the filter, so the unfiltered-only half is not enough alone.

- **FJS-D964 — What spells a zone-less local datetime?** eventmark 1.3: a schedule whose times have no zone by design stores its `@@exclude` range as `String @datetime` with a `Z` it does not mean, since `@datetime` is an instant and `2025-08-25T09:00` is refused. `FJS-3053` (immich EXIF `DateTimeOriginal`) needs the same value and one more thing: it is `@zoned(takenZone)` once placed, but `@zoned` is refused on anything but a `@date`/`@time` (`parser.js` @zone/@zoned validation), so the seed splits it into two columns that can be nulled apart. Whichever option is picked, say whether `@zoned` accepts the local datetime.
  - **A** — an option on the existing word, `@datetime(local: true)`, accepting `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM[:SS]` and refusing an offset; `@@exclude` grades it as text.
  - **B** — a new validator `@localdatetime` beside `@date`/`@time`: one more noun.
  - **C** — leave it; the app stores `@date` and `@time` as two columns. Keeps the vocabulary, but `@@exclude` cannot span the pair (`FJS-2781`).
  - **Recommend A** — it is the third kind `@date` and `@time` already complete, and an option on `@datetime` coins no noun.

- **FJS-D973 — Does a plugin `ready()` run before the listener binds and fail `start()` on a throw, or stay after `listen` and forgiving?** `runStartPhases` runs `listen` then `ready-hooks` (`packages/junction/src/core/app.ts`), and a throw in `ready` is `logger.error`'d while the app goes on; `docs/internals.md` § Plugin phases states "only `ready` is forgiving" as the rule. The only shipped `ready()` is devtools, which binds its own admin port there and catches its own `EADDRINUSE` (`FJS-420`). Red test, FJS-2825: a plugin whose `ready()` throws — `start()` resolves and the app answers `/health`, where a plugin whose `boot()` throws rejects `start()`.
  - **A** — keep the order and the forgiveness, and say so: `ready` is "after the app is serving", for work that must not hold the first request (devtools' port). Closes FJS-2825 as documented behavior and fixes the `CLAUDE.md` line "only a throw in `ready` lets the app start", which reads as the opposite.
  - **B** — keep `ready` after `listen`, but a throw fails `start()` (shutdown unwinds plugins as `boot-plugins` does). The app can still answer requests before another plugin's `ready` finishes, and a failed `start()` leaves a bound port to release.
  - **C** — move `ready-hooks` before `listen` and make a throw fail `start()`, so nothing is answered before every plugin is ready. Devtools' `ready()` binds its own port and does not need `app.http.port`, but any third-party `ready()` reading `app.http.port` breaks (it is unbound until `listen`), and `needsHost` for the phase must still skip it in `_startForTest()`.
  - **Recommend A** — the only caller wants forgiveness (a held devtools port must not stop the app), so B and C buy strictness nothing asks for; work that must finish before the first request already has `boot()`.

- **FJS-D974 — How does a connection get into a bound room (`channels({ rooms })`) whose row was created after its socket opened: the client asks by `subscribe`, or the server joins every live connection the new row admits?** `FJS-2807`: the only server moment that names a connection is `channels.on('connection')`, and a `subscribe` from a non-member is ignored (`transport/channels.ts`, `parsed.type === 'subscribe'`), so a `SupportThread` created after the customer's socket opened answers no `presence:sync` on `support:{id}` until the socket reconnects. The red test's assertion: open a socket, create the row, send `subscribe` on `support:{id}`, expect a `presence:sync` listing that connection.
  - **A** — a `subscribe` on a BOUND name joins, through the same `admitToRoom` the server's join takes (graded at the Data boundary, re-asked after every write); an unbound name is ignored as today. No new option and no new noun; it amends `FJS-D175`'s "subscribe does NOT join" for exactly the names `FJS-D596` made gradable. Only the tab that asks is in the room, and each ask costs one row read, bounded by the frame rate.
  - **B** — a rule on `rooms`: when a write creates a row of a bound model, grade every live connection against it and join the admitted ones. Needs no client change, but it is an option or a rule word, grades O(connections) per create, and puts every tab of every admitted person in a room none of them opened, so presence lies about who is looking.
  - **C** — keep admission the app's: document the `localConnectionsOf(userId)` scan from a service hook as the paved road. Nothing to build, but it over-joins every tab of the session and each app restates the read policy in a hook (Invariant 6).
  - **Recommend A** — membership stays server-graded by the one owner, the client only asks to be considered, and presence shows who opened the room.

- **FJS-D999 — What counts the distinct local days an instant column falls on, when SQLite has no zone database?** `FJS-2615`: `aggregate({ _count: { distinct: 'sentAt' } })` is `COUNT(DISTINCT col)` (`client.js` `aggregate`), so it counts instants, not days; ELA's contact bars key each instant through `Intl.DateTimeFormat` in a hand-written read step. Neither engine can do it in SQL exactly: `bun:sqlite` registers no user function (`rg 'createFunction' packages/litestone/src` is empty), and SQLite's date modifiers take a fixed offset, so `date(col, '-5 hours')` is wrong for the half of the year a DST zone is not on that offset. The zone is also the account's, a relation, which `_count` cannot read.
  - **A** — `_count: { distinct: { day: 'sentAt', offset: '-05:00' } }` (or a bound `$offset`): `COUNT(DISTINCT date(col, ?))`. One query, no new noun beyond the object form, but a fixed offset is the zone only outside DST, so the answer is silently an hour off across a transition — the `FJS-2170` failure again.
  - **B** — the app passes a day table: litestone grows `distinctDays(field, { zone, where })` as a helper that selects the instants (`select: { sentAt }`, graded and policy-filtered as any read) and keys them in JS through `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, DST-exact on either engine. A second read path beside `aggregate`, and O(rows) rather than one aggregate, but the rows are one parent's last 30 days.
  - **C** — nothing in litestone: this is a display-side question of the same family as `FJS-2008`, `FJS-D288` already ruled a zone is spent only where an instant and a day cross, and the toolbelt `datetime` kit is where that crossing lives (`toolbelt` grows `distinctDays(instants, zone)`). The read step stays, but calls one tested function instead of its own `Intl` keying. Closes `FJS-2615` as a toolbelt row.
  - **Recommend C** — no SQL form is exact (A) and a second read path in litestone (B) re-owns a crossing `FJS-D288` put in the kit; the caller already holds the instants, so one tested kit function removes the duplication with no new litestone surface.

- **FJS-D1000 — Should the theme switch offer a mode+accent pair, or stay one whole theme per choice?** `FJS-2513`: `applyTheme` (`packages/sierra/src/theme/index.js:266`) does `classList.add(value)` for one name from `theme.themes`, so `theme-dark` + `theme-sunset` (accent-only, `packages/css/src/themes/sunset.css`, which nests over any surface) cannot both come from the switch; `classList.add('theme-dark theme-sunset')` throws `InvalidCharacterError`. The red test: `setTheme('theme-dark theme-sunset')` with that entry declared leaves `<html>` carrying both classes and neither stale one; today it is refused or throws.
  - **A** — a `themes` entry may name several classes, space-separated. `applyTheme` splits it and removes every class of every declared entry before adding; `setTheme`, `toggleTheme`, persistence and `theme.value` keep treating the entry as one opaque choice, and `buildThemeScript` splits the same way. No new noun or option; a picker's swatch wears the entry's classes.
  - **B** — two axes: `theme: { modes, accents }` with `setMode` / `setAccent`, persisted under two keys and written as two classes. The honest model of "dark with a red accent", but two public verbs, a second persisted key and a `system` map that must say which accent follows the OS.
  - **C** — no switch change: the kit ships (or the app writes) one whole dark-with-accent theme per accent. Sierra is untouched, but it multiplies kit themes by modes and is the app stylesheet restating `theme-dark`'s tokens the row names as the cost.
  - **Recommend A** — the switch already owns a list of whole choices and the css themes already nest, so letting an entry name more than one class closes the gap without a second axis, a new verb or a second persisted key.

- **FJS-D1003 — Where does a list cell's label come from when the row holds only a key?** `columns()` names the label field (`FJS-1973`) but `list()` is built with no `include`, so `<Cell>` finds no `record[relation]` and prints the id; a `@values` enum column (`display: 'enum'`, `set/model/valueField/labelField`, no `options`) has no relation to include at all, so `enumLabel` prints `String(v)`. The red assertion: for `Order.customerId` with a `Customer.name`, `orders.columns().include` is `{ customer: true }` (today `undefined`); for a column bound `@values`, a resolved `options` list reaches the entry (today `enumLabel` has none).
  - **A** — two halves, each at its owner. `columns()` answers a top-level `include` (`{ customer: true }`, one entry per relation column it shows) and the generated list passes it as `list({ directives: { include } })`; a value-set enum is resolved by a new async `resource.columnOptions(cols)` that fills `options` from `options(field)` (one request per value set, not per row), which the page awaits before drawing. Server joins once for relations, one cached lookup per set; costs one new answer key and one new async method.
  - **B** — relations only: `columns()` answers `include` and the generated list passes it; a value-set enum stays the app's job (`column.options` from `resource.options(field)`, which `enumLabel` already honors), documented as a hazard. Smallest change, but the generated list still prints an id for every `@values` column.
  - **C** — `<Cell>` resolves its own labels, given the `resource`: a relation not on the record is read through `resource.options(relation)` and an enum through the same call, cached per resource and column. No change to `list()` or the page, but every `<Cell>` gains a resource prop and an async state, and a table of 50 rows makes the cell a data fetcher rather than a renderer.
  - **Recommend A** — `include` is already the transport's word for "carry the related row" (`FJS-D237`), `columns()` already knows which columns are relations, and `options(field)` is the existing owner of a value set's members; A adds no second place that knows what a label is, where C splits it across the cell.

- **FJS-D1005 — Where does the download of a screen's filtered list live: a prop on FilterBar/Table, an `ExportButton` component, or a resource helper?** `FJS-2835`: `exporter`'s `splitQuery` (`packages/junction/src/plugins/export/index.ts:96`) takes a screen's filter keys as-is and refuses any `$` key (Invariant 10), and `client.fetch('/exports/<dataset>?…')` plus `download(blob, name)` (`packages/ui/utils.js`) already carry the session and save the file — what no component does is turn the list's query into that call. The red test's assertion: a mounted list with `{ status: 'open' }` triggers a fetch of `/exports/Order?status=open` and `download` receives its blob; today nothing on FilterBar or Table can be clicked to do either.
  - **A** — a prop: `<FilterBar export="Order" {client}>` (and Table) renders a Download button beside the filters. One tag, but it gives two display components a `client` and a fetch-and-save duty, and the same button can then be on both, so the owner is unclear.
  - **B** — a component: `<ExportButton dataset="Order" query={page.query} {client}>` in `packages/ui`, which drops `$` keys, calls `client.fetch` with `encodeQueryString`, and hands the blob to `download`. FilterBar and Table stay unaware of transport; a screen places the button where it likes.
  - **C** — a resource helper: `resource.export({ query })` in sierra's `createResource`, which already holds the client and the noun (the model name is the dataset), returns the blob; a screen pairs it with `download` on any `<Button>`. No new UI noun, but the UI kit ships no one-click affordance.
  - **Recommend B** — a download is one element with one job, and a Resource's query already names the dataset, so the button takes them as two props; a prop on two display components (A) is the same behavior declared twice, and C leaves every screen to rewrite the same four lines.

- **FJS-D1007 — How does an answer say every signed-in staff member writes a row?** A salon's staff all reschedule every appointment, and no access word gives a write to every signed-in user: `shared` opens the read only, and a write goes to an actor link or, when none holds it, an administrator (`emit.js` `accessFor`). The read half of `FJS-2550` is fixed (`public: ["create"]` now sits beside `shared`); the red test for this half is an answer whose entity says every signed-in user updates a row and whose emitted `@@gate` and `@@allow` let a second signed-in user update it, where today the update is gate 5 and refused.
  - **A** — `shared` takes the ops it covers: `shared: { reason, ops: ['read', 'update'] }`, a bare string staying read-only. One word, the finding already states the reach in a sentence, and the gate level 4 with no `@@allow` is what `shared` already emits for the read.
  - **B** — a new access word, `team: 'reason'` (every signed-in user reads, creates and updates, deletion stays with an author link or an administrator). Names the one-business shape directly; a second word beside `shared` that differs by the ops it opens.
  - **C** — no spelling: the one-business team shape is out of scope, and the `open` question (the builder writing a rule outside the answer) stays the answer. The person's rule lives in the schema's `@@allow`, outside Oracle.
  - **Recommend A** — it extends the word that already means *every signed-in user*, coins no second noun, and the ops it opens are stated and graded as a finding like the read already is.

- **FJS-D1012 — Does a dispatch into a RUNNING `unique` key queue one follow-up (changing what `unique` means, or behind a new option), or stay dropped as documented?** `findByUniqueKey` and the `jobs_unique_live` partial index both cover `status IN ('pending','running')` (`packages/caravan/src/db.ts:126,781`), so `dispatch` (`src/index.ts:757`) returns the running job's id and a recompute asked for after the running one read its inputs is lost (ELA `crew-budget`, FJS-2118). The red test: dispatch `unique: 'k'`, claim it so it is `running`, dispatch `unique: 'k'` again — today returns the running id and the table holds one row; the row wants a second `pending` row, and a third dispatch absorbed into it. README and `CLAUDE.md` state `unique` as "only one of these at a time", so a follow-up that a second worker could claim while the first runs breaks the lock apps rely on (`deploy:1.2.3`).
  - **A** — change `unique` itself to coalesce-while-pending: the index covers `pending` only, a dispatch into a running key inserts one pending follow-up, and the claim query skips a pending job whose key is running, so same-key work still never overlaps. Cost: the meaning of `unique` moves from "no second queued-or-running" to "no second queued", and a caller that wanted the drop-while-running behavior loses it.
  - **B** — keep `unique` as the lock and add `DispatchOptions.coalesce: true` (with `unique`) that does A's behavior for that key only. Cost: one more option on `dispatch`, and the index must still cover both states for plain `unique`, so the follow-up row needs a distinct key form or a second index.
  - **C** — leave it: record in `api-hazards` that `unique` drops into a running key, and the app uses a time-bucketed key or no `unique` (the row's stated outs). Cost: the framework never says the recompute shape.
  - **Recommend A** — "a lock on work in flight" and "queue the recompute asked for after the read" are the same intent seen from the two ends of one job, and the claim skip keeps the no-overlap guarantee; B grows an option for a distinction the index can carry.

- **FJS-D1013 — Which claim does Oracle's emitter write into an update policy so the app's system principal can make a `@system` move on an owner-only model?** `accessFor` ([emit.js](packages/oracle/src/emit.js)) emits `update` as `hostId == auth().id` for an owner link, so `Booking.complete: … @system` is a move `createApp({ system })` cannot make: the fire finds no row (`FJS-1788`'s throw names it at runtime, never at emit). The red test: emit an answer whose entity has an owner link and a lifecycle move `by: 'system'`; its `update` policy today has no term but the owner's. Any fix widens who may write the row, so the claim is the choice.
  - **A** — `auth().level >= 7` appended to `update` on a model holding a `by: 'system'` move: SYSADMIN is the one level the framework itself names (`gate.js`), and `FJS-D296` plus orion's `level >= 5` reads are the precedent. Cost: every SYSADMIN, not only the system principal, may then update those rows by plain `update`, past the owner-only rule; and an app whose `getLevel` grades its system principal lower stays silent-broken.
  - **B** — a dedicated claim, `auth().isSystem`, that the app's `SYSTEM` principal states and nothing else carries. Narrowest: exactly the principal that makes the move. Cost: a new noun every app must put on its principal, and a missing claim is the same silent no-row, so it needs the `fli check` half (`FJS-1884`) to be safe.
  - **C** — emit nothing and have the emitter refuse (or `checkAnswer` name) a `by: 'system'` move on an owner-only entity until the answer states who else may update it, so the person approves the widening in the answer rather than the emitter inventing it.
  - **Recommend B** — the move is the application's and `@system` already says so; naming the one principal that makes it widens nothing for a human SYSADMIN, and `FJS-1884`'s check is the same claim's other half.

- **FJS-D1016 — Which directories does a workspace walk count as an app when there is no schema: any manifest plus a surface dir, or only one whose surface holds its own config?** `findApps` ([runnables.js](packages/cli/core/runnables.js)) demands `db/schema.lite`; `checks.js`'s `isAppRoot` (FJS-1617) accepts a manifest plus any `SURFACES` dir. Red test: a tree `ws/apps/webonly` holding `package.json` + `web/src/routes/index.mesa` — `findApps(ws)` is `[]` today, should list it. Probed with `findApps` switched to `isAppRoot` over this repo: it adds `website` (private, a real `site/` — 8 errors, 1445 findings), `packages/orion` (a `web/` dir; 0 errors) and `packages/cli` (a `web/` that is the GUI's `index.html` served by `server.js`, no Sierra config; 95 errors), so CI's `structure` phase, which runs `findApps(ROOT)`, goes red on a library that is not an app.
  - **A** — `findApps` = `isAppRoot` as is (moved to runnables.js beside it, one definition), and the three directories take allowances in `scripts/ci-allowances.json` or are fixed (website's 8 errors). Cost: `packages/cli` is a library wearing an app's rules, and every library with a `web/` dir meets the same.
  - **B** — tighten `isAppRoot` itself: a surface counts only when it holds its own build config (`sierra.config.js`, `vite.config.*`, `desktop.config.js`, `manifest.json`), the file `surface-config` already looks for. Excludes `packages/cli` and `packages/orion`; keeps `website`. Cost: a surface directory with no config is no longer an app, so `app-layout`'s "a config is missing" finding stops firing for the web-only case it was written for.
  - **C** — A, and exclude workspace members that are published (a non-`private` `package.json` under `packages/`) unless they carry a schema. Cost: a second, manifest-derived sign beside the layout one.
  - **Recommend B** — Invariant 3 defines a surface by what it builds, and a directory with `index.html` and no build config is not one; the same predicate then answers both the single-root rules and the walk.

- **FJS-D1021 — Where does a per-queue rate limit live: a window counted over the jobs rows, a claim ledger table, or nowhere shared?** `QueueConfig` has `concurrency` and nothing that bounds starts per window, so a queue in front of a rate-limited provider (`FJS-1522`) can only be throttled per replica, and N replicas multiply the limit. The claim (`claimNext`, [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts)) is already the one atomic place that decides a job starts, and `jobs.started_at` is overwritten on every claim. Red test: a `rateLimit: { max: 2, perMs: 1000 }` queue with 5 pending jobs and two `caravan` instances on one `jobs.db` starts at most 2 in the first second; today it starts all 5.
  - **A** — `rateLimit: { max, perMs }` on `QueueConfig`, enforced in the claim's `WHERE` by counting `jobs` rows of that queue with `started_at > now - perMs`. No new state, shared by every replica through `jobs.db`. Cost: it counts each job once at its LATEST attempt (a retry overwrites `started_at`), and `cleanupAfter` shorter than `perMs` forgets finished jobs, so a retry storm against the provider is undercounted.
  - **B** — the same option, enforced by a `queue_claims(queue, at)` row appended inside the claim's transaction and pruned past `perMs`. Exact, every attempt counts, shared through `jobs.db`. Cost: one more table the claim writes to, and an index.
  - **C** — a per-process token bucket on `QueueWorker`, no schema. Cost: it is the per-replica workaround the row says is incorrect; N replicas run at N times the limit.
  - **Recommend B** — a retry is a call to the provider and the provider counts it, so A's undercount defeats the option's one purpose; C does not answer the row.

- **FJS-D1024 — What does `dndzone` grow for ELA's inbox: drop ONTO an item (merge), a foreign item that lands as something else (promote, with a placeholder), and a multi-item drag?** Found fixing `FJS-2004`. Today `hitTest` ([dnd.js](packages/ui/dnd.js)) answers one question — which insertion INDEX in which zone — and `finishDrag` hands `onfinalize` the zone's whole list with the dragged item spliced in at `placeholderIndex`; the dragged item is always one record of the zone's own `items`. Each of the three gestures breaks one of those: a drop onto an item has no index (it names an item), a foreign item that becomes a ticket is not an element of the target's `items` (the placeholder must stand for a record the host builds), and a selection is N records leaving the origin at once. The red test would be a drop with the pointer over the middle of a card, asserting an `onfinalize` that names that card as the target; nothing today can say it. `FJS-D671`'s `createSelection(keyOf)` is the selection ELA drags, so the multi-drag half has a source already.
  - **A** — options on `dndzone`, one session: `dropOnto: true | (item) => bool` makes the middle band of each item a target (edges keep inserting) and finalizes with trigger `droppedOntoItem` and `info.target` = that item, the list unchanged; `accept: (dragged) => record | null` on the receiving zone turns a foreign item into the record the host wants, shown in the placeholder; `dragSet: () => items[]` on the source (the host passes `selection`'s rows) makes the ghost a stack and the finalize carry `info.items`. Three options on one attachment, one hit-test, one set of triggers. Cost: `dndzone` is already 262 lines and `configure` 91; the three interact (a stack dropped onto an item), and every zone pays for options only the inbox uses.
  - **B** — ship only the two that need the hit-test: `dropOnto` and multi-item `dragSet`; leave the foreign-item placeholder to the host, which already receives `onconsider` with a foreign item in the list and can render the placeholder (`isDndShadowItem`) as a ticket card itself. That is the contract today, so nothing is added for it. Cost: the host maps a foreign record to its landing shape in its own `{#each}`, once per screen.
  - **C** — nothing in `dnd.js`: the inbox draws the merge target as its own pointer-band drop target (`data-dnd-target` is already styled by the app) and `dndzone` stays a flow-list reorderer, the contract its header states. Cost: ELA reimplements pointer capture, ghost, touch arming and auto-scroll for the one gesture `dnd.js` already owns, and keyboard drag (`FJS-1323`) would not reach it.
  - **Recommend B** — `dropOnto` and `dragSet` are the two things only the zone's hit-test and ghost can do, while the foreign-item placeholder already works through `onconsider` plus the shadow item, so adding `accept` would restate a mechanism the host holds. Ship `dropOnto` first, since the merge gesture is the one nothing else can reach.

- **FJS-D1025 — `FJS-D1006` ruled a role-limited move as `by <role>` beside `by: 'system'`, but Litestone's `@@transitions` carries only `@gate`, `@system` and `@seals` per move, and an update policy is a WHERE over the PRE-state row that cannot tell a move from a plain write. What `.lite` does the emitter write for `finalize: open -> final by finance`?** Found fixing `FJS-2761`. Red test: an answer whose Membership `role` is `admin|finance|support`, Invoice moves `finalize` and `void` `by: 'finance'`; a `support` member's `invoice.transition(id, 'finalize')` is expected refused, and today `checkAnswer` refuses `by: 'finance'` outright (`answer.js:479`) and the emitted update is every member's. The plain-write half (`roles: { update, delete }` on any entity access, `FJS-D623`'s key generalised) needs no ruling and ships with whichever of these is picked.
  - **A** — Litestone grows a per-move row predicate in the policy grammar, `finalize: open -> final @allow(org.members.some(userId == auth().id && role == 'finance'))`, graded on the pre-state row beside the move's `@gate`; Oracle emits it from `by`. Exact, and a move refused names itself (`TransitionGateError`). Cost: a fourth per-move attribute and a parser, client and `x-transitions` change in litestone, for one consumer.
  - **B** — No litestone change: `by: <role>` narrows the entity's update policy on the move's FROM-states (`status != 'open' || <role term>`). Cost: it also bars the other roles from plain edits of a row in that state, so 'any member edits a draft, only a manager submits' has no spelling, and the refusal is a silent empty update.
  - **C** — `by: <role>` is refused with a finding that sends the move to the app (`open` item, a hand-written `@gate`), and only the plain-write `roles` ships. Today's behavior for moves; Charter's People step collects roles for writes but not for moves.
  - **Recommend A** — a move's role is a question about a row and a caller, which is what a policy already answers, and B can only get there by over-restricting. The attribute is the same shape as `@gate` on the move, and the client already renders `transitions(row)` from the gate's verdict, so a UI would hide the button a role cannot press with no new path.

- ~~**FJS-D1026 — How does a keyboard drag in `dndzone` cross into another zone, and how does a host word its announcements?**~~ **Answered 2026-11-08 (`FJS-D1026`): A — Cross: the arrows PERPENDICULAR to the zone's axis hop to the nearest zone of the same type in that direction (by measured rect), landing at the index nearest the item's current position; no new key. Wording: one option `announcements: (event, info) => string | undefined` where `event` is `lifted | moved | dropped | cancelled` and `info` is `{ label, position, total, zone }`; `undefined` falls back to the English default. Cost: a wrapped or grid zone already reads by line, and perpendicular arrows there are ambiguous with line moves.** Found fixing `FJS-2659`. `startKeyboardDrag` ([dnd.js](packages/ui/dnd.js)) measures only the origin zone into `session.zones`, and `keyboardKey` moves `meta.placeholderIndex` along `meta.axis` inside `session.current`, so a roster that assigns by dragging into another zone has no keyboard path; `announce()` and `where()` build English literals with no seam. Both halves add a surface to the zone's options or keys, so neither is derivable from the tree.
  - **A** — Cross: the arrows PERPENDICULAR to the zone's axis hop to the nearest zone of the same type in that direction (by measured rect), landing at the index nearest the item's current position; no new key. Wording: one option `announcements: (event, info) => string | undefined` where `event` is `lifted | moved | dropped | cancelled` and `info` is `{ label, position, total, zone }`; `undefined` falls back to the English default. Cost: a wrapped or grid zone already reads by line, and perpendicular arrows there are ambiguous with line moves.
  - **B** — Cross: a modifier on the axis arrows (Shift+Arrow along the axis hops to the previous/next zone of the type in registry order). Unambiguous in wrapped zones and needs no geometry, but the key is undiscoverable and the order is the DOM's, not the screen's. Wording: an `announcements` object of one function per event, each returning a string — four options to state instead of one.
  - **C** — Cross: a drop-target cycle on `[` / `]` (or PageUp/PageDown), announced on lift; geometry-free like B, no collision with arrows. Wording: a `messages` object of template strings (`'{label}, lifted, {position} of {total}'`). Cost: a template syntax is a second mini-language, and a language with plural or word-order rules needs a function anyway.
  - **Recommend A** — a kanban's columns sit across the axis of its lists, so the perpendicular arrow is what a sighted user already expects, and one `announcements` function is the single seam both English and another language need; the wrapped-zone ambiguity is answered by taking the perpendicular arrow as a zone hop only when no item lies that way within the current zone.

- **FJS-D1027 — Which copy survives the one-hop bound: convert `Order`/`PaymentMethod`/`Subscription`/`Invoice` to `customer.userId` and trade the grandchildren to `customerId`, keep every `userId` copy, or lift the bound to two hops?** Found fixing `FJS-915`. Nine copies, not seven: `Order`, `PaymentMethod`, `Subscription`, `Invoice` hang off `Customer` (one hop to `customer.userId`); `OrderLine`, `Payment`, `SubscriptionPeriod`, `InvoiceLine`, `CreditNote` hang off those (`order.customer.userId` is two hops, a parse error under `FJS-D221`). Converting a parent makes its child need a copy of `customerId` instead, so the copy is traded rather than removed. Behavior moves too: `Order.userId` is stamped at sale and stays null for a guest sale on purpose (the `Order.userId` comment), whereas `customer.userId` makes an account registered later read the customer's old guest orders. Every model is read by `verify:account`, `verify:cart`, `verify:money` and `verify:billing`, so a conversion is five-drive work.
  - **A** — Convert the four direct children to `customer.userId` (four copies gone outright); each grandchild keeps ONE copy, of `customerId` (an immutable relation key that cannot drift when an account is linked) in place of `userId`. Accepts the late-registration read of old guest orders and says so in the `Order` comment.
  - **B** — Keep all nine `userId` copies; rewrite the nine comments to cite the bound as measured and make `carts.checkout`/billing's one transaction the stated owner. Zero behavior change, and `FJS-915` closes as a measurement.
  - **C** — Lift `FJS-D221` to two hops in Litestone's parser and compile (an `EXISTS` chain), then delete every copy. Removes all nine, and costs a parser/compiler change plus a re-ruling of a bound that was deliberate.
  - **Recommend A** — it removes the four copies a single hop can remove, shrinks the survivors from a mutable account id to an immutable key, and leaves the bound alone; C is the right next ruling only if a second app also pays for two hops.

- **FJS-D1031 — How does a method answer in frames through the pipeline: what spells the declaration, and does a service context name its connection?** Found fixing `FJS-1420`. `FJS-D853` ruled that a streaming method DECLARES itself and a non-streaming transport buffers, but nothing in the tree implements it: `refuseStream` (`core/envelope.ts:234`) still refuses every stream, `CustomMethodFn` (`core/service.ts:1273`) has no word for "answers in parts", and `ServiceContext` carries a session and no connection, so an after-return frame is `app.channel(x).send` to a PERSON (the notion `pages.enter` joins both of a person's tabs). The red test is a declared method that yields three frames: today the call is refused by name, and the frames reach no store. `IDEAS/chat-surface.md` Part 1 has the behavior (hooks and gate before the first frame, each frame through `protect()` and the after-hooks, `ctx.sse()` under HTTP and the `event` frame under WS, one client call shape yielding chunks); what is open is the spelling and the second half of the row.
  - **A** — Frames only. `methods: { search: { stream: true, input, gate } }`, the method an async generator, each yielded value a frame through `protect()`; the client's `invoke()` returns an async iterable that resolves to the last frame. Frames go over the calling connection by construction (the transport owns it), so a service context needs no connection. Closes the Portal search shape and the chat answer; leaves notion's `pages.enter` (join ONE connection, not the session's) open as its own row.
  - **B** — A, plus `ctx.connection` (an id on the service context for HTTP-with-socket and WS calls) so `app.channel(x).join(ctx.connection)` and a late `send` can address one connection. Closes the notion half too; adds a noun and a way to learn the id on an HTTP call that has no socket (undefined, which every caller then handles).
  - **C** — Only `ctx.connection`, no streaming declaration: a method returns once and sends later frames to its own connection, ungraded as today. Smallest, but the frames skip `protect()` and the pipeline, which is the row's complaint, and `FJS-D853` is left unimplemented.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the shape `FJS-D853` and chat-surface Part 1 already ruled, it puts every frame through the pipeline rather than around it, and the connection half is a separate defect with a different fix that can follow once frames exist.

- **FJS-D1032 — How does a button list learn the grid — what the caller HOLDS — so `resource.transitions` grades `@@capabilities` (and the post-update policy)?** Found fixing `FJS-1508`. `transitionsAt` (`sierra/src/resource/field-rules.js`) has the move→capability names already (`x-capabilities.moves`, emitted by `jsonschema.js`) but not the caller's held set, and no browser path carries it: a staff `session.user` may hold `capabilities` (`sessionFields`), a `principal`-resolver caller's set is read per request and never on `session.user`, and a bearer's is on a grant row behind an httpOnly cookie. Litestone's own `db.<model>.transitions(row)` (`client.js:9304`) grades gate, `@system` and policy and not the grid either. The red test: `createResource` over a schema with `x-capabilities.moves: { withdraw: 'Application.withdraw' }` → `transitions({ stage: 'applied' }, 0)` marks `reject` `allowed: true` where the server answers 403.
  - **A** — Sierra only: `resource.transitions(row, level, held)`, `held` an array/Set of capability names, and `transitionsAt` refuses a move whose `x-capabilities.moves[name]` is not in it (`refusedBy: 'capability'`). Absent `held` stays permissive. The screen supplies the set, so staff on `sessionFields` works and the per-request `principal` and the bearer do not; the policy half stays ungraded.
  - **B** — A, plus the server says the held set per load: Junction answers `$capabilitiesFor(request principal)` (a built-in method or the session payload) and `createResource` keeps it, so `transitions(row, level)` grades the grid with no third argument for all three callers. New wire surface, one owner (litestone's `$capabilitiesFor`); the policy half stays ungraded.
  - **C** — Ask the SERVER for the list: litestone's `transitions()` also grades the grid (it holds the principal's capabilities), Junction exposes it as a call, and `resource.transitions` stays the permissive gate-only affordance for the cases a round trip is wrong. Closes the grid AND the post-update policy half (Rita's `approve`) with one grader, at one request per record.
  - **Recommend C** — three gaps (grid, policy, held set unknown) share one cause, that the browser regrades what only the Data boundary can know; `transitionsAt`'s own comment already says *a screen that needs the true list asks the SERVER*, and it keeps Invariant 4's one grader.
  - **Also holds `FJS-2997`** — `resource.can('update')` offers Edit on a row the caller reads and the update policy refuses; litestone's `$checkWrite(accessor, 'update', row)` already grades gate, grid and policy and answers `{ ok, rule }`. Red assertion: under a row policy `owner = $auth.id`, a reader's `can('update', level)` is `true` while the PATCH is 403. Under C the per-row ask answers `can` and `transitions` with one call; under A the row stays open.

- **FJS-D1033 — Where does a vendor subscription's lifecycle live, and is it a new noun?** Found fixing FJS-2573. Nothing in `packages/conduit/src` or `packages/caravan/src` models a vendor-side subscription (conduit's `subscriptions` map is live BROKER consumers, `FJS-D235`, a different thing), so there is no red test to write until a shape is chosen. The need, measured in ELA's Graph mail and Gmail's watch: a lease with a max lifespan (4230 min / 7 days), renewed before it lapses, re-created on `reauthorizationRequired` / `subscriptionRemoved` / `missed`, with a per-subscription secret (`clientState`) minted at create, kept on renew and rotated on re-create; a hand-built self-re-arming chain dies silently. The red assertion: a subscription declared with `lifespan` and `renewBefore`, after a renew job throws once, still has a renew scheduled and `stats()` names it overdue — today there is nothing to declare.
  - **A** — a conduit noun: a connector declares `subscription { create, renew, lifespan, renewBefore, onLifecycle(event) }` (pure vendor calls, `FJS-D153`), the app binds it to a credential, and conduit owns the schedule (a Caravan job under a stated `dispatch({ id })`), the secret's mint/keep/rotate, and an overdue entry in `stats()` and readiness. A new noun and a new conduit→caravan dependency edge, and the lifecycle events arrive through the Receiver, so it waits on `FJS-D899`.
  - **B** — no new noun: the connector exports `create`/`renew`/`recreate` as plain functions and a `renewAt(subscription)` helper; the app writes one `*.job.ts` cron that calls them and re-dispatches itself. Smallest, but it is the hand-built chain the row says dies silently, now with a helper.
  - **C** — a Caravan primitive, vendor-blind: a job that re-arms itself by declaration (`rearm: { before: expiresAt }`) and reports a broken chain as overdue; secrets, create and lifecycle events stay in the connector and app. Fixes the silent death for every self-re-arming job, not just subscriptions, and leaves conduit unchanged, but the secret and re-create halves of the row stay app code.
  - **Recommend C first, A's declaration only when a second vendor asks** — the silent death is the measured harm and it is a Caravan property, not a vendor one; `FJS-D899` C already keeps lifecycle events out of the Receiver until a second app asks, so A would be built against a record not yet ruled.

- **FJS-D1034 — Is a polled feed — a vendor read on a clock, resumed from a durable cursor — a new noun, and does it live in Caravan, in Conduit, or in the app?** Found fixing `FJS-2572`. `conduit-connectors.md` § *What is deliberately not on it* rules sync engines out of conduit ("their own noun when wanted") and ELA wants three (Service Autopilot's day window, 3CX's overlap-and-cap window, Graph's per-folder `deltaLink` with a 410 restart), each hand-building the cursor row, overlap and dedupe, cap, suspension, 04:00 backstop and one-runner-per-feed. Nothing in `packages/caravan/src` or `packages/conduit/src` holds a cursor; Caravan owns the clock and `dispatch({ id })` the one-runner guarantee, Conduit owns the dial and its breaker (`FJS-2580`, in memory). No red test yet — the shape decides what it asserts (a cursor that survives a restart and advances only on a completed non-backstop run).
  - **A** — a Caravan primitive, `defineFeed({ every, poll(cursor, ctx) -> { items, next }, overlap, cap, backstop })`: Caravan keeps the cursor in its own table, stamps it only when a round completes, clamps cold-start and future cursors, dispatches under a stated `id` per feed so there is one runner, and a thrown 410-class error resets the cursor. Vendor-blind (the vendor call is the app's `poll`, through `app.conduit.send`), so `FJS-D177` and `FJS-D153` are untouched; dedupe and tombstones stay the app's, in `items`. New noun and one new table.
  - **B** — no new noun: Caravan gains only a durable per-job `state` (get/set a JSON cursor atomically with the job's completion); each feed stays a `*.job.ts` that computes its own window. Smallest, and `FJS-D1033` C (re-arming) may want the same state column, but overlap, cap, backstop and the single runner stay hand-built three times.
  - **C** — a Conduit noun: a connector declares a `feed` beside its targets and Conduit owns the cursor and the schedule. Puts a sync contract in conduit — the `FJS-D153` inversion `conduit-connectors.md` names — and couples the clock to a package that has none.
  - **Recommend A, built on B's state column** — the harm measured is the same hand-built cursor, window and runner three times, and all of it is clock-and-state, which is Caravan's; the vendor call stays outside, so no sync contract enters conduit.
