  - **Prior art — Junior** ([usejunior-sh/junior@533052b](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/tree/533052b), read 2026-10-25), a local agent that polls GitHub, Slack and MCP sources on a clock. It supports A and adds four things A's shape should hold:
    - *A second feed shape: a snapshot diff, not an event cursor.* Its PR watch ([core/babysit/watch.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/core/babysit/watch.ts)) diffs this poll's snapshot against the last one, a pure function with no I/O and no clock. Between polls it keeps only what a dedupe key can't (fix attempts per check, one rerun per check per commit, fingerprints of comments already handled), and the tasks dedupe on their key. An order's status or a PR's checks have no cursor to resume from, so A's `next` is the feed's opaque state, not necessarily a position.
    - *Dedupe by id makes the window safe.* Its job runs keep no cursor at all ([core/job/connectors.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/core/job/connectors.ts)): each run scans a look-back window, and every match is an idempotent upsert keyed by a canonical id (`github:owner/repo#N`). That is `FJS-D1354`'s dispatch under the event's own id, so overlap needs no ledger beyond the job id, and A can leave dedupe to `dispatch({ id })`.
    - *A failed read is never an empty read.* Matches arrive as tool calls rather than parsed JSON, because "a parse failure reads as empty"; a malformed row is discarded, never repaired, and a round that found nothing is still recorded. The red test asserts the cursor holds when `poll` throws or returns an unreadable page, and that each round leaves a record saying *found 0* or *failed*.
    - *A missed slot is recorded, then run once.* A minute tick with no cron ([server/ticks.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/server/ticks.ts)); a slot the process slept through is marked `missed`, and a run cut short by sleep is marked `slept` ([core/job/run-guard.ts](https://github.com/usejunior-sh/junior/blob/533052b/core/job/run-guard.ts)). On wake the missed slot fires once and never stacks. That is A's catch-up rule, and it sits beside `backstop` rather than replacing it. Cadence adapts: the PR search runs every minute while something moves and every five otherwise, so `every` may want an active and an idle value.

- **FJS-D1035 — Where does a target's breaker state persist across a restart and between replicas?** Found fixing `FJS-2580`. `Resilience` (`packages/conduit/src/resilience.ts:66`) keeps `failures`/`openedAt` in a private `Map`, and `ConduitStore` (`types.ts:392`) has no breaker surface, so a target opened with `reset_ms` 30 min is `closed` again after a restart; the red test registers a target, trips it past `failure_threshold`, builds a second `createConduit` over the same `createSQLiteStore(db)` and asserts `admit` answers `circuit_open` — it answers `ok` today.
  - **A** — optional breaker methods on `ConduitStore` (`loadBreakers()` / `saveBreaker(id, { failures, openedAt })`): the SQLite store gets a `conduit_breakers` table and the memory store keeps today's behavior. `Resilience` writes through on open/close transitions only (not on every failure) and reads on init; replicas converge by re-reading at `admit` when the window might have changed. No new option — the store the app already passes is the choice.
  - **B** — a separate `resilience.store` option beside the registry store, so a breaker can persist in SQLite while the registry is memory (or the reverse). One more option and one more interface to implement for a networked backend.
  - **C** — no persistence in conduit: a breaker is a per-process load shedder (its own header says it protects request handlers), and a caller that needs a suspension across a restart keeps it itself, which is `FJS-2572`'s polled-feed suspension. Smallest, but leaves a crash loop re-sending to a failing provider on every boot for every non-feed caller.
  - **Recommend A** — the registry store is already the thing that survives a restart and is shared by replicas (`ConduitStore` says so), so a breaker is one more fact about a target it holds; write only on open/close so the hot path stays in memory.

- **FJS-D1037 — How does `analytics:` switch the same-origin Plausible proxy on, and what path does the tag point at?** Found fixing `FJS-2893`. `analytics/tag.js` `vendorTag` writes `${apiHost ?? 'https://plausible.io'}/js/script.js` and the script posts events to that origin, so an ad blocker drops both; ksite answers this with a hand-written `functions/api/event.js` per site. Sierra already owns the URL and `postbuild/functions.js` already owns the function entry, so the proxy is derivable, but the switch and the paths are a new key and a new build output. The red test's assertion: with `analytics: { provider: 'plausible', domain, <switch> }`, the written `.sierra/functions.js` routes hold `/js/script.js` and `/api/event` with no `functions:` directory, and `vendorTagHtml` emits `src="/js/script.js"`; today it holds neither.
  - **A** — one key, `analytics.proxy: true` (Plausible only; refused on `gtm` and on a custom provider, by name). It fixes the two paths Plausible's own proxy guide uses, `/js/script.js` and `/api/event`, so there is no path option. The build adds both routes to the functions entry, written even when the site has no `functions:` directory; `tag.js` changes `src` to the same-origin path for both the static tag and the SPA runtime, and `apiHost` stays the upstream. A site's own file at either path is refused as a duplicate, which `functionRoutes` already does.
  - **B** — no key: whenever the provider is `plausible` and a host will mount functions (a `functions:` key is present), the tag points same-origin. Nothing to write, but a site gets a changed tag by adding an unrelated directory, and a host with no functions (a plain CDN) silently loses its analytics.
  - **C** — derive nothing: ship the handler as `@frontierjs/sierra/plausible-proxy` and a site's `functions/api/event.js` becomes one re-export line, plus a second for the script. The tag URL and the proxy path are still two places that must agree, which is what the paper's *two owners hold one URL* clause rules out.
  - **Recommend A** — an explicit switch keeps `plausible.io` the default and the change visible in `sierra.config.js`, and fixed paths mean the tag and the proxy cannot disagree because one function names both.

- **FJS-D1039 — Does `conduit-telnyx` become `packages/conduit-telnyx` now, ahead of the promotion order and with its error codes unchecked against an account, or wait its turn at #13?** Found fixing `FJS-2571`. No `packages/conduit-*` exists yet (D890 names `@frontierjs/conduit-stripe` as the first extraction), so this would be the first connector in the tree and the one `connector-contract` is first graded on. Two sources exist: the sibling `conduit-connectors/packages/conduit-telnyx` (written new, sink-only, 10 drive rows, `src/errors.ts` unverified) and `fjs-prototypes/quo/api/src/providers/telnyx/` (1143 lines in use; also sink-only, so no source has met Telnyx). The register's gate, "error codes checked against an account", cannot be met on any machine here. ELA slice S7 (Q9) and maid.tech wait on the package.
  - **A** — promote now from quo's code (the one an app runs), folding in the prototype's `errors.ts`/`verify.ts` split; the README states the error codes are unverified, as google-maps' wording is. ELA, SSTime and quo move onto it in the same change.
  - **B** — hold at #13: leave quo, SSTime and ELA on their copies until stripe..push have been promoted; `FJS-2571` stays a tracker.
  - **C** — promote right after stripe: stripe is the shipped-behavior extraction that settles the package shape (peer range, Packages row, `connector-contract`, snapshots), telnyx follows it directly as the second, and the unverified-codes gate becomes a README line, not a blocker.
  - **Recommend C** — the first extraction should be the one with shipped behavior behind it, not a connector written new, and three copies of a send path argue against waiting to #13; the account check is unreachable for everyone, so gating on it only stalls.

- **FJS-D1041 — When a column's `@scale` changes, does the migrator refuse until an operation says what to do with the rows, or emit the multiply/divide itself — and where does it read the OLD scale from?** Found fixing `FJS-2140`. Both sides of the differ (live DB, migration-history shadow) are introspected DDL, and `@scale(n)` leaves only `CHECK ("c" BETWEEN -2^53 AND 2^53)` in it (`src/core/ddl.js:361`), identical at every n. So `@scale(9)` → `@scale(6)` is a no-op where the DDL is unchanged and a verbatim `INSERT … SELECT` where the table is rebuilt for another reason. The red test's assertion: `createAgainstHistory` over a history with `Int @scale(9)` and a schema with `Int @scale(6)` returns `created: true` with a `-- Accept data loss`-style gate, not `the migration history already builds the schema`.
  - **A** — put n in the DDL: the CHECK carries it as a trailing comment (`CHECK ("c" BETWEEN -X AND X) /* scale 6 */`, kept by `normalizeTableDdl` if comments survive), so `diffColumns` sees old and new n. A change is then **refused** like a lossy rebuild: the file carries a box (`-- Rescale: units 9 → 6, multiply by 10^-3 — choose: keep | rescale`) that `migrate apply` refuses until the author edits it to `UPDATE … SET c = c * 1000` / `/ 1000` or deletes the box. Reuses the BLOCKED/data-loss gate; nothing is guessed.
  - **B** — same DDL footprint as A, but the migrator **emits** the `UPDATE` (multiply when n grows; divide when it shrinks, with a `WHERE c % 10^k <> 0` precondition that refuses the file if any row would lose digits, and the 2^53 CHECK refusing an overflow on multiply). No author edit in the common case; a shrink that truncates is the only block.
  - **C** — refuse only, keyed on an explicit `--rescale Model.field=9->6` operation beside `--rename`; with no DDL footprint the differ still cannot see an unannounced change, so `FJS-2140`'s second half (ChargeTier, no statement at all) stays open.
  - **Recommend B** — the old and new n are all the information a rescale needs, so asking the author to type the multiplication (A) reintroduces the silent-wrong-edit risk; the one case with a real choice (a shrink that truncates) is the one B refuses.

- **FJS-D1046 — How does a schema say *this column belongs to these types* once, so the JSON schema, the generated types and `<Form>` all read it?** Found fixing `FJS-1718`. A model whose `type` column selects its columns says *needed when* as `@required(where: type == 'todo')`, which crosses as `x-litestone-required-where` (AST, `jsonschema.js:621`) and which `requiredFor` (`field-rules.js:2136`) evaluates against the record on screen. Its mirror, *only when*, is a field `@check("type = 'todo' OR checked IS NULL", …)`: a SQL string that reaches the boundary and `x-messages.check`, and nothing else. The red test: a `Block` with `@required(where: type == 'todo')` and `@check("type = 'todo' OR checked IS NULL")` on `checked`; `generateJsonSchema(schema, 'client').properties.checked` carries no key naming the `type` condition, `litestone types` emits `checked?: boolean | null` on every type, and `requiredFields({type: 'heading'})`/`<Form>` still offers the `checked` control.
  - **A** — carry the field `@check`'s predicate as an AST when it reads only this row's own columns (the same grammar and refusals `FJS-D259` gave `@required(where:)`), as `x-litestone-check-where`, and read it in three places: sierra hides or disables a control the row's type does not own (the evaluator is the toolbelt's, as `requiredFor`), `litestone types` emits a union of per-type shapes when a column's two conditions agree, `fli db:explain` names it. No new schema word; the author already wrote the `@check`. The cost is that a free-form SQL `CHECK` is parsed as the policy grammar, and one that does not parse stays server-only.
  - **B** — a new word for the union, one declaration instead of two mirrored ones: `@@variant(type, todo: [checked], heading: [level])` (name open — `@@union`, `@@discriminator`), from which litestone DERIVES both the `@required(where:)` and the CHECK, the JSON schema's `oneOf`, the generated discriminated union and the control set. Single owner and nothing to keep in step, but it coins a noun, overlaps `IDEAS/overview.md` 4.26 (variants that only RELAX), and every existing hand-written pair has to move to it.
  - **C** — leave the schema as it is and state the limit: `@required(where:)` is the one conditional the client reads, a *only when* is a boundary refusal with a path and a message (measured, all 7 shapes refused), and an app that wants a menu of types restates them. Zero new surface; the notion add menu's `TEXT_TYPES` stays a hand-kept copy of `Block.text`'s condition.
  - **Recommend A** — the author has already written both halves, the predicate grammar and its browser evaluator exist, and A makes the second half cross without a new noun (`FJS-D259`'s own reasoning); B can come later as sugar over A if the pair proves too tedious.

- **FJS-D1049 — How does an `@@fts` index match without the marks (Greek accents, Hebrew points, U+1F77 vs U+03AF), and who keeps the fold on both sides?** Found fixing `FJS-1466`. Today `ALLOWED_TOKENIZERS` is `unicode61 | ascii | porter | trigram` (`parser.js`), `createFts` (`ddl.js`) emits an external-content `fts5` table whose INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE triggers copy `new."col"` straight from the source table, and litestone registers no SQL function (`bun:sqlite` has no `create_function`; a custom FTS5 tokenizer is C-only), so the fold cannot run inside the index. The red test: a `@@fts([text])` model holding `βίβλος` answers `$search('βίβλος')` and `search('biblos'-style folded query)` with 0 rows; `normalize('NFD').replace(/\p{M}/gu, '')` of both sides is what would match.
  - **A** — a word on the index, `@@fts([text], fold: marks)` (NFD, strip `\p{M}`, NFC otherwise): litestone adds a hidden shadow column per folded field, stamps it in JS on every client `create`/`update`/`upsert` like `@default(ulid())`, points the index and triggers at the shadow, and folds the query inside `search()` and the `$search` directive. One owner, `$search`/Resource/console agree. Cost: a generated column in DDL and migrations (the `FJS-1463` add-to-loaded-table path), a one-time JS refold when the word is added to a loaded table, and a raw `UPDATE` of the source column leaves the shadow stale (same shape as the clock hazard).
  - **B** — same word, no shadow column: the FTS table stops being external-content and holds the folded text itself; litestone writes the index rows from JS after each client write and drops the triggers. Model gains no column, but a raw SQL write or an `@@sync` apply skips the index entirely, and a rebuild is a JS pass rather than `INSERT INTO fts(fts) VALUES('rebuild')`.
  - **C** — no word: state the limit, document the app-side recipe (loader-written shadow column plus a folded query in the service, remnant's `fold.ts`), and add `normalize('NFC')` to the `$search` query side only. Zero new surface; `$search` still answers `βίβλος` with 0 rows, and nothing ties the shadow to its source.
  - **Recommend A** — the fold is a fact with many possible answers and must have one (`toolbelt`'s stated job), the schema then declares the intent once and both sides derive from it, and the stale-shadow hazard is the same one `@updatedAt` already carries and `data-hazards` already names; B trades a column for losing every non-client write path to the index.

- **FJS-D1050 — Is the FJS dictionary DERIVED or AUTHORED: does `ARCHITECT.md` § 2 become generated, or does the dictionary derive only what has an enforcer and cite § 2 for the rest?** Found fixing `FJS-1211`. `fli ws:terms` (`packages/cli/core/terms.js`) already measures spread and is deliberately ungated; the gated `ws:dictionary` is what it becomes once this settles. § 2 is hand-written (22 Use/Not rows, 12 clarifications, 5 terms under *Not yet named*) and nothing grades that the code still uses the left column, so a second authored list beside it fails *another origin of truth* by construction. Sources readable today: litestone's `catalog.snapshot.md`, `seams.snapshot.md` from `bridge-index`, and the `FJS-D##` rulings most clarifications already cite. `IDEAS/lexicon.md` is the i18n half (`FJS-D12`) and is not this.
  - **A** — derive what has an enforcer, cite § 2 for the rest: a gated `ws:dictionary` snapshot with one row per term — its enforcer (catalog entry, seam, `fli check` rule, `FJS-D##`) or `none`, the way `invariants.snapshot.md` marks a `none` row (`FJS-D190`). § 2 stays authored; its Use/Not rows are read as the dictionary's input, and the snapshot fails when a *Not* word is live in code or a *Use* word has no hit. Other realms get a catalog only as each term needs an enforcer to cite.
  - **B** — § 2 becomes generated: the authored claim moves down to the places that enforce it (a per-realm catalog for Mesa blocks, Sierra targets and UI-kit terms; a term line on the `FJS-D##` that coined it) and `ARCHITECT.md` § 2 is emitted from them. One origin per term, but it first needs the three missing catalogs and a migration of every clarification, and § 2's doctrine prose has no enforcer to move to.
  - **C** — authored only: a hand-written dictionary graded by `ws:terms`' audit tab, ungated. Cheapest, but it is the second authored list, and *a word three packages enforce and a word the docs alone use* still read identically.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only shape that grades the left column without a second origin of truth or a migration first, and a term marked `none` is the audit the row asks for; B can follow term by term as catalogs appear.

- **FJS-D1051 — How does a schema name "the last related row's column equals this row's column" as one filterable column?** Found fixing `FJS-2809`. The red test: `lastAuthor Int? @from(Msg, last: authorId, orderBy: id)` is a parse error today (`Expected BOOL, got 'authorId'`, `parser.js` `parseFrom`, `case 'last'` eats `TK.BOOL`), `last: true` answers the whole row, and nothing in `@from where:` or `@derived` can compare a related column with the row's own, so `supportThreads.find` has no `waiting` column to filter and the derived CLI/MCP cannot offer one.
  - **A** — a scalar form of `first:`/`last:`: `last: authorId` reads that column of the row `orderBy:` picks (typed as the column, `null` when none; `true` stays the whole row, like `max:`/`min:` already name a column). The comparison then lives in a `@derived(lastAuthorId == userId)` Boolean, which needs `@derived` to read a `@from` field (the `@derived` table in `schema.md` refuses only another `@derived` and a `@computed`). Two small pieces, and the result is a real column every `find`, the CLI and MCP already filter and sort by.
  - **B** — one Boolean op that states the comparison: `@from(SupportMessage, last: authorId, orderBy: createdAt, equals: userId)` (or a `matches:` option naming the parent column), compiled to one correlated subquery. One piece and one field, but a new option on `@from` that serves only this comparison, and a second spelling of "compare to my own column" beside `@derived`.
  - **C** — a column reference in a query `where` value (`{ lastAuthorId: { equals: { $col: 'userId' } } }`) beside the scalar form of A. General (it also answers `updatedAt > createdAt`), but the answer is a per-call filter and not a column, so the derived CLI's `find` still has no "waiting" and the app writes the filter into each caller; it also puts a new `$`-keyed form in the where language (Invariant 10 territory).
  - **Recommend A** — the gap is "no column says waiting", and A is the one shape that ends in a column; the scalar `last: col` is the form `max:`/`min:` already set, and `@derived` reading a `@from` is the missing half of what `relations.md` already says an `@derived` parent column can be.

- **FJS-D1052 — Does a write that moves a value set's source row out of the set refuse when a `required` or `open` `@values` binding still points at it — derived from the binding that exists — or is it a new word, or the app's hook?** Found fixing `FJS-2797`. `enforceBoundValueSets` (`packages/litestone/src/core/valuesets.js`) runs only from the referencing model's six write paths; no write to the SOURCE model reads `ctx.shapes[*].valueSets` backwards, so `Offer.parentId @values(OfferPackage)` refuses a part under a service while patching the package itself to `type = 'service'` answers 200 and strands every part (and, on the join, both `OfferPart.packageId` and `OfferPart.partId`). Red test: a source `update` that makes `where` false while a referrer holds its value resolves instead of throwing a `ValidationError`.
  - **A** — derived, no new word: for each `required`/`open` binding whose source is the written model and whose set carries a `where` or `dependsOn`, a source update/updateMany/upsert that leaves a referenced value outside the set is refused with a `ValidationError` naming the set and the count of referrers, inside the write's transaction. Referrers are counted past the caller's read scope (a row the caller cannot see still strands), with soft-deleted rows included, so this is the one `@values` check not run through the caller's accessor; an unscoped set checks only a change to `valueField`. Cost: one extra count per matching source write, none for a model that is no set's source.
  - **B** — a word: `valueset X { where "…", onLeave refuse }` (default silent as today). Schemas opt in per set, nothing existing changes behavior, but a stranding gap stays the default and the word is a fourth `valueset` option beside `where`, `label` and `value`.
  - **C** — no engine change: state the limit in `data-hazards` and docs/valuesets, and leave the app's `before.patch` hook as the recipe (ela's `offers.service.ts`). Zero surface, but the hook lives in a service and a write that bypasses services (a job, `asSystem()`) strands anyway, contrary to Invariant 6.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 6 puts access and integrity at the Data boundary, the binding already says the referrer must stay in the set, and a gap that only a service hook closes is the silent wrong answer that Invariant 6 forbids; the cost lands only on models that are some set's source, and the one departure (referrers read past the caller's scope) is forced, since a refusal that counts only visible rows would still strand.

- **FJS-D1053 — How does a day window (`@@expires`, `@@effective`) over a `String @date @zoned(path)` column read its row's zone?** `@@expires(expiresOn)` on `expiresOn String @date @zoned(customer.timeZone)` reads every row against `effAsOf`'s one UTC day (`client.js` `effAsOf`; `asOf` on a day window is itself a plain date, so only the unstated clock reaches a zone). Red assertion (probed, not committed): clock `2026-10-03T15:00Z`, a Tokyo quote and a Los Angeles quote both expiring `2026-10-04` — `findMany()` returns both; Tokyo is already on Oct 4, so the answer is `['la']`. `due()` grades the same column per row (`FJS-2170`); the window does not (`FJS-2782`).
  - **A** — lower it into the read's SQL, by `FJS-D908`'s rule (SQL never evaluates a zone; zones are enumerated, every comparison is a day literal). The clock's day in each zone takes at most three distinct values, so the window becomes `(zone IN (<zones on day D1>) AND col > D1) OR (zone IN (<zones on D2>) AND col > D2) …`; a zone one relation away (`customer.timeZone`) is the correlated subquery a policy path already builds, and a null zone reads in UTC. Every verb, `include`, `count` and `limit` stay right since it is a predicate and not a post-filter, and `asOf` keeps meaning a stated day. Cost: a zone list in the bind set, and the nested-include window in `include.js` needs the same lowering.
  - **B** — refuse by name: a parser error on `@@expires`/`@@effective` over a `@zoned` column ("a window reads one zone per call; materialize the instant"), the cure being a `DateTime` column the app writes (`FJS-D908`'s B). Zero read-path change and the matrix gains a refused cell, but `due()` honors the binding that a window on the same column may not, and the common shape (a quote expiring on its account's calendar) has no spelling at all.
  - **C** — grade in JavaScript as `due()` does: widen the SQL to the latest/earliest day any zone is on, then drop rows in their own zone. Wrong for `limit`/`offset`, `count`, aggregates and `include`, which is why `due()` can do it (a sweep) and a read cannot.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 6 puts what counts at the Data boundary and `@zoned` already declares the zone, so a window that ignores it is the silent wrong answer; B leaves the declared quote-expiry shape unwritable, and C breaks every verb that counts or pages.

- **FJS-D1055 — How does a member read one row's trail: what is the subject key, and who may read it?** Found fixing `FJS-2738`. The synthesized `<Db>Trail` model (`audit-log.js` `makeLoggerAutoModel`) is `@@gate("8")` (`FJS-2139`) and has no record-id column: a row's id lives only inside `records` (a `Json` array of ids, `buildLogEntry` `records: JSON.stringify(records)`), which the jsonl driver's filters cannot match (scalar operators only), so `db.auditTrail.findMany({ where: <this row> })` is unaskable below system and a record's Activity falls back to its own timestamps. Red assertion: a level-1 member who may read `Lead 7` calls anything for "what happened to Lead 7" and gets a refusal at the gate or, as system, a full scan of `records`.
  - **A** — a `recordId` column (indexed) written one trail row per written id, plus a graded verb `db.<accessor>.history(id)`: it reads the row through the CALLER's principal first (an unreadable row answers as absent, a banned member's empty room stays empty), then reads the trail as system and returns entries with `before`/`after` cut to the fields the caller may read (`$readAs`). One column, one verb, and "whoever may read the row" is the row's own read policy by construction, so no second policy to write. Cost: a multi-record `updateMany` writes N rows instead of one, and a DELETED row has no row left to read through, so its history is system-only unless the model's gate level alone decides.
  - **B** — keep one trail row per write and add a `subjects` index over the ids in `records` (a side table or a jsonl index keyed by id), same graded verb as A. Write amplification stays at one entry, but it is a second structure the trail driver must keep in step with the entry and no flavor has one today.
  - **C** — open the trail model: let the schema declare `@@allow`/`@@gate` on `<Db>Trail` (`FJS-2148` refuses an `@@allow` on it today) with the subject column added as in A, and the app writes the policy ("a member reads trail rows whose model they can read"). Smallest engine surface, but a trail row's policy cannot see the row it is about, so the app re-derives row visibility by hand: the second copy of access that Invariant 6 forbids.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 6 puts access at the Data boundary and the row's read policy already says who may know the row exists; A reuses it, so a banned member reads nothing and a field-level `@guarded` stays redacted in the image (Invariant 7). The deleted-row case is the one departure and falls to the gate level, stated in `docs/trail.md`.

- **FJS-D1056 — What is the bulk path for loading an `@@fts` model?** Found fixing `FJS-2653`. Every row written fires `<table>_fts_insert` (and `<table>_search_insert` when the model has an `@label`), each an FTS5 trigram insert that touches many pages, so a load pays per row. Measured on a scratch bench (60k rows, 4 text columns, 1,000 per transaction): 16.6 s with the trigger, 15.6 s with it and a 256 MB `cache_size`, 5.2 s with no trigger and one `INSERT INTO c_fts(c_fts) VALUES('rebuild')` at the end (the rebuild is 5.0 of those). Red assertion: nothing in `db` lets a caller skip the per-row index writes and pay one rebuild, so a move or import has no spelling for it but raw DDL.
  - **A** — a scoped client verb, `await db.$deferIndex(['Contact'], async tx => { … })`: inside the write transaction (so no other writer sees the missing triggers, and a throw rolls the DDL back with the rows) it drops the named models' `_fts_*` and `_search_*` triggers, runs the callback, then rebuilds each per-model index with `'rebuild'` and the shared `_search` rows with `searchBackfillSql`, and recreates the triggers. One verb, built from statements `migrate.js` and `ddl.js` already write. Cost: the rebuild is O(table), so it only wins when the load is large against what is already there, and a caller must know that.
  - **B** — an option on the write, `createMany({ data, index: 'defer' })`. The unit is wrong: each call would rebuild the whole table, so a 1,000-row call into a 100k-row table is slower than today, and a loop of calls pays N rebuilds.
  - **C** — no new surface: raise the default `cache_size` for a file holding an `@@fts` model. The row measured 4.9 s down to 0.7 s at 256 MB, but this bench moved 16.6 s to 15.6 s, so the gain depends on the data, and the memory is held for every process that opens the file.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only one that names the unit the saving belongs to, a load rather than a call, and it keeps the trigger set one owner's output (`ddl.js`) instead of leaving raw DDL to each importer; B is a regression for the common small call and C is a bet on a corpus.

- **FJS-D1057 — Under `resolveFrom: 'schema'`, is a relative `env("VAR")` path written against the schema's app root or against the process working directory?** Found fixing `FJS-2622`. `resolveDbPath` (`src/core/databases.js`) anchors a literal, an env value and an env default alike, so `DATABASE_URL=./db/app.db` follows the schema FILE. Bun hands `import.meta.url` as the real path, so a scratch app whose `db/` or `api/` is a symlink passes the ORIGINAL's schema path and `createClient({ path, resolveFrom: 'schema' })` reports `/orig/db/app.db` while the operator typed `./db/app.db` in the scratch directory. Probe: a schema at `orig/db/schema.lite`, cwd `scratch/`, `DATABASE_URL=./db/app.db`, `path: realpath('./db/schema.lite')` → `$databases.main.path` is `orig/db/app.db`. `ela/api/src/core/db.ts` documents relying on the anchor for the env value, so an operator's env is today read as app-root-relative.
  - **A** — an env-STATED value (set in the process environment) is relative to the process, like a `createClient({ db })` override; only a literal and an env DEFAULT anchor to the schema's app root. A scratch copy redirects by env and cwd alone. It reverses the documented `ela` reliance and `FJS-449` for the env half (`studio` run from `db/` with `DATABASE_URL=./db/app.db` then means `db/db/app.db`).
  - **B** — keep the anchor and make it visible: log the resolved absolute path (and `resolveFrom`) at open, and warn when an env-stated relative path resolves differently under the anchor than under the cwd. No behavior change; a scratch app still opens the original but says so.
  - **C** — refuse an env-stated relative path under `resolveFrom: 'schema'` that differs between the two anchors, naming both, so the operator states an absolute path or `:memory:`.
  - **Recommend A** — an env value is typed by an operator in a shell, and the cwd is the only directory the operator can see; the schema file's directory is a fact the operator never states and Bun's symlink resolution changes without telling them, which is how a scratch run reached production data.

- **FJS-D1058 — How does `@@transitions` declare that a move to a state the row is already past is dropped, not refused?** Found fixing `FJS-2435`. `checkTransitions` (`packages/litestone/src/core/client.js:1600`) has one answer for a write whose `(from, to)` matches no move: `TransitionViolationError`. A carrier that sends `delivered` before `sent` leaves the row at `delivered`, then writes `status: 'sent'` and is refused as an illegal move when it is a stale one; quo keeps its own `RANK` in `inbox.ts` and the schema carries a `WANTED (Q3)` comment. Red test: with `@@transitions(status, deliver: sent -> delivered)` and a row at `delivered`, `update({ data: { status: 'sent' } })` throws today where the declaration wants a no-op.
  - **A** — a block-level word on the transitions, `@@transitions(status, ..., behind: drop)`, where a write to a state that is BEHIND the row's in declaration order (enum order) is dropped — the column keeps its value, the rest of the payload is still written, the audit trail records a skipped move — and any other unmatched move still refuses. Rank is the enum's declared order, so no second list.
  - **B** — an explicit rank: `@@transitions(status, rank: [queued, sent, delivered])`, states at equal rank refuse each other, a lower rank drops. A second list beside the enum, but it lets `failed` and `delivered` sit at one rank, which quo's `RANK` needs and enum order cannot say.
  - **C** — no schema word; the app's hook drops the stale write before it reaches the Data boundary. Keeps litestone smaller, and is what quo does today; the cost is that every carrier-driven status column repeats the rank table, and the audit trail never sees the dropped receipt.
  - **Recommend B** — quo's `RANK` has two states at rank 2, which enum order cannot express, and a rank list is one line the schema owns instead of a table every app copies; the drop must keep the rest of the payload (`deliveredAt`) written, which is the part to put in the red test.

- **FJS-D1059 — How does one model get its own retention window while staying in its database with its relations: a model attribute, a per-model map in the database block, or the app's own sweep?** Found fixing `FJS-2289`. `retention` parses only inside `database { … }` (`parser.js:296`), so serpgrid's Ranking (90 days, beneath kept-for-ever Scan and GridPoint) had to move to `database serp { retention 90d }`, where `@relation` to GridPoint is refused ('a relation cannot cross databases') and `migrate create` writes a new empty table there and a commented `-- DROP TABLE` in main. `FJS-D657` coined no retention word until "a second product whose clock is a column trait" — serpgrid is that product (Ranking's clock is a column, not `createdAt`; `FJS-2285`'s `skipped: 'no createdAt DateTime'` is the same gap). Red test: a `main` schema with `model Ranking { … scanId Int @relation(…) \n @@retention(90d) }` beside `Scan` parses, validates, and `$retain()` removes only Ranking rows older than 90d while Scan rows and the relation survive; today the attribute is an unknown `@@`-attribute.
  - **A** — a model attribute `@@retention(90d, from: capturedAt)`, the database word reused at model grain; `from:` names a `DateTime` column and defaults to `createdAt`, so it also answers `FJS-2285`'s non-`createdAt` clock. The sweep (`runSqliteRetention`, `retention.js:120`) reads the model's own window before the database's; a database `retention` stays the default for models that name none. The model keeps its file, relations and cascade; no migration moves rows. New attribute, one word for both grains (`VOCABULARY.md` Retention row widens).
  - **B** — a per-model map inside the database block (`database main { retention { Ranking 90d } }`), no new attribute. The window lives away from the model it governs and cannot name a clock column, so `FJS-2285` stays open.
  - **C** — no schema word; the app sweeps its own table (`delete … where scannedAt < ?` in a job), as serpgrid's `domain/scan.ts` does for the cascade. Litestone stays smaller; every app repeats the sweep, `$retain()` never reports the table, and the trail sees no window.
  - **Recommend A** — retention is the word `$retain()` and the database block already own, a column-trait clock is the exact condition `FJS-D657` waited on, and it is the only option that keeps the relations without moving rows; the red test above is the first step.

- **FJS-D1060 — How does a declared machine (`@@transitions(field, declaredBy:, scope:)`) reach `x-transitions`, whose readers all iterate moves keyed by name?** Found fixing FJS-2920. Red assertion: `generateJsonSchema(parse(SCHEMA).schema).$defs.Issue['x-transitions']` over `@@transitions(stateId, declaredBy: WorkflowTransition, scope: teamId)` is `{ stateId: {} }` today — an empty machine, because `a.transitions` is `{}` for a declared one ([jsonschema.js](packages/litestone/src/jsonschema.js) § x-transitions). The moves are ROWS a tenant wrote, so the static schema cannot list them; sierra's `field-rules.js:288` and `resource.transitions`, mcp's `projection.ts:222` and `breadcrumbs.ts:87`, and `jsonschema-snapshot.js:292` all read each entry under a field as `{ from, to, gate, system }` by move name, and `fli check`'s `transition-methods` reads the schema's moves, not this JSON.
  - **A** — the field's entry carries a marker beside no moves: `x-transitions.stateId = { 'x-declared-by': { model: 'WorkflowTransition', scope: 'teamId' } }`. One keyword, one place; but every reader iterating `Object.entries(moves)` sees `x-declared-by` as a move named that and crashes on `move.from.includes`, so each of the five readers grows a guard and a missed one fails at runtime.
  - **B** — a sibling keyword, `x-declared-transitions: { stateId: { declaredBy, scope } }`, beside `x-transitions` the way `x-commitments` sits (`FJS-D353`); `x-transitions` omits a declared machine entirely (no empty `{}`), so no existing reader changes shape. A reader that wants the machine opts in; sierra's `resource.transitions` answers `[]` client-side for it and a screen asks the server (`table.transitions(row)` serves it).
  - **Recommend B** — `x-transitions` has one shape (moves by name) that five readers rely on, a declared machine has no moves to put there, and a sibling keyword is the precedent `x-commitments` set for a declaration that is not a move.

- **FJS-D1061 — Does a document numbered when it is ISSUED (gapless per scope, given by the move that seals it) get a word on `@sequence`, or stay the hand spelling?** Found fixing FJS-2167. `applySequences` (`stamps.js:165`) runs on INSERT only, bumping `_litestone_sequences` for `(model, field, scope)`, so the number is a creation-order number and a deleted draft's number is never reused. Red assertion: `Invoice { number Int? @sequence(scope: orgId, on: issue) }` with `@@transitions(status, issue: draft -> issued)` — create three drafts, issue the second then the first, expect numbers 1 and 2 in issue order; today the schema does not parse (`on:` is not an argument) and the column is minted at create, so the drafts hold 1, 2, 3 before any issue.
  - **A** — `@sequence(scope: orgId, on: issue)`: the column is nullable, `on:` names a move of this model's `@@transitions`, and the counter bump happens inside that move's compare-and-swap write, in the same transaction, so a rolled-back issue takes its number back. A create names no value; an explicit value on the move is refused like any `@sequence` update. Same counter table, same `(model, field, scope)` key; the parser checks `on:` against the declared moves.
  - **B** — a sibling attribute `@issuedSequence(scope: orgId, on: issue)`, so `@sequence` keeps its one meaning (number at create) and the new word carries the nullable-column and move-bound rules. Two words for one counter table.
  - **C** — no word: document the hand spelling in `docs/sequences.md` (max + 1 read inside the write transaction of a `@@transitions` move beside `@@unique([orgId, number], where: number != null)`), and close the row as by design. Zero code; the measured lago run (24 invoices, three processes, none twice) is the evidence it works, but every app re-derives the transaction and the partial unique, and the `where:` unique is the half an author forgets.
  - **Recommend A** — the transition is already the schema's name for "the move that seals it" and already runs a transaction; `on:` is a reference to it, not a new noun, and the counter table already gives the no-reuse and rollback properties C has to re-spell by hand.

- **FJS-D1062 — How does an aggregate name a member of a Json column at call time, and what refuses a non-numeric member?** Found fixing `FJS-2154`. `aggregate()` builds `SUM("col")` from a column NAME via `_aggCol` (`client.js` `aggregate`), `refuseAggregateKeys` grades that name against `aggregatableKeysFor`, and a Json column is refused as a value aggregate; a path is a second kind of name, so it needs a shape, a grade and an answer for a member that is text. The red test: `db.event.aggregate({ _sum: { properties: { path: ['amount'] } } })` throws today and should answer the exact sum under the caller's client, and the same call over a `@guarded` Json column must still refuse.
  - **A** — a `path` on the value aggregate: `_sum: { properties: { path: ['amount'] } }` (also `_avg` `_min` `_max`, and a named aggregate's `sum: { column, path }`), compiled to `json_extract("properties", ?)` with the path BOUND (Invariant 8), keyed in the result by `properties.amount`. The column goes through `fieldReadRefusal` unchanged, so `@guarded`/`@encrypted` refuse as now. A member that is not a number refuses at read: the SQL adds `json_type(...) IN ('integer','real')` to a count of the strays and throws naming the first, rather than answering `0`. Past 2^53 refuses as `exactSum` does.
  - **B** — a new verb, `db.event.sumPath(column, path, args)`, leaving `_sum` column-only. Costs a fourth way to aggregate beside `aggregate`, `groupBy` and the named form, and a `having`/`orderBy`/`by` over a member then has no spelling.
  - **C** — decline: a Json whose shape rows declare per tenant is the case `FJS-D227` says to hold as columns; the engine keeps folding in JS or `asSystem().sql` and the row closes as by design. Zero code, and metering stays the one place a policy-free `sql` is the answer.
  - **Recommend A** — a path is a name the caller already supplies and `aggregate` already grades every name; one key on the existing shape keeps `groupBy`, `having` and the named aggregate working, and the numeric refusal is what keeps `SUM` over text from answering a plausible `0` (the failure `FJS-2154`'s own doc section warns of).

- **FJS-D1064 — Where does the freed-page scrub live: derived at connect from `@personal`, inside `forget()` only, or its own verb?** Found fixing FJS-2099. `forget()` and the walk (`IDEAS/forgetting.md` § 4) are unbuilt, so today nothing reaches `PRAGMA secure_delete` or `VACUUM`: after a `@personal` column is nulled or its row deleted, the old value is still in the file's bytes (jazzhr: 209 of 288 emails on delete, 90 on tombstone; `secure_delete = ON` through `asSystem().sql` leaves 18 and does not persist across connections; `VACUUM` leaves 0). The red test is: write a row with a distinctive `@personal` value, null it (and, separately, delete the row), read the main file's bytes plus `-wal`, and assert the value is absent. `pragmas.js` already says a vacuum is a blocking rewrite and "the operator's call", so the compaction half is the contested one.
  - **A** — derived at connect: a schema that declares any `@personal` column opens its write connection with `secure_delete = ON` (a pragma the connection owner in `pragmas.js` applies, beside `applyBusyTimeout`), so every null and delete zeroes its page content with no verb. `forget()` later adds a `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` and a compaction as its last statements. Closes most of the 209/90 now; costs a write-path slowdown on every table of such a schema, and leaves the WAL and any pre-existing freed pages to the verb.
  - **B** — inside `forget()` only, as § 4 already says ("runs its statements under `secure_delete` and leaves the file compacted"): the row waits for the walk to be built, and the scrub is its closing step. Nothing else on the connection changes; an ordinary `delete` of a `@personal` row stays recoverable from freed pages until the app calls `forget()`.
  - **C** — its own verb (`db.$scrub()`), callable by an app's retention job after its own deletes; `forget()` calls it. Adds a noun to the surface that A and B do not.
  - **Recommend A** — the paper's silence test (§ 6.9) is *no store litestone owns holds a `@personal` value*, and an ordinary retention `delete` breaks it today with no `forget()` involved; deriving the pragma from the mark is the owner-of-the-translation answer and adds no word. `forget()`'s compaction rides on top when the walk is built.

- **FJS-D1065 — How does `test:mutate` grade an `@@allow` whose predicate crosses a relation (`check()`, a path, `.some()`)?** Found fixing FJS-2052. `verifyRowPolicies` returns `skipped` for every read/update rule holding a `check`/`path`/`some` node (`src/testing.js` `_hasCheckNode`), and `mutationScore` counts `skipped` as inconclusive (`src/mutate.js` INCONCLUSIVE), so the membership shape oracle emits can lose any `@@allow` unseen. Probe, a six-rule Org/Member/Note schema through `mutationScore({ kinds: ['allow-drop'] })`: `{ total: 6, graded: 6, killed: 0, survived: 6 }`, every rule `skipped … crosses a relation`. The red assertion is `killed > 0` for that run; a green one needs a seeded parent that admits the principal and one that does not, which the one-row factory cannot build.
  - **A** — Grade them: `verifyRowPolicies` seeds a small world (a parent the principal reaches through the delegated rule's own `_interestingValues`, one it does not, a child under each) and takes the expected verdict from `evalJs` over the ORIGINAL schema's shapes against the mutant's rows. Real kills; ~150 lines in `testing.js` and original-schema shapes built outside a client (`shapeFor` is private to `client.js`).
  - **B** — Say it: an `allow-drop` on a model whose rules were all `skipped` is reported in its own bucket (`unreached — the derived checks cannot exercise this rule`), apart from `survived` and outside the score's denominator, and the footer names the app-supplied `check` as the way to grade them (lago's `mutate-access.ts`). Kills nothing, but the output stops implying the rules were exercised, which is the row's stated cost.
  - **C** — Both, in order: B now, A as its own row once a second app wants it. Add the per-conjunct drop operator from the row's last note as a separate row.
  - **Recommend C** — B is small and closes the dishonesty today; A is a new oracle whose design (what counts as a world, which principal) deserves its own paper rather than a fix-loop session.

- **FJS-D1066 — How does a data move place a row at a non-entry `@@transitions` state?** `asSystem().client.create({ data: { stage: 'client' } })` → `TransitionViolationError` and `system: ['@@transitions']` is refused by name (D575's list is closed: a `@system` column, a field with declared moves, or `'@@gate'`). Three callers have now hit it (ela, sstime, maidtech); the raw `sys.sql` UPDATE `FJS-D502` leaves skips the audit trail (no row says 148 contacts were placed) and, on a `@@commitment` model, leaves a window between the create and the placement in which the sweep fires a years-old row (maidtech `actions.ts` closes it by hand with one `sys.$transaction` per batch). `FJS-D502` named `asSystem({ lift: ['transitions'] })` as the answer once a third caller appeared, but `FJS-D575` has since spelled "what this call does as the application" as the per-call `system:` list. Red test: `litestone.<model>.createMany({ data: [{ status: 'succeeded' }], system: ['@@transitions'] })` as `asSystem()` inserts the row at `succeeded`, and the audit row's `meta.lifted` is `['@@transitions']`.
  - **A** — `system: ['@@transitions']` on `create` / `createMany` only: graded SYSTEM for that one call, the start-state rule (D470) lifted for it, the audit row's `lifted` naming it (the path `'@@gate'` already takes through `buildLogEntry`), no other write lifted. `update` / `updateMany` stay refused (a move is still a declared move, D502). Extends D575's closed list by one entry and keeps its single spelling; the `@@commitment` window closes because the row is born at its state, so there is nothing to sweep.
  - **B** — `db.asSystem({ lift: ['transitions'] })` as D502 wrote it: a client-wide reach that also lifts `update` moves and the bulk-write refusal. More power than any of the three callers asked for, a second spelling beside `system:`, and a client that lifts an integrity rule for every call made through it.
  - **C** — Neither: keep the `sys.sql` path and add what is missing around it — a `fli check` rule naming a `@@commitment` model whose move creates at the entry state, and a hazard line. No new option; the audit gap and the commitment window stay with the caller.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the narrowest lift that serves all three measured callers (each only creates at a state), reuses D575's per-call spelling and the existing `lifted` audit field, and closes the commitment window by construction.

- **FJS-D1067 — How does a schema say a per-parent cap that is a column on the parent and counts a subset of children, and is it graded on every writer?** Found fixing `FJS-2000`. Chatwoot's `Member.maxOpen` ("at most N OPEN conversations assigned to a person") is app code in `assign/next.ts`: held only inside the creating `$transaction`, and an agent's PATCH of `assigneeId` is held to nothing. `@maxItems` (`FJS-D347`, `cardinality.js` `buildCardinalityMap`) reads a literal `attr.value` and counts every non-soft-deleted child, so neither the bound's source nor the `status == 'open'` subset has a spelling. The grade already runs at the outermost commit under the write lock and already fires on a child write that moves a parent's count, so the enforcement half exists; what is missing is the word. Red test: a schema with `Member.maxOpen Int?` and `Conversation.assigneeId`, `maxOpen = 1`, two `update({ assigneeId })` of open conversations outside any `$transaction` — the second is accepted today.
  - **A** — extend the relation bound: `@maxItems(maxOpen, where: "status == 'open'")` on `Member.conversations`, the bound a sibling column name on the parent row, the subset the same `where:` string `@from` already takes. No new attribute; `buildCardinalityMap` resolves the column per parent at grade time (one COUNT with the filter appended to `rule.filters`, the bound read from the parent row, NULL = unbounded). Cost: `@maxItems` now takes a number or a column name, and `@minItems` gets the same for symmetry or is left asymmetric.
  - **B** — a model-level word on the child beside `@@exclude` (`FJS-D346`): `@@capacity(assigneeId, by: Member.maxOpen, where: "status == 'open'")`. Reads as the rule it is (a cap on rows per key) and leaves `@maxItems` a literal, but is a second counting declaration beside the first, graded by a second ledger path.
  - **C** — decline: the count-then-write under `$transaction` is the intended shape (`$lock` documents it) and the PATCH hole is closed by an app `beforeUpdate` hook that opens one. Zero code, but Invariant 6 says access rules live in the schema, not hooks, and the cap is exactly what the stressor measured going unenforced.
  - **Recommend A** — it adds a column-valued bound and a `where:` to the one declaration `FJS-D347` already owns, reuses `@from`'s `where:` grammar and the commit-time grade, and covers PATCH and concurrent writers by the same ledger with no second owner.

- **FJS-D1071 — Should `litestone migrate create` / `migrate apply` refuse a schema `createClient` refuses at boot?** Found fixing `FJS-1936`. `advise` and `fli check`'s `schema-runs` build the client over memory and exit 1 (`cmdAdvise`, `FJS-1242`); `migrate create` on a schema whose `@@allow` reads an undeclared `auth().contactId` under `@@auth` still writes the migration and `migrate apply` applies it, so a database exists for a schema the app cannot boot with. Red assertion: `litestone migrate create --schema <that file>` exits 0 and writes `migrations/*.sql`.
  - **A** — refuse in both: lift the probe out of `cmdAdvise` into one helper (Invariant 4, one owner) and call it first in `migrate create` and `migrate apply`. No unbootable schema reaches a migration file; costs a memory-client build per migrate and blocks a migration written for a schema mid-edit.
  - **B** — refuse in `migrate apply` only: a draft migration may be written while the schema is half-done, but nothing reaches a database that the app cannot boot over. Smaller reach; `create` still wrote a file nobody can apply until fixed.
  - **C** — neither: `advise` and `fli check` are the surface for "what this schema says wrong", and `migrate` stays a differ. Zero code; the row's measured sequence (a migration written and applied for an unbootable schema) stays possible when a person skips both.
  - **Recommend A** — a migration is the one schema act that writes state outside the repo, and `FJS-1242` already put the probe at the CLI boundary; one shared helper keeps a single owner for "the client refuses this".

- **FJS-D1072 — How does a view state its key and its parent relation: the model spellings (`@unique`, `@relation` on a column) or two view-level attributes?** Found fixing `FJS-1810`. `parseView` (`src/core/parser.js` `parseView`) refuses every field attribute outside `VIEW_COLUMN_ATTRS` (`@money`, `@scale`, `@unit`), so `id String @unique` and `conversation Conversation @relation(fields: [conversationId], references: [id])` on a `view` both fail at parse. Red assertion: parsing a `view TimelineItem` whose `id` carries `@unique` and which declares a `@relation` to `Conversation` is `valid: false` today; once admitted, `findManyCursor({ orderBy: [{ at: 'desc' }] })` must append `id` as the tiebreak instead of throwing "not a total order", and a view `@@gate` naming `check(conversation)` must compile. Either way the key is a CLAIM about `@@sql`'s output that no DDL enforces (a `UNION ALL` can repeat it), so the choice also decides what, if anything, grades it.
  - **A** — the model spellings: `@unique` (or `@id`) on a view column and a relation field with `@relation(...)`, admitted into `VIEW_COLUMN_ATTRS`' successor. Knowing a model predicts a view; the policy compiler, `buildRelations()` and `shapes[view.name]` already read these facts off the view-as-model stub, so no second reader. Cost: `@unique` on a view reads as a storage constraint it is not, and the parse comment ("no column to constrain") has to be rewritten.
  - **B** — two view-level attributes, `@@key(id)` and `@@parent(conversation: Conversation via conversationId)`: states plainly that these are promises about `@@sql`, never constraints. Cost: two new words in the catalog (tier, axis), a second spelling of a key and a relation that models already have, and the stub must translate them into the facts the compilers read.
  - **C** — neither: close as the header-table (`@@arc`) or service-merge spelling the row measured, and document it. Zero code; the policy stays restated in the service and a missed header row stays silent.
  - **Recommend A** — one spelling per fact (`FJS-D46`'s view-as-model stub already means a view is read as a model), and the unenforced-key risk is identical under B, so B buys a new word and no safety.

- **FJS-D1150 — How does a child's read delegate a NARROWER rule than the parent's whole read: a named rule on the parent (`check(num, 'inbox')`), or one more hop inside `some()`?** Found fixing `FJS-1801`. Probed in the tree: `VALID_POLICY_OPS` ([parser.js](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js)) is the fixed five, so `@@allow('inbox', …)` is a `ParseError` and `check(num, 'inbox')` has nothing to name; `check(field, op)` accepts any string at parse and only resolves it against those five. `Conversation @@allow('read', check(phoneNumber))` therefore carries PhoneNumber's admin clause (`check(org, 'update')`) onto every thread, and chatwoot's `check(inbox)` carried the visitor clause onto an agent's note (`FJS-1953`'s revert). The second half, a `NumberMember`'s user being a Member of the number's org, is `this.userId in phoneNumber.org.members`: two hops, refused by `FJS-D221`. The red test: the quo `telnyx.test.ts` § *Who sees a number* case that pins an admin who answers no number reading all its threads, flipped to expect none.
  - **A** — A NAMED rule: `@@allow('inbox', members.some(userId == auth().id))` declares a rule that is not one of the five operations, and `check(phoneNumber, 'inbox')` delegates exactly it. A named rule is never applied by the engine itself, only through a `check()`, so it widens no read, and it reads as the question the child means (*a member of the number*). Fixes the first half and the chatwoot leak; the second (`org.members`) stays out of reach. Cost: `VALID_POLICY_OPS` stops being closed, so a typo in `@@allow('raed', …)` is no longer a parse error unless named rules are declared by a separate word (`@@rule('inbox', …)`), which is a second noun.
  - **B** — One more hop INSIDE `some()` (and a path): `phoneNumber.members.some(userId == auth().id)` and `this.userId in phoneNumber.org.members` as the two-hop EXISTS chain `FJS-D1027`'s option C already names. Fixes both halves with no new word and no delegation, so the child states what it means. Cost: re-rules D221 and D566's deliberate one-hop bound, the compiler and `evalSome` grow a chain, and every extra hop is another unindexed correlated scan (`advise`'s `foreign-key-without-index` has to follow the chain).
  - **C** — Neither: close the row as a measurement; the app restates the narrow rule with a copy (`Conversation.memberIds`-style stamped column, or `numberId`'s `members.some` on a denormalized `Conversation.phoneNumber` key). Zero code; the copies drift on revoke, which `FJS-D566` was written to end.
  - **Recommend A and B both, A first** — A is the only shape that stops `check(parent)` inheriting a clause the child never meant (`FJS-1789`, `FJS-1801`, `FJS-1953` are one defect), and B is `FJS-D1027`'s C, owed a second app's evidence that Quo now supplies; if one must go first, A, and declare it with `@@allow` on a name the schema lists rather than a separate noun.

- **FJS-D1096 — How does a policy name a grant level that sits between two operations, so a child model can reach it through `check()`?** Notion's *can comment* (read < comment < edit) is not one of the five operations a parent has (`POLICY_OPS`, `access.js:28`; `VALID_POLICY_OPS`, `parser.js:3385`), so `Comment @@allow('create', check(block, 'update'))` refuses a comment-level guest; `probes/q4/comment-level.ts` still prints both spellings refused at parse (*crosses two relations*, *tests rows two relations away*). Red test's assertion: a guest granted `comment` creates a Comment on her page (today 403), while a `read` guest still cannot.
  - **A** — `@@allow` takes a declared extra operation: `Page { @@operations('comment') @@allow('comment', access.some(userId == auth().id && level in ['comment','edit','full'])) }`, and a child says `check(block, 'comment')` (Block forwards with `@@allow('comment', check(page, 'comment'))`). The operation is a name a model declares, never a sixth CRUD verb; undeclared names stay a parse error, and the grade stays at the Data boundary.
  - **B** — A policy may cross two relations when every hop is a `check()`-style delegation to a rule the far model already states: `check(block.page, 'comment')`. No new operation, but it breaks the one-hop rule (`FJS-499`) and the EXPLAIN-no-automatic-index guarantee.
  - **C** — The level becomes a first-class value, `@@level('Page', access.some(...) → level)` ordered by an `enum` (`read < comment < edit < full`), and `check(rel, level >= 'comment')` compares. One mechanism for every ordered grant, but it coins a noun and a second grading axis beside the operations.
  - **D** — No change: the child carries the grant (a copied `pageId` plus a denormalized `commentable Boolean` the owner of the page maintains in the same transaction). Stays inside four operations; the denormalized flag is a trigger-shaped hazard the schema cannot enforce.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the smallest spelling that keeps Invariant 6 (access declared in the schema), reuses `check()`'s one-hop delegation, and leaves the operation set closed unless a model opts in; `FJS-1245`'s *named move more permissive than an edit* reads as the same shape (a declared name on the parent) and could ride the same keyword.

- **FJS-D1104 — Does the trail get a read grain at the DATA OWNER's level (a count per row per period, no actor, no clock, no correlation id), or does that stay an app-written `@@anonymous` counter?** Found fixing `FJS-1424`. `@@trail(audit, reads: true)` writes one entry per read carrying `actorId`, `createdAt` and `correlationId` (parser `trail` case, ~2652), so a search that reads Joe's lens leaves a per-person clock of every search that joins to the query text in each provider's log (`FJS-1413`); `@@anonymous` (`FJS-D349`) turns the model's logging off whole. Between the two there is no spelling for *Joe's golf lens was used N times this month*. The red test would assert a read of a lens by a grantee leaves a row naming the lens and a count and no actor, clock or correlation id; there is no spelling to write it in until this is ruled.
  - **A** — A third value of `reads`: `@@trail(audit, reads: counted)`. The trail keeps one row per (model, record id, UTC day) holding `n`, upserted on each read; no actor, no per-read clock, no correlation id. One new value on an existing option and one new trail-side table. Leaves the single-grantee hole (the count is that grantee's topic frequency), so it needs a floor (`min`) or a documented refusal for a model with one reader.
  - **B** — No framework change. The app keeps an `@@anonymous` counter model (Portal's `Usage`) and increments it in the service that reads; the trail stays reader-grained and the doc says which one to use. Zero new nouns; the cost is each app re-deriving a counter, and nothing stops it being written beside an attributed read in one transaction (the `FJS-D349` check refuses only a logged WRITE beside it).
  - **C** — Coarsen the existing entry instead: `reads: true` plus a word that drops `actorId` and `correlationId` and rounds `createdAt` to the day. Reuses the trail table, but a coarse per-read row is still one row per read, so rowid order re-attributes it exactly as `FJS-D349` describes; it only works if rows are folded, which is A.
  - **Recommend B** — the row's own measurement says it works today and that the unsolved part (a lone grantee's count is their topic frequency) is a question of grain no attribute fixes; A earns its place when a second app needs the counter, and then as `reads: counted` with the floor decided against two real cases rather than Portal's one.

- **FJS-D1105 — Should a fresh app root carry four markdown files (README, AGENTS, AGENTS.project, CLAUDE), or fewer?** `appAgentsMd`/`appAgentsProjectMd`/`appClaudeMd` in `packages/cli/core/app-config.js` write three agent files that import each other (`CLAUDE.md` → `AGENTS.md` → `AGENTS.project.md`) beside the README; the layering keeps the framework's text and the app's own apart, and `AGENTS.project.md` is a seven-line stub until somebody fills it. Found fixing FJS-2972.
  - **A** — Keep all four: the import chain is why framework text can be rewritten without touching what the developer wrote.
  - **B** — Drop `AGENTS.project.md`: `AGENTS.md` ends with a `## This app` section the developer owns and the framework never rewrites after `fli new`; `CLAUDE.md` still imports `AGENTS.md`. Three files.
  - **C** — Drop `CLAUDE.md` too and name `AGENTS.md` in the README for Claude Code users. Two files, but Claude Code never opens `AGENTS.md` on its own.
  - **Recommend B** — the stub is the only file a fresh app carries with no content of its own, and nothing the framework writes afterwards touches either half.

- **FJS-D1106 — Should a nested hasMany/m2m create write its children as one `createMany` batch, or keep each child a full `create` verb?** Found fixing `FJS-3002`. `processHasManyNested` (`client.js` ~5601) awaits `tbl.create` once per child. Measured on `:memory:`, 200 children under one parent: nested 7.75 ms, the same rows through `createMany` 1.52 ms (38.7 µs vs 7.6 µs per child). The saving is the per-verb overhead, not statements: `createMany` also runs one INSERT per row, and the `onQuery` tap reports it once per shape. What a batch changes is what each child sees: `beforeCreate` gets one call with a `data` array instead of one call per row, the write event is one `collection` event instead of N `row` events (`FJS-307`), and a child carrying its own nested ops cannot be batched at all.
  - **A** — Close as designed. A nested child is a full create so every plugin hook, `$tapEvents` consumer and live store sees the same N row events whether the caller nested or looped; the 5x is the price of that. Document it in the nested-writes doc.
  - **B** — Batch only the flat case. A hasMany `create` whose children name no nested ops of their own goes through `prepareCreateMany` + `createManyPlan` inside the parent's unit; anything else stays per child. The event for a nested child becomes one collection event, so the batch is announced the way `createMany` is, and the doc states that nesting and looping no longer announce alike.
  - **C** — Batch flat children and keep per-row events: `createManyPlan` runs, then the announcement is split back into one `row` event per child. Same speed as B, same hook shape as B for `beforeCreate`, but no consumer sees a difference on the event side. Costs a second announce path beside the owner (Invariant 4).
  - **Recommend C** — a consumer of write events should not be able to tell a nested create from a loop, so the gain has to land without moving the event; the `beforeCreate` array-vs-row difference is the one visible change left, and `createMany` already sets that precedent for every bulk write.

- **FJS-D1108 — What exactly does `FJS-D908`'s zone-aware `where` operator mean, and how does the sync where-builder learn the zones present?** Found fixing `FJS-1234`. `FJS-D908` ruled `{ startsAt: { localTime: { atOrBefore: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone' } } }` lowered to `(zone = ? AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`, and left three things the lowering has to decide. (1) `08:00` on WHICH day: `atOrBefore` reads as a time-of-day test on `startsAt` ("starts at or before 08:00 local" — the `tooLate` rows), while the reminder the row needs is "08:00 on the morning of `startsAt`'s local date has passed", an instant per row. (2) `buildWhere` (`query.js`) and `buildWhereWithEncryption` (`client.js`) are sync and called from count, update and delete as well as `findMany`, and `SELECT DISTINCT zone` is a query, which in the worker engine is async. (3) The row's own measured case is a zone two relations away (`Shift → Employee → User`), which A does not reach. Red test: `findMany({ where: { startsAt: { localTime: … } } })` on a `@zone`-column model throws the unknown-operator error today and should return only the rows whose zone-local reminder instant is at or before `now`.
  - **A** — the operator is "the wall-clock `<time>` on `startsAt`'s own local date, in the row's zone, has passed `<now>`": `{ startsAt: { localTime: { reached: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone', at: now } } }`, name it for the reminder question rather than `atOrBefore`. The zones come from one awaited `SELECT DISTINCT zone` before the SQL is built, so the builder stays sync and the read path gains one pre-step (the extensible/encrypted rewrites' slot); it is refused on `count`/`update`/`delete` by name until a caller needs it there. Same-row zone only, as ruled.
  - **B** — as A, but enumerate rather than read: group `Intl.supportedValuesOf('timeZone')` by their current offset and lower to `(zone IN (…) AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`, one branch per distinct offset. No extra query and the builder stays sync everywhere, at the cost of a wider OR and a zone name ICU does not list silently matching nothing.
  - **C** — keep `atOrBefore` as the time-of-day test it reads as (the `tooLate` question) and answer the reminder question with `FJS-D963`'s derived instant column instead, so no operator is added and the two-relation case is solved by the same mechanism. Closes `FJS-1234` by closing `FJS-D963`; the `where` stays instant-only.
  - **Recommend C** — the row's measured case is two relations away, which A and B both leave open, and `FJS-D963` already asks litestone to derive the instant a wall clock and its zone resolve to; that is a column `where` can index, the zone-correction staleness it names is the same defect, and it avoids the sync-builder question altogether. If `FJS-D963` is refused, A.

- **FJS-D1110 — Does `FJS-1980`'s sighting reopen `FJS-D817`: where does an enum member's tone live so `<Cell>` can draw it?** Found fixing `FJS-1980`. `FJS-D817` refused `@tone` in `.lite` ("a `.lite` file names no UI word") and named `FJS-1980` as the sighting that reopens it; the settled home is the resource's `<script module>` (`ui-hazards` resources-and-forms). `Cell.mesa:219` draws an enum as `<Badge>{enumLabel(value)}</Badge>` with no tone, so ela keeps `TaskStatus`/`AssetStatus`/`InvoiceStatus` maps in the template. A red test would assert a Cell over an enum field with a stated tone renders `.badge.<tone>`; today it renders none.
  - **A** — keep `FJS-D817`: no tone in the schema or in `<Cell>`; apps keep a template map. Close `FJS-1980` as accepted cost.
  - **B** — the resource's `<script module>` states a tone map per enum field and `<Cell>` reads it from the resource, so the map lives once and `.lite` stays free of UI words.
  - **C** — reopen `FJS-D817`: `@tone(<ident>)` on a member beside `@label`, emitted as `x-tones`, folded into `rule.options` by `buildFieldRules()`; the tone list moves to toolbelt so the parser refuses an unknown one (the withdrawn paper's option B).
  - **Recommend B** — it removes the repeated map without undoing the ruling that a schema names no UI word, and `<Cell>` already receives the resource.

- **FJS-D1111 — Does `upsert`'s `update` half take an atomic operator (reopening `FJS-D27`'s refusal), and does the one-statement fast path learn a composite unique `where`?** Found fixing `FJS-1498`. `extractWriteOps(updateData, { where: 'upsert' })` (`client.js` `planUpsert`) refuses `{ count: { increment: 1 } }` because `FJS-D27` judged the fast path (SET from `excluded`) and the slow path (`update()`) could not both apply it. Two facts cut against that: `upsertPlan` builds its SET itself, so it can emit `"count" = "count" + ?` as `updateMany` does, and the slow path's `this.update(...)` already applies operators. The row's own case, `where: { source, quarter }`, never reaches the fast path anyway: `planUpsert` returns null unless `where` names ONE column in `_upsertUniqueCols()`, so a pair falls to findFirst, then create, then update after a caught conflict. Red test: `upsert({ where: { source, quarter }, create: { …, count: 1 }, update: { count: { increment: 1 } } })` throws today; it should leave `count` at 2 after two calls, with no lost bump under concurrency.
  - **A** — Operators allowed in the update half only (create still refuses: nothing to change). The fast path emits the operator in its SET, the slow path passes it to `update()`, and the fast path accepts a `where` naming every column of a declared `@@unique` pair as its `ON CONFLICT(a, b)` target. The counter becomes one statement. Rewrites `FJS-D27`'s upsert clause; the `@gte`-style refusal on the column stays.
  - **B** — As A without the composite target. The operator is accepted and both paths apply it, but a pair-keyed counter stays findFirst + create + update-on-conflict. Correct under concurrency through the existing retry, still three round trips, so `FJS-1498`'s title is not met.
  - **C** — Keep `FJS-D27` as ruled and make the refusal name the way out (`updateMany` with the operator, then `create`, retrying `updateMany` on a unique conflict). Smallest change; every aggregate table keeps writing the three-statement loop.
  - **Recommend A** — the refusal's reason (one path could not apply an operator) does not hold, and without the composite target the operator buys nothing for the pair-keyed aggregate that motivated the row.

- **FJS-D1113 — Does a field's `@default` read a column across a to-one relation (`@default(account.taxPercent)`) at create, and what does the parent's read rule owe it?** Found fixing `FJS-2711`. Today `@default` takes a literal, `now()`, `cuid()` or `auth().<field>` (`applyAuthDefaults`, `src/core/client.js`), so ELA's `Document.taxPercent` copying `Account.taxPercent` is a service hook, and the generated create form seeds `0`, which beats the hook's fill-only-null copy. The red test's assertion: `db.document.create({ accountId })` stores `taxPercent` equal to the account's; today it stores null/refused. `@from` is the nearest word and is a LIVE read, not a copy frozen at create.
  - **A** — `@default(<relation>.<column>)`, one hop, evaluated in the create path after the FK is resolved (read as the engine, like `@default(auth().id)`); the field is optional in `create`'s JSON Schema and the form leaves it blank-to-fill, so `make()` stops seeding 0. A later change to the parent never touches the row. The parent column must be readable by the caller (`FJS-D576`: a foreign key is a read) or the copy is refused with the same `ForeignKeyError`.
  - **B** — as A, but the copy reads as `asSystem()` regardless of the parent's gate (a blind copy, like a `@system` stamp), so a caller who may attach but not read the parent still gets the value. Smaller surprise for the form, but a gate-protected parent column leaks into a row the caller can read.
  - **C** — keep the service hook and fix only the browser cost: `make()` seeds nothing for a field a hook fills (`@transient`-style "server fills" marker), so the create form omits it. No new default form; the copy stays app code and `FJS-2711` closes as accepted.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the one reading that puts the copy in the schema (Invariant 6's direction), reuses the engine-stamped-default seam the `auth()` form already owns, and keeps `FJS-D576`'s rule that a foreign key is a read instead of opening a leak path; B trades that rule for convenience and C leaves every app writing the same hook.

- **FJS-D1115 — Does a `findFirst` whose `where` pins one row by a unique key read as the single-row read (`@omit` present), or does `findFirst` stay list-mode?** Found fixing `FJS-2253`. `findFirst({ where: { id: 1 } })` reads with `mode: 'list'` (`client.js` findFirst, `read(_raw, { mode: 'list' })`), so `@omit` leaves `bio` out, while `findUnique({ where: { id: 1 } })` returns it; `litestone.test.ts` "`@omit`: excluded from findFirst" pins today's behavior and `docs/schema.md:149` states it. The red assertion: `'bio' in await db.user.findFirst({ where: { id: 1 } })` is false today and true under A or C.
  - **A** — `findFirst` reads as `single` when its `where` is exactly equality on a unique key or the primary key (the shape `findUnique` accepts, already classified for the fast path); any other `findFirst` stays list. The two spellings of one read agree; `findFirst({ where: { role: 'admin' } })` still strips. The mode then depends on the `where`'s shape, which one more sentence in the catalog states.
  - **B** — keep `findFirst` list-mode as written and tested; sharpen the `@omit` catalog line to say `findFirst` is a list read capped at one, and that `findUnique` is the only single-row read. No code change; the surprise is documented, not removed.
  - **C** — every `findFirst` reads as `single` (it returns one row, so it is the single-row read by its own name). `@omit(lists)` would then mean only `findMany`/`findManyAndCount`; `@omit(all)` unchanged. Simplest rule, but a `findFirst({ orderBy })` over a wide table now carries the long column.
  - **Recommend A** — the surprise is specifically the by-key `findFirst` that reads as a single-row lookup; A removes it without making a scan-and-take-one carry the heavy column, and C trades one surprise for another.

- **FJS-D1119 — Does the schema get a frequency word ("at most one row per key per window", refused at write with a 409) and what is its shape — a literal window, or a window read from another row?** Found fixing FJS-2087. ela's survey cap is "one Survey per `clientId` in `SurveySetting.minDaysBetween` days", written as a query in the batch, so a manual send, a seed or a re-run is not held to it. `@@unique` cannot say a window and `@@index(where:)` cannot read `now()` or another row's column. The red test: create two rows for one key inside the window; today both succeed, where a unique key gives `UniqueConflictError`. It needs a new `@@` word, so no source was touched.
  - **A** — `@@rate([clientId], per: 30d, at: createdAt)`: a literal window, checked inside the write's transaction (SQLite's single writer makes the count-then-insert atomic), refused with a 409 that names the key and the moment the window reopens. It cannot say ela's per-client override, which is another row's column, so ela keeps the override in its batch.
  - **B** — A, with `per:` also taking a column path (`per: days(setting.minDaysBetween)`, null meaning no cap): the window is read from the row's relation at write time. It covers ela whole, at the price of a path resolver in `per:` (`FJS-D908` already pays that for zones).
  - **C** — no word: document a bucket column (`@@unique([clientId, periodStart])` stamped by a hook) as the fixed-window answer. A rolling window stays a hand-written query, and the row stays what it is.
  - **Recommend B** — the harm measured is a rule with two writers and one of them unguarded, and the per-client override is half of that case; A would close the first half and leave the rule in the batch anyway.
  - **Also `FJS-3027`: the key may be the CALLER, not a column.** posthog's stolen `ProjectKey` posted 2,000 events in 1.99 s and ghost proposes `@@rate(create, 5 per 1min)` per principal, so the same count-in-the-write's-transaction check keyed by `principal` instead of `[clientId]`, with a `retry-after` rather than a reopen moment. Whichever shape is ruled should name both keys, or `FJS-3027` waits on a second word.

- **FJS-D1123 — How does a deploy declare that an app answers hosts its config does not name?** Found fixing FJS-2268. `edgeRoutes()` (`packages/cli/core/edge.js`) throws `EdgeError` when no side names a domain, writes one `host`-matched route per `deploy.web.domain` / `deploy.api.domain`, and `applyEdge` sets `skip_unmapped_hosts`; so an agency's `reports.acme.com` CNAME reaches no app and Caddy fetches no certificate. A fix needs three things the edge lacks: a hostless fallback route (today refused: "a route matching every host would take over whatever else the machine serves"), a `tls.automation.policies[].on_demand` with `permission: { module: http, endpoint: <ask URL> }`, and a dev proxy that stops rewriting Host (`changeOrigin: true` in the `new.md` scaffold). The red test is `edgeRoutes({ …, tenantHosts })` returning a route with no `host` matcher and `applyEdge` output carrying the on-demand policy; none exists to write until the key's spelling is ruled.
