One owner of the count, no grammar, and the service says in one line what it was hand-counting; but the schema does not say the set must be covered, so a second service that forgets the option rotates a partial vault.
  - **C** — no engine spelling: `data-hazards` names `$transaction` plus a count as THE spelling for a complete-set write, and Junction may offer a `ctx` helper that does the count. Nothing coined; the rule lives in each service, which is the status quo the row calls a gap.
  - **Recommend A** — every sibling guard on a write (`@@transitions`, `@@commitment`, `@immutable`, `@@check`) is a schema attribute enforced at the Data boundary, and B's rule lives in the caller, so a second service that forgets the option rotates a partial vault with nothing reporting it (§ V 9); B is the reopen path if the ledger A needs proves too costly. Confidence 2: outside practice is C, and only Postgres has a schema-level commit-time check.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Vaultwarden's key rotation loads the user's ciphers, folders and sends, then refuses with "All existing ciphers must be included in the rotation" unless the request is a superset ([source](https://github.com/dani-garcia/vaultwarden/blob/main/src/api/core/accounts.rs)). That is a service-level check, as in C. Postgres offers a schema-level commit-time check through `CONSTRAINT TRIGGER ... DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED`, but the author writes the trigger body ([docs](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createtrigger.html)). No product declares a complete-set rule on a column.

- **FJS-D1285 — What declares that a recurring wall-clock rule fires exactly once per occurrence across a DST gap and overlap: a schema attribute that owns the once-ness CAS, a caravan schedule read from a row, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3060`: statuspage's `MaintenanceWindow` (weekly 01:30 in `Europe/Lisbon`) is a recurring rule with no row per occurrence, so `@@transitions`' compare-and-swap has nothing to swap and `@@commitment` fires on a column's instant, not a rule. Measured 2026-10-16 (`measure/dst-window.mjs`, `TZ=America/Denver`): both hand-written spellings open once on spring, ordinary and autumn Sundays — (a) a caravan cron `30 1 * * 0` with `timeZone`, (b) a per-minute sweep CASing a `lastOpenedOn @date @zoned @system` column — so once-ness is reachable today and only the declaration is missing. Red assertion owed: a schema stating the rule once opens one occurrence on each of the three Sundays with no hand-written `lastOpenedOn` column. `FJS-D143` refused the general RRULE and any subset must earn its name; `FJS-D312` made the calendar Caravan's.
  - **A** — a litestone attribute on a model, `@@recurs(on: <wall-clock fields>, zone: <path>, opens: <move>)`, whose firing litestone owns: it keeps the per-model last-fired occurrence (a framework-owned `@system @date @zoned` column added the way `@@hasTemplates` adds its own) and CASes it, then runs the named move as `asSystem()`. Closest to `@@commitment`; cost is a second clock mechanism in the language and the first recurrence grammar (weekly + time-of-day), which `FJS-D143` said must be a named subset, not RRULE.
  - **B** — no litestone grammar; Caravan reads the rule off a row: `handle({ cron, timeZone })` is given a per-row source (`schedules(model, { cron: 'cronField', timeZone: 'zone.path' })`) and its existing `cron:<job>:<epoch-minute>` key is the once-ness. The CAS stays Caravan's, the schema only marks which columns are the cron and the zone (`@cron` validator beside `@zone`). Smallest new surface, keeps `FJS-D312`'s one owner; cost is that the wall-clock gap (spring's 01:30 fires at 02:00) is Caravan's rule and the schema cannot state "skip" versus "slide".
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the hand-written `lastOpenedOn` CAS or the fixed-time cron as THE spelling, with the Lisbon measurement as its proof, and the Window trait gains the missing duration form (`opensFor`) so a 01:30–03:00 window is not 1h and 2.5h by accident. Nothing coined; every app keeps writing the same 20 lines.
  - **Recommend B** — `FJS-D312` already put the calendar in Caravan and the measurement shows its key gives once-ness on all three Sundays, so the missing piece is only the row-to-cron binding; A builds a second scheduler inside the Data realm for one grammar. Every scheduler studied keeps once-ness in the scheduler and the rule plus zone as data it reads. Confidence 3: if a second consumer needs the occurrence as a queryable row, A wins.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Quartz keeps cron triggers as database rows and its CronTrigger skips a time that does not exist on a DST day ([FAQ](https://www.quartz-scheduler.org/documentation/2.3.1-SNAPSHOT/faq.html)). Temporal Schedules carry a `timezoneName` in the schedule object and the scheduler owns firing ([ScheduleSpec](https://typescript.temporal.io/api/interfaces/client.ScheduleSpec)). Cron never matches a skipped hour and runs a repeated hour twice ([crontab(5)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/crontab.5.html)). None puts once-ness in the data layer, and gap behavior differs per scheduler, which matches B's cost.

- **FJS-D1286 — How does a schema say "this move is refused while a related row is in a state" — a guard on the move, not on the state the write lands in?** Found fixing `FJS-3059` (statuspage Q3 / C4): `@@deny('post-update', status == 'resolved' && component.lastCheckOk == false)` guards the move `resolve`, but grades the state the write lands in, so retitling an already-resolved incident while the component fails again is refused with the deny's message. `@@commitment`'s `while:` reads this row alone and fires a move rather than refusing one. `@@transitions` (`parseTransitionsArg`, `parser.js:3112`) takes only `declaredBy:`/`scope:` arguments and `name: from -> to` entries, so there is no place a per-move guard could be written today. Red assertion owed: with `Incident` `resolved` and its component failing, `db.incident.update({ title })` succeeds while `db.incident.transition(id, 'resolve')` from `open` is refused. Costs 2–4 of the row (same 403 code as a seniority refusal, a stale copy column `lastCheckOk` because the monitor is two hops away, 21 s flip) stay under A and B unless the guard reads the relation live.
  - **A** — a guard clause on a `@@transitions` entry, `resolve: open -> resolved while: component.lastCheckOk != false` (or `unless:`), graded only when the CAS moves the column from `from` to `to`, reading this row and ONE hop (the `FJS-D356` one-hop reach of policy paths, `FJS-499`). Refusal carries its own code (not `ACCESS_DENIED`), `transitions(row)` reports `refusedBy: 'guard'`. Cost: a second predicate position beside `@@commitment while:` with different reach rules, and a new refusal code through the Data→API error owner.
  - **B** — no new grammar: give `@@deny` / `@@allow` a move-aware term, `moving(resolve)` (true only when the write is that transition), so `@@deny('post-update', moving(resolve) && component.lastCheckOk == false)` says the rule once in the language the schema already has. Reuses the policy grader and its one-hop paths; cost: a function that reads the write's shape, which no policy term does today (`auth()`, `now()`, fields), and the refusal is still `ACCESS_DENIED`.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the post-update deny as THE spelling, with the retitle-while-failing refusal as its stated cost, and the app guards the retitle with a second `pre-update` allow. Nothing coined; the staleness and the state-grading stay the app's.
  - **Recommend A** — the move is where the guard belongs: a clause on the `@@transitions` entry, graded only when the CAS moves the column from `from` to `to`, reading this row and one hop (`FJS-D356`), with its own refusal code so it is not mistaken for `ACCESS_DENIED`; B is the reopen path if the grammar cost of a second predicate position proves too high. Confidence 3: both state-machine libraries below put the guard on the transition.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — AASM sets "a guard per transition" with `guard:`, `if:` or `unless:`, and a failing guard raises its own `AASM::InvalidTransition` ([README](https://github.com/aasm/aasm)). django-fsm takes a `conditions` list on each `@transition`, and a caller can ask `can_proceed` first ([README](https://github.com/viewflow/django-fsm)). Both put the guard on the move, not on the state the write lands in, and neither reuses a general row-policy term.

- **FJS-D1287 — How does a schema say a move posts a child row, so the column and its timeline are one write by construction, and what stops a direct post that disagrees with the column?** Found fixing `FJS-3058` (statuspage C3): `Incident.status` is stored and moved by `@@transitions`, `IncidentUpdate` carries a copy of the status after each move, and nothing ties them. Measured: `incident.transition(id, 'identify')` alone moves the column and posts nothing; `IncidentUpdate.create({ status: 'resolved' })` on an identified incident is accepted; `postedAt` is writable at create, so a backdated post reorders the projection. A `@from(IncidentUpdate, last: true)` cannot carry the machine (the CAS needs a stored column) and a `@@check` reaches one table. The red test is `transition(id, 'identify')` leaving `updates.length === 0`.
  - **A** — a clause on the move, `identify: investigating -> identified posts IncidentUpdate(incidentId: id, status: to)`, whose row the engine inserts in the move's own transaction after the CAS wins (so a lost CAS posts nothing), with the named child columns `@system`-filled and refused on a direct `create` by the existing `@system` rule. Closes (1) by construction, (2) because `status` is no longer caller-writable on the child, and (3) if `postedAt` is declared engine-stamped like `@stamps`. The post's `body` comes from the call: `transition(id, 'identify', { post: { body } })`, required when the clause has a non-defaulted column.
  - **B** — the child declares the tie, no clause on the move: `@@follows(incident.status, copy: status)` on `IncidentUpdate` grades a create against the parent's CURRENT column (one hop, the `FJS-D356` reach), and a parent move with no matching child row in the same `$transaction` is refused at commit. Closes (2) and (1) but needs a commit-time check the engine does not have, and a deferred check is a second enforcement point beside the CAS.
  - **C** — no grammar: `data-hazards` § transitions names the service's `$transaction` pair as THE spelling, adds `postedAt @system` and a `@@require` comparing the child's `status` with the parent's as the stated closure of (2) and (3), and says (1) is the service's to keep. Nothing coined; seam (1) stays open for any second caller of `transition`.
  - **Recommend A** — `@stamps`, `@system` and `@@transitions` already put every sibling guard on a move in the move's own `UPDATE`, and A is the only option where a caller using `transition()` directly cannot reach the half-written state; B adds a commit-time check beside the CAS. state_machines-audit_trail and django-fsm-log both declare the transition record on the machine, not on the child. Confidence 3: if a second consumer needs the row posted conditionally, A's clause wants a `when:` and C looks smaller.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — The `audit_trail` line inside a `state_machine` block registers an `after_transition` callback that writes every transition to a separate model and table ([README](https://github.com/state-machines/state_machines-audit_trail)). django-fsm-log listens to the `post_transition` signal and creates one record per transition ([README](https://github.com/jazzband/django-fsm-log)). Both declare the timeline on the machine, so no caller of the move can skip it. Neither lets the child be created directly with a disagreeing status, which is A's `@system` half.

- **FJS-D1288 — What word keeps a readable column's value out of the audit trail when the value is not a person: a new attribute, an argument on an existing one, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3057` (statuspage C1): `Monitor.url` carries a key in its query string, staff must READ it, and the trail must not keep it. `logMarks` in `makeTable` (`src/core/client.js`) is fed only by `isProtected` (`[redacted]`, and every protected field is hidden from a non-system read), `p.personal` (`[personal]`, `FJS-D657`, a value about a person) and `p.opaque` (a digest, `FJS-D1158`, bytes a client sealed), so no combination is readable AND unlogged. The red test's assertion: a `url String @<word>` field is returned by `findMany` for a staff reader, and the `create` entry's `after.url` reads the mark and never `https://x?key=1`.
  - **A** — a new field attribute that is only a trail mark, the shape `@personal` already is: `url String @redacted` logs `[redacted]` in field entries and in `before`/`after`, changes nothing about who reads it (`@omit` and the gate stay that question), and lands as one more flag in `buildFieldPolicyMap` (`src/core/schema-maps.js`), the owner `export.js`, `testing.js` and `query.js` already read. The data map reports it as kept from the trail. It collides with nothing: `@personal` + `@redacted` logs `[redacted]`, as protected + personal already does.
  - **B** — an argument on the trail's own field attribute: `url String @trail(audit, value: false)` (alongside `reads:`/`writes:`), so the trail's whole grammar stays in the one word and nothing is coined; the entry still names the field and the mark is `[redacted]`. Cost: `@trail` is today synthesized for protected fields and parsed only to name a database, so the argument is a second meaning for a word `@@trail(audit)` also carries at the model grain, and a model with `@@trail` and no field `@trail` has no place to write it.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § audit trail names the split as THE spelling (the key goes in `secret String @encrypted`, the URL stays clean, as the statuspage draft already does), and `FJS-1485`'s second instance is closed as an app-modeling rule. Nothing coined; a URL with an inline key still reaches the trail the first time an app forgets, and nothing reports it.
  - **Recommend A** — `@personal` is the sibling that decided this shape (a flag in the field-policy owner, a mark in the trail, no effect on who reads), and the word has to be a plain description of what the trail does because `@sensitive` is refused by name (`IDEAS/data-classification.md`, graded levels carry no behavior); C leaves the leak silent, which is the question-9 failure. django-auditlog's `mask_fields` is the same split. Confidence 3: if the owner would rather not add an attribute whose only effect is `[redacted]`, B is the same behavior inside an existing word.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — django-auditlog keeps a field tracked but hides its value with `mask_fields`, and `exclude_fields` drops it from the log, with neither changing who can read the model ([docs](https://django-auditlog.readthedocs.io/en/latest/usage.html)). Rails `filter_attributes` hides listed columns in `inspect` output while the attribute stays readable ([API docs](https://api.rubyonrails.org/classes/ActiveRecord/Core/ClassMethods.html)). Both are per-field marks independent of read access, which is A's shape. Auditlog declares them in the audit config rather than as a column word *(inference)*, which is nearer B.

- **FJS-D1289 — How does a model state that its offline writes are PATCHES only, when its rows are created by another protocol (an upload)?** Found fixing `FJS-3055` (immich C6): `Asset.isFavorite` is toggled on the phone offline and must replay, but an Asset row is only ever created by the upload protocol, and its `File` is required. `@@sync(<policy>)` (`parser.js` `case 'sync'`, `SYNC_POLICIES`) is one collision policy per model over every operation; `server`/`refuse`/`field` make the create syncable too, so `sync-required-file` (`advise.js`) warns and the offline create is refused, while `append` is the one operation-shaped member and is wrong for an update. Today the only way to hold the patch is to give up the file's required-ness or split the toggle onto a second model. The red test: a model with a required `File` and `@@sync(refuse)` grades `sync-required-file`, and no spelling makes it say "updates only" without the warning.
  - **A** — an operation argument on the existing word: `@@sync(refuse, on: update)` (`on:` takes `create`/`update`/`delete`, default all), so the policy still comes from the closed set and the direction rides beside it as `@@sync(read)` already did (`FJS-D488`). The offline queue refuses a create or delete not listed, `x-sync` crosses as `{ policy, on }`, and `sync-required-file` and `sync-server-key-file` skip a model whose `on:` omits `create`. One word, one place to look.
  - **B** — a second policy member that names the shape: `@@sync(patch)`, "held for update only, collision policy `server`", beside `read`. Smallest parse change, but it fixes the collision policy at `server` so an Asset cannot also be `refuse` or `field`, and every later "update-only with X" needs another member — the set grows by product.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` names the split as THE spelling — the offline-toggled columns move to a sibling model (`AssetFlag`, one-to-one, client-mintable id, `@@sync(field)`) and the upload-created `Asset` stays unsyncable. Nothing coined; the cost is a join on every read of a favorite and a model that exists only to dodge the grammar.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D488` already put direction on `@@sync` as an argument rather than a second word, and the collision policy and the operations are independent axes, which B collapses; C is the status quo the row calls a gap. Confidence 3: Postgres and PowerSync both treat the operation as its own axis. If no second app has a model created elsewhere and patched on a device, C costs less than a grammar.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Postgres scopes each row policy to one command with `FOR { ALL | SELECT | INSERT | UPDATE | DELETE }` ([docs](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createpolicy.html)). PowerSync's client queue records every write as `PUT`, `PATCH` or `DELETE`, and the backend connector switches on that op ([docs](https://docs.powersync.com/handling-writes/writing-client-changes.md)). Both keep the operation separate from the rule applied to it, which is `A`'s `on:` argument and not a new policy member.

- **FJS-D1290 — How does a wall clock written with a fixed offset (EXIF, ISO `+05:00`) state how it was read: can `@zoned` name an `Int @unit(min)` offset column, a new word, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3054` (immich C5): `Asset.takenOn`/`takenTime` are `@zoned(takenZone)`, but the camera wrote an OFFSET, which `@zone` refuses by design (`FJS-D143`: a day read in a fixed offset is not the same day once a rule changes), so the offset sits in `takenOffsetMin Int? @unit(min)` and no `@zoned` read resolves it. Red assertion owed: `due()` over a day-kind `@@commitment` whose `@date` is `@zoned(takenOffsetMin)` reads the day at that row's offset, where today the parser refuses the path (`@zoned(…) must be a String @zone`).
  - **A** — the same word, a second target type: `@zoned(path)` accepts an `Int @unit(min)` column as well as a `@zone` String, validated at parse (type, and a range of -720..840 minutes at write), read per row as a fixed offset by the one resolver `due()` already uses. No new noun; `@zone` keeps refusing an offset, so the IANA rule stands where a FUTURE moment is computed, and an offset is only ever the record of a moment already observed. Cost: `@zoned` then names two kinds of target and `db:explain @zoned` must say which reads which.
  - **B** — a second attribute for the second thing: `@offset(takenOffsetMin)` on the wall clock, beside `@zoned`, so the zone word keeps meaning IANA only and a reader sees which is which. Cost: two words that answer the same question (*in what frame is this wall clock read*), the restating the decision rules refuse.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` § transitions names the split as THE spelling — the app stores the instant (`takenAt`, already there) and the offset as plain data, and a day-kind commitment on an offset wall clock is not offered. Nothing coined; the cost is that EXIF/ISO wall clocks stay a column litestone describes and no read resolves, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D512` already made `@zoned` the single declaration a reader resolves a wall clock by, and an offset is the same fact in a narrower form; B is a second word for it and C is the status quo the row calls a gap. Java and Temporal both accept a fixed offset wherever a zone is accepted. Confidence 3: if no second app records a wall clock by offset and nothing reads it but `due()`, C costs nothing and A is a parser branch with one reader.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Temporal accepts an offset time zone identifier such as `+05:30` wherever it accepts an IANA name, and warns to prefer the named zone for future moments ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Global_Objects/Temporal/ZonedDateTime)). Java's `ZoneOffset` is a `ZoneId`, so `ZoneId.of("+05:00")` works ([ZoneId](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/api/java.base/java/time/ZoneId.html)). Postgres `AT TIME ZONE` takes names and offsets in one slot ([docs](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/datatype-datetime.html)). Both libraries use one word with two target kinds, which is A.

- **FJS-D1291 — How does a schema say a column may only grow (a resume offset a replayed chunk never moves backwards): a new attribute, a before-image term in a policy, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3052` (immich C3): `Upload.received` has `@@check("received <= size")` for the ceiling, but a `post-update` rule grades the row the write would leave and never the row it replaces, so `update({ received: 10 })` over a row at 40 is accepted. Red test: create an Upload at `received: 40`, `update({ received: 10 })` — passes today, must refuse.
  - **A** — a column attribute, `received Int @monotonic` (`Int`/`BigInt`/`DateTime`, optionally `@monotonic(down)`), enforced the way `@@transitions` and the `version` column already are: the update's `WHERE` is narrowed by `AND "received" <= :new`, so a lower value matches no row and the write is refused as a named error (`MonotonicError`, 409) in the same statement, race-free under concurrent chunks. A replayed chunk is refused, not silently dropped; a `$increment`/`$max`-style write is naturally legal. `@immutable` is the sibling that froze a column outright, so this is its "may move one way" form. Cost: one more attribute, and the choice of REFUSE over CLAMP (keep the larger) is made for every user.
  - **B** — no new column word: give `@@deny` / `@@allow` a before-image term, `old.received`, so `@@deny('update', received < old.received)` says the rule in the policy language the schema already has, and also covers rules `@monotonic` cannot (*status may only go up by one*, *total never below paid*). Cost: the policy grader today evaluates a `post-update` rule in JS over the merged row (`FJS-D356` one-hop reach); a before-image term must also compile into the UPDATE's `WHERE` to stay race-free, which is the larger change, and it is a second predicate grammar if `@@transitions` stays.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` names a service `before` hook that reads the row and refuses as THE spelling, with the read-then-write race named as its cost (two concurrent chunks can both pass), and the row is closed as an app-modeling rule. Nothing coined; the one place the invariant is not at the Data boundary (Invariant 6).
  - **Recommend A** — the case is a column whose whole contract is a direction, `@immutable` and `version` already enforce their half of the same family in the UPDATE's own `WHERE`, and A is the only option that is both declared in the schema (Invariant 6) and race-free without a new predicate grammar; B is the stronger reopen if a second app wants a before-image rule that is not a direction. Confidence 2: outside precedent is a general before-image condition, not a column word.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — DynamoDB's `UpdateItem` takes a condition expression such as `Price > :limit`, checked against the current item, and the update fails when it is false ([docs](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Expressions.ConditionExpressions.html)). Firestore rules compare `request.resource.data` with `resource.data` in one update rule ([docs](https://firebase.google.com/docs/firestore/security/rules-conditions)). Both use a general before-image condition, which is B's shape. I found no product with a monotonic column attribute *(inference)*.

- **FJS-D1293 — How does a schema say two rows reference one stored object (one copy of the bytes, freed when the last reference goes): a File attribute that keys the object by digest and guards cleanup, a Blob model the app refcounts, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3050` (immich Q2/C1): a File column is the row's own — `serialize` in `src/storage/file-storage.js` mints a fresh key per upload and the cleanup at `file-storage.js:374` deletes the object when its row's ref goes — so "exactly one stored copy, two ownership rows" is one copy per row, and the draft settles for `@@unique([ownerId, sha256])`. The red test's assertion: two creates of the same bytes on two rows store two objects today, and deleting one row deletes the bytes the other still names.
  - **A** — a File attribute that makes the key a function of the bytes and guards cleanup: `file File @shared` (keyed by the `FJS-D1292` digest; two rows holding the same bytes hold the SAME ref and neither owns the object alone). Cleanup deletes the object only when no other File ref in the schema names its key — one scan of the schema's File columns, at the owner of the cleanup. The column stays the row's own, so FK-read (`FJS-D576`) never enters: there is no Blob row to be unreadable. Cost: a `.lite` word, a catalog row, a cross-column reference count in a tx, and it leans on D1292 A (a digest to key by).
  - **B** — no litestone change: the app models `Blob { sha256 @id, file File @keepVersions }` with ownership rows holding an FK to it, and a hook refcounts. Nothing coined, but a Blob keyed on the hash has no owner a row policy can name, so an ownership row's caller cannot read the Blob through `@@gate("8")` (`FJS-D576` answers it as missing) and the app reaches for `asSystem()` — the exact thing Invariant 6 says the schema should have declared.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` states one copy per row as the contract and the row closes as an app-modeling rule (`@@unique([ownerId, sha256])` is the answer). Nothing coined; storage cost grows with every owner of the same bytes, which is the row's complaint.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is "these two refs are one object", which is a statement about the schema and not about a hook; B forces `asSystem()` around a read the schema could grade, and C is the status quo. Confidence 2: A is only worth a word if a second app has cross-owner dedup, and it should land with D1292 A or not at all. Precedent splits on keying by digest, but both products delete the bytes only when no reference remains.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Rails Active Storage keeps a `Blob` row with attachment rows and destroys the blob and file only when no attachments remain, with random keys and no dedup ([guide](https://edgeguides.rubyonrails.org/active_storage_overview.html)). The Docker registry stores layers by SHA-256 digest, shares them between manifests and deletes one only when no manifest refers to it, by a stop-the-world mark and sweep ([docs](https://distribution.github.io/distribution/about/garbage-collection/)). Keying by digest has precedent, and so does a Blob model. Both guard deletion on references.

- **FJS-D1294 — How does a schema say a column's every former value stays a readable row (every slug a post ever had, read by the router): a column attribute naming the target model, a model attribute, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3036` (ghost C4): `Post.slug` is renamed by its author and every value it ever held must still answer as a redirect, so Ghost carries a `Slug` model a `posts.service.ts` hook fills (after create/update/patch, in the write's transaction, the `Slug` create lifted with `system: ['@@gate']`). Measured there: rename x3 then back gives exactly four `Slug` rows; but `asSystem()` renaming a post records nothing, so the address it renamed TO 404s after the next rename. The words that exist each miss: `@@trail(audit)` is another file at gate 8 (unreadable by a router, `FJS-2738`), `@@effective` is a window nothing writes on change, `@keepVersions` is File-only. A write-side word is the only form that closes the seed and the `asSystem()` paths, since the hook lives in a service and a seed never enters one.
  - **A** — a column attribute naming the model that receives the values: `slug String @unique @history(into: Slug)`, where `Slug` is an ordinary model the schema declares (`value String @id`, `postId`, `createdAt`), with its own gate and `@@allow`, so a router reads it at the level the schema gives it. On create, and on any update that changes the column, the same transaction appends `{value: <old or new>, <fk to the owner>}` through the client's own write path under the system grade, so no hook, no `asSystem()` and no caller can skip it. The target's `@id`/`@unique` on the value gives "a second post renamed to a former slug is refused 409" for free. Cost: one attribute, one write-path branch beside `@updatedAt`/`@version`, and a parse rule that the target has exactly one relation back.
  - **B** — a model attribute, `@@history(slug, into: Slug)`, the shape of `@@trail(db)` and `@@effective(from:, to:)`: the same mechanism as A, but the Post model carries it and may name several columns (`@@history([slug, handle], into: Slug)`). Cost: a second way to say a thing that is a fact about one column (the restating `decision-rules` refuse), and a multi-column form has no second app asking for it.
  - **C** — no spelling: `data-hazards` states the hook (`after create/update/patch` in the write's transaction, `system: ['@@gate']`) as THE spelling and the row closes as an app-modeling rule, with the `asSystem()` rename named as its cost. Nothing coined; the guarantee stays one a seed or a tinker script can skip, which is the row's complaint, and it is the one place Invariant 6 (access and history declared in the schema, not hooks) is left to a service.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is about one column and its target ("every value this column held is a row of that model"), which is what `@updatedAt` and `@immutable` already state as column attributes, and it is the only form that is at the Data boundary (Invariant 6) so the seed and `asSystem()` paths record too; B restates it at the model, C is the status quo. The one product with this feature ships it as a framework mechanism, not an app hook. Confidence 3: if no second app keeps a column's past values as rows a reader joins, C costs nothing and A is a parser branch with one reader.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Rails `friendly_id` ships a `history` module that logs every slug in a `friendly_id_slugs` table, writes it in an `after_save` callback and falls back to that table in `find` so old slugs still resolve ([history.rb](https://github.com/norman/friendly_id/blob/master/lib/friendly_id/history.rb)). The module names one column and a reverting slug deletes its stale history row. It is a Rails callback, so a raw `update_column` skips it *(inference)*. The mechanism is framework-provided and not an app hook, which rules out C.

- **FJS-D1295 — Under `tenancy { strategy database }`, how does a schema say one sqlite database is marketplace-wide (a buyer, a cart, one payment that belong to no shop): a database-block option, a model attribute, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3032` (etsy C2): `TenantClient` (`packages/litestone/src/tenant.js`, the `sqliteOverrides` loop) points EVERY sqlite database at the tenant's own file and only `jsonl`/`trail` stay schema-global, so a global table runs no policy engine and has nowhere to live but a second app. Red test: a schema with `database main {}` plus `database market { path "./market.db" }` under `strategy database`, two tenants, one `Buyer` row written through tenant a and read back through tenant b — fails today, because each tenant sees its own `market` tables.
  - **A** — a database-block word, `database market { path "./market.db"  scope global }`: the registry leaves it out of `sqliteOverrides`, opens it once for the process (not once per tenant, not in the LRU pool), and the tenant client reaches it as the same `db.<model>` accessor with the full policy engine. The rule jsonl/trail already follow by driver becomes a stated word that sqlite can take too. Cost: one option, and a `$transaction` spanning a global and a tenant database is two files (`FJS-D232`), which must be refused or stated.
  - **B** — a model attribute, `@@tenant(none)` read under `strategy database` too: the model's rows go to a shared file the registry opens beside the tenants. Reuses a word `strategy row` already has, but routes by model what `FJS-D232` says is routed by database (a file), so one schema would say it two ways.
  - **C** — no spelling: the closed-world answer is a second app that peers to the first (`FJS-D1188`), and `data-hazards` states that `strategy database` holds only tenant data. Nothing coined; the cost is the row's complaint, a buyer whose cart spans shops lives in a service a gate cannot see.
  - **Recommend A** — a database is a file (`FJS-D232`), so which file is shared is a fact about the database block, and jsonl/trail already make it implicitly by driver; saying it as a word is the narrowest change and keeps B's cross-model routing out. Confidence 2: precedent splits by grain, and A's grain fits where the tenant boundary is a file; if the transaction refusal proves to need its own design, C holds until a second app asks.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Rails declares the database on an abstract base class with `connects_to` and models inherit it, and joins do not cross clusters ([guide](https://guides.rubyonrails.org/active_record_multiple_databases.html)). django-tenants lists shared apps in `SHARED_APPS`, per app and not per model ([docs](https://django-tenants.readthedocs.io/en/latest/install.html)). Apartment 4 marks a global model with `pin_tenant` ([README](https://github.com/rails-on-services/apartment)). Single-database tools must flag the model or app because tenants share one database. Where a tenant is a file, the database is the grain *(inference)*.

- **FJS-D1296 — Under `strategy row`, how does a model say a row is the tenant's AND a non-member's (an Order the shop and the buyer both read): a `@@tenant` option, an exemption on the generated deny, or no spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-3031` (etsy Q1): the desugar emits `@@deny(read, auth().shopId == null || shopId != auth().shopId)`, a buyer holds no `shopId`, and a `@@deny` beats every `@@allow`, so no `@@allow(read, buyerId == auth().id)` can reach the row. Today the only spelling is `@@tenant(none)` plus `shopId == auth().shopId` written by hand, which forfeits the `@default(auth().shopId)` stamp, the create-time wrong-tenant refusal and the `@@tenant` conformance grading on exactly the models a marketplace is made of. The red test's assertion: under `strategy row`, a model carrying `shopId` with `@@allow(read, buyerId == auth().id)`, read by a principal with no `shopId`, returns the row; today it returns `[]`.
  - **A** — a third `@@tenant` mode, `@@tenant(shared: buyerId == auth().id)`: the stamp and the create refusal stay, and the read/update/delete deny becomes `<tenant test> && !(<expr>)`, i.e. the shop's rows PLUS the rows the expression admits. One expression per model, written where the row is, and the deny is still generated (Invariant 6, enforced at the Data boundary). Coins one word, `shared`, on an attribute that already has `none`/`column`/`via`.
  - **B** — the tenant column's own exemption: `shopId String @default(auth().shopId) @tenantOrElse(buyerId == auth().id)`. Same generated deny, but the exception rides the column, so it cannot be written on a model that has no tenant column. A new field attribute for what is a model-level fact; no shorter than A.
  - **C** — no spelling: the closed-world answer is `@@tenant(none)` and a hand-written policy (what etsy does today), or a second app holding the buyer side (`FJS-D1188`). Nothing coined; the cost is the row's complaint, the stamp and the deny forfeited on Order, OrderLine and Refund. Could be made less silent by having `fli check` report a `@@tenant(none)` model that carries the tenant column and a policy reading it.
  - **Recommend A** — the exception is a statement about WHICH rows, so it belongs on the model beside `none`, and keeping it a deny with an excepting clause preserves the rule that tenancy narrows and never widens an allow (a `@@allow` could not be the spelling, `FJS-D05`). Postgres treats tenant isolation as one permissive predicate OR'd with others, which is the same row set. Confidence 3: if the `Admitted` twin (`FJS-D1100`) is meant to be the one door a non-member walks through, a `@@tenant(admit: …)` reusing that claim is the alternative to weigh first.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Postgres row-level security ORs all `PERMISSIVE` policies and ANDs `RESTRICTIVE` ones, so a tenant policy plus a buyer policy admit the union of both row sets ([CREATE POLICY](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createpolicy.html)). `WITH CHECK` on the tenant policy still refuses wrong-tenant writes. Rails `acts_as_tenant` scopes only when a tenant is set and offers `without_tenant` and `unscoped` for shared routes, which is a whole-query escape and not a per-model row rule ([README](https://github.com/ErwinM/acts_as_tenant)). The union of two row sets is A's `<tenant test> && !(<expr>)` deny.

- **FJS-D1297 — What spells retention that FOLDS (sum raw rows older than a window into a rollup row, then drop them, the chart not moving): a model attribute, a hook on the sweep, or the app's own two jobs with the language grading only the XOR?** Found fixing `FJS-3030` (posthog Q7, statuspage Q5). `runSqliteRetention` (`retention.js:120`) only DELETEs by `createdAt`; `@@expires` hides and removes nothing; so the app writes fold-then-thin as two jobs (measured: foldDay 4-16 ms a day, deleteMany of 626,400 rows 4-5 s) and "a day's rows exist in DailyUptime XOR in Check" is a cross-table invariant no `@@check` reaches. The hole is the LATE row: a check row for a published day lands in Check, the sealed rollup refuses every write (`asSystem` included, `SealedDocumentError` 409), so 1437/1439 stays published while the rows say 1437/1440. Red test: a model with a window and a sealed rollup — a late row for a folded day is accepted by the raw model today and no spelling can refuse it or route it to the rollup.
  - **A** — a model attribute `@@retention(1y, into: DailyUptime, by: day(checkedAt), sum: [ok, total])`, the `FJS-D1059` window grain plus a fold target. The sweep sums the doomed rows into the rollup in the same transaction as the DELETE, and a raw INSERT whose clock falls in a folded span is refused by name (the XOR held at the Data boundary). Costs a grammar extension (target, bucket, aggregate list), a catalog row, and a rule for sealed (`@immutable(after: publish)`) rollups: the late row is refused, not folded into a sealed one.
  - **B** — a hook on the sweep: `$retain({ fold: async (rows, tx) => … })` or a schema `@@retention(1y, fold: 'uptime')` naming an app function. The app writes the sum, litestone owns the transaction and the cutoff (sum and delete commit together), and no aggregate grammar is added. The XOR stays the app's: a late row is still accepted.
  - **C** — no schema word; the app keeps its two jobs, and litestone only adds a refusal spelling for the late row (a `@@check`-style guard on Check naming the rollup model: `@@foldedBy(DailyUptime, day(checkedAt))`). Smallest; fold and thin can still diverge between the two jobs.
  - **Recommend A** — the fold target and aggregate list are schema facts, and `FJS-D1059` already set the schema-word-at-model-grain precedent. ClickHouse and Timescale both declare fold-then-drop in the schema and accept any aggregate function, so the grammar is a named aggregate list (`sum`, `max`, later others) and not a closed `sum`-only set. B stays the escape for a fold no aggregate states. The late-row refusal and the sealed-rollup rule have no outside precedent. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — ClickHouse `MergeTree` declares `TTL d + INTERVAL 1 MONTH GROUP BY k1, k2 SET x = max(x), y = min(y)` on the table, so expired rows fold into one row and the raw rows go ([docs](https://clickhouse.com/docs/en/engines/table-engines/mergetree-family/mergetree#table_engine-mergetree-ttl)). Timescale keeps a continuous aggregate while a retention policy drops the raw chunks ([docs](https://www.tigerdata.com/docs/use-timescale/latest/continuous-aggregates/drop-data)). Neither says what a late row does. Both put the fold in the schema, not in an app hook.

- **FJS-D1298 — Is a merge (two rows become one, children re-pointed, the trail naming it once with both ids) a language/client act, or the app's own service method with a row of its own?** Found fixing FJS-3029. posthog's `identify()` moves every `Event.personId` from A to B and ends A; the trail records N `Event.personId` updates and one `Person` delete (FK strategy) or one `PersonDistinctId` update (alias strategy) sharing a `correlationId`, and no row says "A became B". The red assertion: after a merge, one trail entry exists whose action names the merge and carries both ids. Today none can — the trail's actions are create/update/delete/read. chatwoot already writes the same act as a `merge` service method (`contacts/merge.ts`) under `asSystem()`.
  - **A** — a client verb `db.$merge(accessor, { survivor, loser })`: in one transaction it re-points every FK that targets the model (derived from `buildRelations()`), deletes the loser, and writes ONE trail entry (`action: 'merge'`, `before` = loser image, `after` = survivor id). Gated as a write on both rows; `@@immutable` columns the re-point touches are refused unless named `@system`.
  - **B** — a model attribute `@@mergeable` that declares which relations re-point and which side wins, so the verb is only legal on a model that said so and `fli check` can grade the declaration; the trail entry is as in A.
  - **C** — no language surface: the app keeps the service method under `asSystem()` in a transaction and writes a `PersonMerge { survivorId, loserId, at }` row of its own, which IS the "A became B" record and is queryable by `survivorId`. Litestone adds only the documented convention (the shared `correlationId` ties the N updates to that row) in `docs/trail.md`.
  - **Recommend C** — two apps (posthog, chatwoot) spell it as a service method today and differ in what a merge moves (FK rewrite vs alias row vs revoking a grant), so a closed verb (A/B) would be rebuilt as hooks the first time a third shape arrives; C moves no callers and adds no noun, and A is the reopen path once a third app wants the same re-point set. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Salesforce ships merge as a platform statement: `merge` takes a main record and up to two others, reparents their related records, deletes the rest, and works for only leads, contacts, cases and accounts ([docs](https://developer.salesforce.com/docs/atlas.en-us.apexcode.meta/api/langCon_apex_dml_examples_merge.htm)). It is a closed verb over a fixed set of objects. General ORMs I looked at (Django, Prisma, Rails) ship none *(inference, not searched for merge specifically)*. A built-in verb exists where the product fixes its own object kinds.

- **FJS-D1301 — Does a rollup over a tree (a category's weighted mean of its children, recursive, with drop-lowest) get a declared spelling, or does the app keep the rollup job and litestone only says when the stored aggregate is stale?** Found fixing `FJS-3023` (moodle C5). `@derived` is a row-local SQL expression (`schema.md` § `@derived`) and `@from(rel, avg)` is one level with no weight, drop or recursion; a `view` can write the recursive CTE but is not graded per caller; so the draft stores the category grade as a `Grade` row on the category's own `GradeItem`, written by a rollup job, and no word in the seed says when that row is stale. Red assertion: after a child `Grade.score` changes, the parent category's stored aggregate is reachable as stale (or recomputed) from the schema alone; today nothing in `db/schema.lite` or the catalog names the dependency.
  - **A** — a model-grain aggregate over a tree: `@@rollup(into: Grade.score, children: GradeItem.parent, weight: weight, fn: mean, drop: lowest(n))`, recomputed in the same transaction as the child write and graded as the parent's row. Costs a grammar (relation, weight column, closed list of fns, drop spelling), a recursion bound, and the same closed-grammar risk `FJS-D1297` A names: the next product folds a percentile or a curve.
  - **B** — a hook seam like `FJS-D1297` B: `@@rollup(children: GradeItem.parent, by: 'gradeCategory')` names an app function; litestone owns the walk up the parent chain and the transaction (child write and recomputed parent commit together, parents visited leaf-to-root), the app owns the arithmetic. No aggregate grammar.
  - **C** — declare only the dependency: `@stale(on: Grade.score, marks: GradeItem.parentId)` or a catalog row (`rollupSources`) so `fli check` and the client can say "this stored aggregate depends on those rows" and a read can ask `stale: true`; the job stays the app's. Smallest; recompute is still a promise.
  - **D** — no language surface: the view (recursive CTE) is the declared value and the app reads it under `asSystem()` plus its own row policy filter; the stored `Grade` row is dropped. Nothing coined, but the read is ungraded per caller (the row's own complaint) and drop-lowest per level is hand-written SQL.
  - **Recommend B** — the arithmetic differs per product (weighted mean, drop-lowest, extra credit, curve), which is the argument `FJS-D1297` made for B, while the part only the framework can give is the transactional walk up the parent chain in leaf-to-root order, which a job cannot make atomic with the child write. Confidence 2 (precedent splits, one product found): if a second tree-rollup (org-chart headcount, bill of materials) turns out to be sum/mean only, A's closed grammar is the reopen path; C is the first step if B is judged too much for one stressor.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Moodle's gradebook is the source stressor and does both halves. A category gets its own `grade_item` that stores the aggregate, `force_regrading()` sets a `needsupdate` flag, and a regrade engine recomputes parents in order ([docs](https://docs.moodle.org/dev/Grades)). Its aggregation is a fixed set of strategies chosen by constant, which suits one product. I found no general ORM or database with a tree-rollup word *(inference)*. The stale flag is C, the built-in strategies are A.

- **FJS-D1302 — Who hands an unpacked stranger's package an origin of its own: a second listener junction owns, a Host-dispatched wildcard on the one listener, or Outpost's inline-app listener with the unpack job only recording the URL?** Found fixing `FJS-3022` (moodle C4). `serveStatic` (`packages/junction/src/transport/static.ts`) takes `untrusted: true` and answers `attachment` for anything outside the inline allow-list (`FJS-D314`), which a SCORM player's HTML and JS cannot be; the app's single `Bun.serve` listener is ONE origin, so serving the pages inline from it puts a stranger's script beside the session cookie. `FJS-D345`'s second listener is Outpost's (`packages/outpost/src/static.js`, 8181) and serves an App's `inline` source, not an app's uploaded rows. Nothing in junction can say "this directory is a different origin", so Moodle carries `Resource.packageOrigin @url @system` written by a job that has nowhere to point it. The red test's assertion: an unpacked package requested through the app's listener answers with a different `Origin` than the app's own, and a request carrying the session cookie to the package origin does not receive it.
  - **A** — junction owns a second listener: a static mount gains `origin: { port }` (or a `app.origin(name, { root, port })` owner) that starts a `Bun.serve` of its own over `root` with `untrusted` inverted — scripts and HTML are allowed because the origin is foreign — plus `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts` and no cookie ever set. The unpack job writes under `root/<resourceId>/` and `packageOrigin` is `http://host:port/<resourceId>/`. A port is an origin (`FJS-D345`'s argument, taken into junction); costs one more port in `docs/PORTS.md` and a third thing `runStartPhases` starts.
  - **B** — one listener, many origins: the mount dispatches on `Host` (`<id>.pkg.example.com`) to a root with the same sandbox headers; the session cookie is host-only so a package host never receives it. Needs wildcard DNS and TLS and does not exist on `localhost` dev without a hosts entry; no extra port, but the isolation rests on the app never setting a `Domain=` cookie.
  - **C** — the unpack job hands the unpacked files to Outpost as an `inline` App (its listener already is a separate origin, `FJS-D345`) and records the origin Outpost answers; junction and litestone add nothing. Works only on a fleet-deployed app, so `bun run dev` has no package origin, and the row's lifecycle (delete a Resource, delete the App) becomes two systems' business.
  - **Recommend B** — cookies do not isolate by port, so A's listener on the same host still receives the app's session cookie and fails the red test's second clause; a host of its own for the package is the shape precedent uses for untrusted content, and a host-only session cookie never reaches it. C splits one row's lifetime across two owners. Confidence 2: `<id>.localhost` serving in dev is *(inference)* and unprobed, and A on a distinct hostname is the fallback.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — RFC 6265 § 8.5: "Cookies do not provide isolation by port" ([RFC 6265](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6265#section-8.5)). GitHub serves user content from `*.githubusercontent.com`, a separate domain, so untrusted content cannot reach github.com cookies ([GitHub CSP journey](https://github.blog/engineering/platform-security/githubs-csp-journey/)). web.dev's guide recommends a sandbox domain, on the Public Suffix List for isolation between users ([web.dev](https://github.com/GoogleChrome/web.dev/blob/main/src/site/content/en/secure/securely-hosting-user-data/index.md)). Isolation is by host, not port, which favors B over A as spelled.

- **FJS-D1303 — What spells a column holding one value per locale: a field word that expands to a column per locale, a built-in `Localized` Json type the read paths understand, or no word and a documented trait?** Found fixing `FJS-3021` (moodle C1–C2). `FJS-D12` reserved the interface tier only; in the data tier a course title is `title` + `titlePt` spliced by a trait, the fallback to the default locale is a read rule nothing declares, `Question.choices` carries `text`/`textPt` by hand, and a third locale is 16 statements. Measured in `fjs-prototypes/moodle/measure/locale.mjs`: `@@label` and `orderBy` refuse a Json column as opaque, `@@fts` over Json indexes the KEYS (`FJS-3075`), and the pt sort is BINARY in every spelling (`FJS-2500`, a separate row — no spelling here fixes it).
  - **A** — a schema-level `locales(en, pt)` (default first) plus a field word `title String @localized`, desugared like `@@tenant` to one physical column per locale (`title`, `titlePt`). Required is stated for the default only; `@@label`, `@@fts`, `orderBy` and `where` on `title` resolve to `COALESCE(<reader locale>, <default>)`. Plain columns, so sort, index and FTS need no new engine support; the cost is that a third locale is still an ALTER per field plus an FTS rebuild (now derived by the differ, not hand-written), and a localized value INSIDE a Json document still has no spelling.
  - **B** — a built-in type `Localized` over Json (`{ "en": …, "pt": … }`), usable as a column AND as a member of another `@type`. Teach the three read paths to see through it: `@@label`/`orderBy` read `json_extract(col, '$.<locale>')` with the default-locale COALESCE, `@@fts` indexes the VALUES not the keys (closing `FJS-3075`), and the default locale's presence is a `CHECK (json_extract(col, '$.en') IS NOT NULL)` (no subquery, so SQLite allows it). A third locale is a `locales` edit and zero migration; one spelling covers the column and the nested case. Cost: an expression per read (an expression index, not a plain one, for sort), and the three read paths each gain a Json-aware branch.
  - **C** — no word: ship the trait (`Titled`) and a `fli check` rule grading that every `<name>Pt` column has its default-locale sibling, and document the fallback as an app read. Nothing coined; the row's complaint — fallback undeclared, nested value unspellable, third locale a migration — stands.
  - **Recommend B** — it is the only one that answers all four complaints (column, fallback, nested document, third locale) with one noun, and the `FJS-3075` fix comes with it; A leaves the nested case and the migration cost, C leaves everything. Confidence 3: if sort speed on a localized label matters more than the nested case, A is the smaller step and B can follow on top of it.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Payload CMS marks a field `localized: true` and stores it as an object keyed by locale (`{ en, es, de }`), also inside array and block fields, with a configurable fallback locale ([docs](https://payloadcms.com/docs/configuration/localization)). Rails Mobility offers a column-per-locale backend, a `jsonb` backend and shared tables, with fallbacks as a per-locale plugin ([README](https://github.com/shioyama/mobility)). The schema-first system stores one value per locale in a Json object. Column-per-locale exists but is one choice among several.

- **FJS-D1305 — When a claimed mirror's create lands a row the caller's read policy cannot yet see, what does it answer?** Found fixing `FJS-3148`. `forwardCreate` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts:1283`) lands the Provider's row as the system, then `landMirrored` re-reads it through the caller's client (`getTable(ctx).findUnique`, 1257); for a `Channel` whose read policy is `type == 'open' || members.some(...)` the creator's `ChannelMember` has not landed, so the re-read is `null`, the bridge answers 204 and junction warns *announced something that is not a Channel row*. Red test's assertion: `POST /channels` as alice with `type: 'private'` answers 201/200 with a body naming the new key, today 204 with none.
  - **A** — when the re-read is `null` the create answers 202 with `{ [key]: value }` only: the write happened, the row is not yet readable, and the client re-reads once the sync lands. Nothing is returned that the policy withheld (Invariant 6), and no Provider contract changes; the announce is suppressed for a row the caller cannot read.
  - **B** — the claim's `forward.create` may also return the side rows the Provider made with the row (`{ row, with: { channelMember: [...] } }`), and `landMirrored` lands them as the system before the re-read, so the policy turns true. The answer stays a row, but every Provider whose read policy runs through a relation must return its side rows, and a Provider that cannot has no answer.
  - **C** — keep the 204 and document it as the hazard: a create under a relation-derived read policy answers no row until the sync lands. Cheapest, but the warning stays and `resource.save` gets no row back.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only one that needs nothing from every Provider and keeps the read policy at the Data boundary; B is the better UX where a Provider can supply side rows and can follow on top of A. Confidence 3. PostgREST keeps 201 for a key-only answer, so the status code is the one open detail.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Postgres refuses to hand back a row the SELECT policy hides: `INSERT ... RETURNING` on such a row throws an error, never silently drops it ([docs](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createpolicy.html)). PostgREST's `Prefer: return=headers-only` answers 201 with a `Location` header holding the key and no body ([docs](https://docs.postgrest.org/en/stable/references/api/preferences.html)). Both keep the read policy on the answer. A key-only reply is the established shape.

- **FJS-D1308 — How does a signed inbound delivery past the 256 KB body bound, or a non-UTF-8 or multipart one, reach a verifier as the bytes the sender signed?** Found fixing `FJS-2927`. Measured through `createApp().post('/hook')`: a 300 KB JSON post is a 413 before any handler runs, and no per-route bound exists (`RouteOptions` is `body: 'stream'` and `middleware` only, so the only lever is the app-wide `http.maxBodySize`); a multipart post has `ctx.rawBody === undefined`; a text post of invalid UTF-8 has a `rawBody` whose replacement characters are not the signed bytes. The bytes themselves are already kept — `await ctx.$raw.$req.arrayBuffer()` returns all of them on a parsed route, multipart included — so the gap is the bound and `conduit`'s `Delivery.rawBody: string` (`packages/conduit/src/receive.ts`), which every dialect verifies and which `plugin.ts` fills with `ctx.rawBody ?? ''`. The red test: a receiver route given a 300 KB signed body answers 413 today, and 200 once it verifies.
  - **A** — `RouteOptions.maxBodySize` (per route, falling back to `http.maxBodySize`) read by the one `parseBody` call; conduit's receiver route states its own bound (a receiver field, default the app's); `Delivery.rawBody` becomes the bytes (`Uint8Array`), filled from `$raw.$req`, and a dialect that needs text decodes it. One owner for the bound and one spelling for the signed body.
  - **B** — no junction option: the receiver route is `{ body: 'stream' }`, and conduit reads the stream to its own cap and parses JSON/urlencoded itself. Junction is untouched, but conduit grows a second body parser beside `transport/body.ts`.
  - **C** — no code: an app that takes vendor deliveries raises `http.maxBodySize` for the whole app, and `Delivery` reads bytes from `$raw.$req`. Every route then accepts the larger body, which is the exposure the 256 KB default is for.
  - **Recommend A** — the bound is a property of the route that verifies a signature, not of the app, and `parseBody` is already the one place a bound is enforced (Invariant 4); B duplicates the parser and C widens every route. Fastify makes the same split with a per-route `bodyLimit`. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Fastify has a per-route `bodyLimit` that overrides the server's default 1 MiB *(override semantics inferred)* ([Routes docs](https://fastify.dev/docs/latest/Reference/Routes/)). Stripe requires the unmodified raw body for signature verification and warns that frameworks must not manipulate it ([Stripe webhooks](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks#verify-manually)). Both point at A: a per-route bound plus the original bytes as the signed input.

- **FJS-D1312 — Does focus on a text field in the terminal shell set INSERT, as `FJS-D987` rules, or NORMAL, as `Shell.mesa` has done since 2781dd2b?** Found fixing `FJS-2931`. `FJS-D987` (owner, over "vim proper") says a text field is INSERT the moment focus lands, and `example/web/test/verify-tui.mjs:179` asserts it. `Shell.mesa`'s `focusin` sets `normal` on every focus except the jump field, because a route focuses its first control on mount and a scene that opened already typing would eat the h j k l it walks on; no ruling records that change. Red assertion today: after Tab onto the path field the footer reads NORMAL, never INSERT, so `the path field is INSERT the moment focus lands on it` fails.
  - **A** — keep `FJS-D987`: `focusin` sets `insert` on a text field. The drive passes unchanged and no key is spent before typing; the cost is the autofocus case above, so a route whose first control is a text field opens swallowing h j k l until Esc.
  - **B** — amend `FJS-D987` to the code: every focus is NORMAL, `i`, `a` or `/` enters INSERT. The drive's L179 check and the CLAUDE.md line in sierra move with it, and the ruling's "keeps every drive that types into a field working unchanged" is given up; any drive that types straight after Tab must send `i` first.
  - **C** — A, except a field that took focus from a route's own mount-time autofocus (no key or Tab preceded it) stays NORMAL. Both stated cases hold, but the shell has to tell a user's Tab from a mount's `focus()`, a new distinction to keep.
  - **Recommend C** (confidence 2) — the measured failure is a route's own mount-time autofocus on the Haven scenes, and no key a person pressed caused it, so C keeps `FJS-D987` for every focus a person makes and stays NORMAL only for the one the page made. The cost is telling a mount's `focus()` from a Tab, which Tridactyl also keeps. B gives up the owner's ruling for every field.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Vimium enters insert mode whenever a text input has focus, including one the page focused on load, and the exit is Esc ([issue 226](https://github.com/philc/vimium/issues/226)). Page-stolen focus is a known complaint ([VimFX 253](https://github.com/akhodakivskiy/VimFx/issues/253)). Tridactyl answers with `set allowautofocus false`, which blocks page-initiated focus and leaves user focus alone ([changelog](https://github.com/tridactyl/tridactyl/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md)). Neither makes every focus NORMAL. This favors A or C over B.

- **FJS-D1313 — How does auth finish an OAuth flow that connects a TENANT's third-party account — tokens to the app's own row, not a Credential and a session?** Found fixing `FJS-2246`. Measured in the tree 2026-10-19: `OauthFlow` (`packages/auth/db/auth.lite:242`) holds `state`, `provider`, `verifier`, `returnTo` and `invitation` and no initiator or tenant; `oauthCallback` (`auth.ts:1472`) returns `{ identity, tokens, returnTo, link }` and the route then runs `oauthResolve`, which finds-or-creates a person and issues a session. `FJS-D960` ruled `connections.connect(provider)` for a signed-in person's second sign-in provider and it is not built (no `connect` in `services.ts`), and a delegated grant — Google Business Profile for an agency, Jobber for an account — wants neither a sign-in nor a `Credential` row: it wants `tokens` written to the app's own row, as the system inside the account the admin chose, for `FJS-D848`'s `getFor` resolver to spend. The red test: begin a flow for a provider declared for the grant, complete the callback, and assert no `User`, `Credential` or session was created and the tokens reached the app's row.
  - **A** — one more flow kind on the existing engine: `oauthBegin` takes `grant: { model, id }` (and `OauthFlow` gains a guarded `grant` and the initiator's id, set only by a service that has graded the caller), and the callback route, seeing a grant on the flow, calls the provider's `onGrant({ tokens, identity, grant, initiator })` that the app declares in `oauthProviders` instead of `oauthResolve`. A provider declares `purpose: 'grant'` and is then absent from `oauthProviderNames()` for sign-in, so `google-business` cannot become a login. Auth owns the redirect, state, PKCE and exchange; the app owns where the tokens land, so auth never learns the app's `Integration` model.
  - **B** — `connections.connect(provider, { into })` completes `FJS-D960` and widens it: the Credential row stays the one place tokens land, with `ownerType`/`ownerId` (user, or the app's row), and conduit's resolver reads `Credential` for any owner. Nothing app-specific to wire, but a rotating refresh token then lives in auth's table rather than the app's `@secret` field, and an app whose row already holds the tokens (Jobber's `Integration`) copies them across.
  - **C** — no auth change: the app composes `oauthBegin`/`oauthCallback` itself in its own routes, as serpgrid did, and auth documents it. Costs each app the pending-row-by-state-digest workaround `FJS-2246` records, and nothing marks the provider as grant-only.
  - **Recommend A** — `oauthBegin`/`oauthCallback` are already the right engine and serpgrid spent them as-is, so what is missing is only the flow's purpose and who it was started for, both facts the flow row can carry; `onGrant` keeps the write at the app's model, where its gate and `@secret` already are, and `purpose` answers the sign-in-provider leak without a second provider table. B moves the tokens out of the row `FJS-D848`'s resolver is already written against, and C is the status quo the row exists to end. Confidence 2: Supabase leaves provider tokens to the app, but Better Auth ties them to a signed-in user's linked account, so precedent splits.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Supabase Auth says "Provider tokens are intentionally not stored in your project's database" and leaves persisting and refreshing them to the app ([docs](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/auth/social-login)). Better Auth's `linkSocial` connects an extra provider, with more scopes by repeating the call, to an already signed-in user's account, and its token calls need that account ([docs](https://www.better-auth.com/docs/concepts/oauth)). Supabase matches A, Better Auth matches B. Neither has a grant-only flow.

- **FJS-D1314 — Which side of each cross-package duplicate export gives way, and does `fli ws:exports` grade the rest?** Found fixing `FJS-2966`. Measured 2026-10-19: `session` is `sierra/resource` (the signed-in state object, `src/resource/session.js:63`) and `@frontierjs/testing` (a `SessionContext` builder, `src/index.ts:310`); `track` is `sierra/analytics` (an event, `index.js:138`) and `mesa/runtime` (a reactive-proxy wrap, `runtime.js:5871`); `mount` is `jetty/runtime` (`mount.js:62`, a page into an extension root) and `mesa/runtime` (a component into a renderer, `runtime-terminal.js:2177`, `runtime-native.js:569`); `status` is `sierra/resource` (the connection state, `index.js:131`) and `litestone/migrations` (`migrations.js:760`, applied-vs-pending); `parseQuery` is junction's HTTP query-string parser (`transport/body.ts:404`) and litestone-base's `{limit,where,orderBy}` builder (`core/litestone.ts:278`), already exported as `parseLitestoneQuery`, so one name is spelled two ways. `FJS-D1219` struck `createTestEnv` as one thing at two layers; none of these five is that, since each pair does a different job.
  - **A** — rename the narrower side to its job: testing `session` → `sessionContext`, mesa `track` stays and sierra `track` → `trackEvent`, jetty `mount` → `mountPage`, litestone `status` → `migrationStatus` (sierra's `status` stays), junction's litestone `parseQuery` → `parseListQuery` at the source so the `as` alias goes. Every caller moves in one change. Then `fli ws:exports` fails a name two packages export unless a `DECISIONS.md` ruling lists the pair, as it already fails a subpath spelled twice (`FJS-2964`).
  - **B** — rename only where one import list can hold both (`parseQuery`, and `session`/`track` where a test or sierra file imports both); `mount` and `status` live on different subpaths and a reader never sees them side by side. `fli ws:exports` grades only same-subpath-or-same-barrel clashes.
  - **C** — rename nothing but `parseQuery`, and have `fli ws:exports` print the cross-package duplicates as a note with no failure. Cheapest, and the register row narrows to a report.
  - **Recommend A** — one name for one thing is the policy and nothing here has shipped, so no caller is stranded; B and C leave the five-line list the row exists to remove, and a grader with a named allowance list is what keeps the sixth collision from arriving. Confidence 2: outside the repo duplicate names across packages are normal, so B is the fallback if the grader proves noisy.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Svelte exports `mount` from `svelte` ([docs](https://svelte.dev/docs/svelte/imperative-component-api)) and Vue Test Utils exports a different `mount` ([docs](https://test-utils.vuejs.org/api/)), and neither renamed. Rust ships `io::Result` beside the prelude `Result` and tells users to qualify it rather than import it ([std docs](https://doc.rust-lang.org/std/io/type.Result.html)). The ecosystem tolerates duplicates that never share an import list, which favors B. Nothing found grades them with a failing check.

- **FJS-D1317 — Does a derived service name keep its filename spelling as a mounted alias (`FJS-D159`), or is the alias removed so one service answers to one name?** Found fixing `FJS-2960`: the `channels:` half of the row is already deleted (`packages/notifications/plugin.ts` has no such refusal), and what remains is `ServiceRegistry.aliasesOf` (`packages/junction/src/core/service.ts:3156`), which `FJS-D159` ruled in on purpose so `product-variants.service.ts` serves `productVariants` AND `/product-variants`. Consumers: `app.ts:2565` (shadow check), `app-model.ts:97` (`aliases`), `example/api/app.snapshot.md` (*also answers to*), `test/service-name-kebab.test.ts`. `FJS-D159` is a ruling, so removal re-rules it; nothing has shipped, so no URL is held by it.
  - **A** — keep the alias; record in `FJS-D159` that it is a ruled mount, not a compat shim, and drop it from `lockdown.md`'s "Done when" list.
  - **B** — remove it: the service is `productVariants` only, the mount is `/productVariants`, and a multi-word file either is camelCase-named or declares `name:`. `aliasesOf`, the `register` aliases argument, the shadow-loop spelling, `app-model` `aliases` and the snapshot line go in one change; the kebab test becomes a refusal test.
  - **C** — invert it: the kebab spelling is the one mounted name (URL and WS frame), the camelCase accessor is only the model/resource key, and no registry alias exists.
  - **Recommend B** — Evolution policy: one name for one thing, and a URL costs an app nothing before release; `FJS-D159`'s only stated reason for the alias was URLs already shipped. Confidence 3: tRPC and PostgREST each mount one spelling, though `FJS-D159` also argued a rename moves every multi-word URL, which B accepts, so the owner may still prefer A. Adjudication: *preservation vs. evolution*, tier Register.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — tRPC names a procedure by its router object key and documents no alias or second spelling ([docs](https://trpc.io/docs/server/routers)). PostgREST serves a table at a path equal to its name, so `fresh_stories` is `/fresh_stories` ([docs](https://docs.postgrest.org/en/stable/references/api/tables_views.html)). Both mount exactly one spelling and leave URL style to the author's chosen name. Neither offers a derived second mount.

- **FJS-D1319 — What do sierra's setup verbs mean: does `init*` start something and `configure*` only store, and does analytics keep both or export one?** Found fixing `FJS-2962`. Measured 2026-10-19: seven `init*` (`initJunction`, `initRouter`, `initTheme`, `initToolbar`, `initSession`, `initPrefetch`, `initAnalytics`) and three `configure*` (`configureFetch`, `configureAnalytics`, `configureLocalDb`). By side effect the split is not clean: `configureFetch` stores options and swaps the fetch (no start) and `configureAnalytics` resolves a provider and loads nothing (`FJS-D608`/`FJS-2058` made it the island entry's call because the tag is already in the HTML), but `configureLocalDb` (`packages/sierra/src/resource/local-db.js:55`) registers `pagehide` listeners and opens the OPFS engine at once, which is an `init*`. Red test: `configureLocalDb` is exported from `sierra/resource` and the rule "a `configure*` starts nothing" fails on it.
  - **A** — keep the split by side effect, as `lockdown.md` 3.13 words it: `configure*` stores and starts nothing, `init*` starts something. `configureLocalDb` becomes `initLocalDb` (virtual-sierra, `resource/index.js`, the test, README); analytics keeps both exports because they are different acts (resolve vs. resolve and load), and the rule is written once in `VOCABULARY.md`.
  - **B** — the same rename, and analytics exports one verb: `initAnalytics(config, { load: false })` for the island entry, and `configureAnalytics` goes. One name per module, at the price of an option that switches a side effect off.
  - **C** — one verb for all ten (`init*`), the `configure*` spelling removed; the stores-only distinction lives in each doc comment, not the name.
  - **Recommend A** — the plan's own words are *split by side effect*, and analytics' two acts really are two (`FJS-D608`'s ruling gave the island entry the no-load half); B trades a second name for a boolean that flips the verb's meaning. Confidence 2: the row's *analytics picks one* reads as B, so the owner may mean it, and no outside library fixes what `init` versus `configure` mean.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Segment's browser SDK separates creating the instance, which does no network work, from `.load()`, which loads destinations and flushes queued events ([README](https://github.com/segmentio/analytics-next/blob/master/packages/browser/README.md)). That is two acts under two names, as A keeps for analytics. PostHog instead has one `init` and switches capture off by option, `opt_out_capturing_by_default` ([docs](https://posthog.com/docs/libraries/js)), which is B's shape. Sentry and Amplify use `init` and `configure` with no stated side-effect rule.

- **FJS-D1321 — Is copying a row a framework verb, where does it live, and how does the schema name a row's owned children — or does each app keep writing it?** Found fixing `FJS-2675`. Two ela instances (a survey template's `duplicate`, a `@@hasTemplates` model's `fromTemplate`) both copy a row minus `@id`, `@unique`/partial-`@@unique` columns, `@default(now())`/`@updatedAt`, tenant and `@system` stamps, plus its children. The schema names every one of those columns, but nothing names which relations are OWNED children (a `SurveyTemplateScore` row belongs to its template; a `User` reference does not), and `@@hasTemplates` knows the template flag but not the `@sequence` to renumber. The red test is a `duplicate` on a model with a `@unique` column: today there is no such method on `db.<model>`, the auto service or a Resource.
  - **A** — Litestone client verb `db.<model>.copy(id, overrides?, { with: ['children'] })`: drops `@id`, `@unique`, the clock columns, tenant and `@system` (Invariant 6, so the gate still grades the insert), and the caller lists the children to carry by relation name. Nothing new in the schema; the app still names the flag to clear and the title suffix in `overrides`. Cost: a unique column with no override is a refusal by name, and each app still writes a 5-line wrapper for ela's `(Copy)` naming.
  - **B** — A schema declaration of ownership first (`@relation(..., owned)` or `@@owns(scores)`, which also gives `@@softDelete(cascade)` and a deep delete the same word), then A's verb copies the owned set by default. Cost: a new attribute and a noun, a parser, a differ and a `jsonschema` change before the verb, and `FJS-D62`-style precedent says to wait for a third caller.
  - **C** — No framework verb: the row stays "each app writes a typed method", and Litestone ships only the helper that answers *which columns a copy must drop* (`db.$copyable(accessor)` beside `$protectedFields`), so the two ela methods stop re-deriving it by hand. Cost: the children and the template flag stay hand-written, which is where the second instance's bugs were.
  - **Recommend C** — the column list is what the schema alone can answer and the only part both instances got wrong; which children are owned and which flag clears are domain choices with two instances, and a verb built on them is a guess, so A or B is the reopen path when a third app writes the same wrapper. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Django has no copy method. Its docs show setting `pk = None` and `_state.adding = True`, and say relations outside the model's own table are not copied ([docs](https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/stable/topics/db/queries/#copying-model-instances)). Rails core ships `dup` with no child copy *(inference, not read)*, and the `amoeba` gem adds per-model `include_association` declarations ([README](https://github.com/amoeba-rb/amoeba)). Core ORMs stop at the column level. Declared ownership of children arrives as an add-on.

- **FJS-D1322 — How does an app watch a list's rows: a live getter-watch in mesa, a nameable signal on `list()`, or the documented `$: { void list.rows.length; fn() }` spelling?** Found fixing `FJS-2016`. `$: list.rows, () => fn()` compiles to `watchPath(list, 'rows')` (`packages/mesa/src/runtime.js:4884`), an edge-trigger signal that only a write through the proxy fires; `list.rows` (`packages/sierra/src/resource/list.js:353`) is a getter over a private signal, so `_warnAccessorWatch` (`runtime.js:4839`) warns "never fire" and the watch is inert. The block form `$: { void list.rows.length; fn() }` compiles to a plain effect that reads the getter, so it tracks the signal and works. Red test: a component with `$: list.rows, () => { n++ }` whose list reloads — `n` stays 0 today.
  - **A** — Mesa makes a getter-only watch live: when the last segment is an accessor with no setter, `watchPath` runs the getter under an effect and fires the node's signal when what it read changes, and the warning narrows to a getter that reads nothing tracked. Fixes `list.rows`, `list.total`, `page.*`-style getters and every future reactive getter with one rule. Cost: the effect is owned by the first caller's owner and `watchPath` is also called from inside getters (`list.query`), so the node needs its own root and an eager getter evaluation per watched path — a new lifetime to get right.
  - **B** — `list()` exposes a plain signal to name (a `rows$` or `list.watch` handle), so the `$:` form watches an object path that really is written. Sierra-only, no mesa semantics change. Cost: a second spelling beside `rows` for the same data, and every reactive getter on a handle (`total`, `loading`, `hasMore`) wants one too.
  - **C** — Docs only: the sierra guide's Routes section and the mesa warning name `$: { void list.rows.length; fn() }` as the spelling for a derived or signal-backed getter. No code moves; the warning's "watch what the getter reads" advice becomes actionable. Cost: the `$: x, () => fn()` form stays inert for every signal-backed getter and the warning remains the only guard.
  - **Recommend C** — it adds no convention and moves no caller, and the warning already fires correctly for exactly the case ELA hit; A is the reopen path if a second signal-backed getter turns up beyond list handles. Vue documents the same line. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Vue's `watch` says you cannot watch `obj.count` because that passes a number. You wrap it in a getter, `watch(() => obj.count, …)`, so the read happens under tracking ([docs](https://vuejs.org/guide/essentials/watchers.html)).
