That is Mesa's inert path plus its documented block spelling. Svelte 5 runes and Solid also track reads inside an effect *(inference, not fetched)*. None makes a bare value live by magic. This supports C.

- **FJS-D1323 — Does a view absent from the schema get dropped by `migrate create`, when a hand-made view is deliberately carried through a rebuild (`FJS-D66`) and `autoMigrate` keeps one the app created?** Red test, `test/migration-history.test.ts`: history builds `view revenue` over `Order`; the schema then drops the view; `create(null, parse(WITHOUT_VIEW), 'drop_view', dir)` returns `created: false` ("the migration history already builds the schema") today, and a later `total Int → String` rebuild emits `DROP VIEW "revenue"` … `CREATE VIEW "revenue"` … `SELECT 1 FROM "revenue"`. A diff-level `droppedViews` fixes both but fails three `litestone.test.ts` tests (`a view the app created survives it too`, `a view over another table is not touched`, `a view the rebuild invalidates fails the migration`), because `diffSchemas` cannot tell a view litestone emitted from one the app wrote — views carry no name pattern the way `idx_` and `*_fts_*` do.
  - **A** — drop every view the schema does not declare, on the shadow path only: `planAgainstHistory` passes `dropUndeclaredViews`, `autoMigrate` against a live database keeps app-created views. A hand-written view inside a migration file is dropped by the next `migrate create`, and the file is the review step, so it shows as a `DROP VIEW` line.
  - **B** — mark ownership: the DDL litestone emits for a view carries a marker in its SQL text, and only a marked view is dropped, on both paths. Hand-made views survive everywhere; a view built before the marker existed is never dropped, and the `changedViews` text comparison has to ignore the marker.
  - **C** — leave behavior, and make `migrate create` print a note naming the orphan view so the author drops it by hand (what quo did). The restore on every rebuild stays.
  - **Recommend A** — the migration files are what a deploy replays, so a view there that the schema does not name is history the schema has left, and a view holds no rows to lose; B adds a mechanism and leaves every existing database unmarked.

- **FJS-D1325 — Where does a static build's feed (RSS/Atom) and the Slug-history `_redirects` come from?** Found fixing `FJS-3085`. `runPostBuild` (`packages/sierra/src/postbuild/index.js:57`) writes sitemap, robots, llms and `_redirects` from the route table and has no feed step, so ghost's `feed-plugin.js` re-runs `publishedPosts()` in a `closeBundle` (step 12) outside the static-safety tap, and its `redirects-plugin.js` must be a Vite `generateBundle` plugin because step 12 runs after step 3 wrote `dist/_redirects`. The red assertion once built: a static build whose index route declares a feed writes `feed.xml` with its `count` newest items, and a `load()` behind it that reads a gated column the route does not `publishes:` fails the build by name — today neither happens, and the app's plugin reads the rows unchecked. `src/` takes no new `postbuild/` step until `test/edge.test.js` classifies it, and `FJS-D608` starts a site-shaped step as site-kit code until a consumer that is not a site-kit site needs it.
  - **A** — a `feed: [{ path, route, count, title }]` key in `sierra.config.js`, in the sitemap's position: the build serializes the named route's already-loaded rows (the ones its `load()` returned, under the same tap) as RSS 2.0, with the sitemap's XML escaping. Item fields come from the route's `.meta.js` (`title`, `date`, `url`). One new key and a step in `postbuild/`; the redirects case is not covered by it.
  - **B** — the route's `.meta.js` exports `feed: { path, count, item(row) }` and the prerender serializes it beside the page. The feed is declared where the page is, so the published set is the page's own `load()`, and a route without it costs nothing. No config key; the redirects case still needs its own answer.
  - **C** — site-kit owns it (`FJS-D608`): `siteFeed`/`siteRedirects` as Vite `generateBundle` plugins beside `config/redirects.js`, reading through `app-import.js` under a tap site-kit asks Sierra to hand it. Sierra gains nothing; every non-site-kit static app (ghost's `site/`) still writes its own.
  - **D** — state it and change nothing: a feed is an app plugin, and `sierra/CLAUDE.md` says to emit it at `generateBundle` (not `closeBundle`) and to wrap its reads in `createReadRecorder`'s tap. Smallest; leaves the feed outside the gate check unless the app remembers.
  - **Recommend B** — the feed and the page read the same rows in the same `load()`, so the published set is decided once and the gate check covers it for free (§ V.5); A restates the route by name in a second file and drifts. Hugo declares the feed on the page kind, Astro as its own endpoint. Confidence 2: whether `_redirects` from a Slug history is the same shape (rows, not a page) or needs a separate seam is unpriced.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Hugo declares a feed per page kind: the default `outputs` config gives `section` both `html` and `rss` ([docs](https://gohugo.io/configuration/outputs/)). That is B, the feed beside the page. Astro splits it out: a separate `.xml.js` endpoint calls `getCollection()` and builds its own `items`, with no shared filter ([docs](https://docs.astro.build/en/recipes/rss/)). That is D, or A with a second query. Precedent splits between declaring it with the page and writing it as its own route.

- **FJS-D1326 — Should `/fjs-sqlite/sqlite3.wasm` get a long Cache-Control when its name is unhashed and the loader beside it revalidates?** The module fetches its `.wasm` by its own published name (`local-db-plugin.js`), so the file cannot be content-addressed, and `cacheFor` sends `max-age=0, must-revalidate` for anything outside `assets/`. `immutable` there would pin a wasm to a loader that revalidates, so a sqlite-wasm upgrade could pair a new `index.js` with a year-old binary.
  - **A** — leave the revalidate default: the service worker precaches `/fjs-sqlite/*` (`test/offline-shell.test.js`) and a 304 keeps Chrome's HTTP-cache entry, which is what its compiled-module cache hangs off.
  - **B** — put a content hash in the directory name (`fjs-sqlite-<hash>/`) and serve it `immutable`; `WASM_URL` in `virtual-sierra.js` then needs the hash at build.
  - **C** — a bounded `max-age` (a day) on `/fjs-sqlite/*` in `_headers`, accepting a stale pairing for that long after an upgrade.
  - **Recommend A** — measure Chrome's compile cache on a revalidated load before paying for B; C trades correctness for a number nobody has measured.

- **FJS-D1327 — When a wildcard cron's minutes pass with the process down, does the scheduler report them, or stay silent as `FJS-D144` leaves it?** Found fixing `FJS-3108`. `_tickWildcard` (`packages/caravan/src/cron.ts`) asks only whether the clock says this minute, so a restart fires the current minute and `jobs.db` keeps `cron:probe:29869534` and `:29869536` with nothing for `:29869535`; the gap is a fact only a reader diffing the minutes can see. A replay is out (`FJS-D144`: a past minute cannot be probed), so the question is whether the gap is reported at all. Red test, `test/next-fire.test.ts` shape with an injected `now`: register `* * * * *`, tick at minute 10, move `now` to minute 13, tick — assert a gap of 11 and 12 is reported once; today nothing is.
  - **A** — an Observer on the job, `onMissed(ctx: { name, from, to, minutes })`, fired by the scheduler when a wildcard tick finds the last minute it fired (read from `jobs.db`'s newest `cron:<name>:` id, so it survives the restart) more than one behind now. The app records a "no check" row itself. Adds one option and a jobs.db read at start; a throw is swallowed like any observer.
  - **B** — a row: the scheduler dispatches each missed minute under `cron:<name>:<minute>` with status `missed` and no handler run, so the id stays the idempotency key and a reader diffing `scheduledFor` finds a row. Adds a terminal status to the jobs table that every list, count and cleanup must learn.
  - **C** — no code: state in `caravan/CLAUDE.md` that a wildcard gap is a fact the app reads off `scheduledFor`, and ship nothing. A probe app diffs the minutes itself.
  - **Recommend A** — the fact is the app's to map (a 'no check' Check is statuspage's noun, not Caravan's), and B puts an app meaning into the queue's status column; C leaves every probing app writing the same jobs.db diff.

- **FJS-D1328 — How does a register row get an edit that is not an append: a replace on `amend`, a verb per edit, or does the append-only rule stand?** Found fixing `FJS-2751`. `amendUnguarded` (`packages/cli/core/amend.js`) only ever writes `${kept.text} · ${added}`, and its header states the rule: what a row already says is how the defect was measured, so an amend that can drop it loses the evidence. The only removal is `drop`, one whole ` · ` segment, reached by `register:repro` and not by a flag. The audit's ~640 hand writes are three kinds of edit the writer cannot do: retarget a link whose file moved, renumber an `FJS-D` id, reword a cell. Red test, `test/amend.test.js` shape: a row whose Detail holds a link to a moved file; `amendIssue({ root, id, replace: ['old', 'new'] })` — today the option is ignored and the cell is refused for having no `detail`.
  - **A** — `--replace "old=>new"` on `amend`, Detail cell of an open row, `old` must occur exactly once or the write is refused; `--detail` stays the append and the two may be given together. Adds one option and relaxes the header's rule to "a replacement names what it replaces". Covers a reword and one link; a renumber across many rows is still a loop of calls, and a § Closed row stays refused.
  - **B** — a verb per edit, none touching `amend`: `fli register:retarget <old> <new>` (every register link resolving to `old`, the pass `archive.js` already runs when it moves rows), and `fli register:renumber <old-id> <new-id>` (the id in its row and every citation, with `register:check` as the put-back). A reword has no verb and stays hand-written. Adds two commands and two nouns, each owning the whole-register edit rather than one row.
  - **C** — no code: a hand edit to a cell is an `Edit` call on one line, which `register:check` already grades afterward; the 339 python replaces were the habit of a model without a writer, not a missing capability. State it in `cli/CLAUDE.md` and ship nothing.
  - **Recommend A** — all three audited edits are a text swap inside one row, one flag covers them with the put-back `amend` already has, and B's whole-register verbs are worth building only after a swap proves too small, which `FJS-2392` and `FJS-2328` would show first.

- **FJS-D1341 — How does a multiselect over a `Model.verb` enum list show its members grouped by model: MultiSelect takes Select's grouped options shape, a `groupBy` prop, a capability-matrix control, or the flat list stays?** `FJS-3151`: basecamp's `capabilities Capability[]` (`packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite:907`) reaches `controlFor` as `{ control: 'multiselect', task: 'select', allowNew: false }` (`packages/sierra/src/resource/field-rules.js:776`), and the 78 members (`'Environment.create' | 'Server.reboot' | …`, `packages/basecamp/db/schema.d.ts:115`) render as one unbroken list. `Select.mesa` already takes `{ group, options }[]` and draws an `<optgroup>` per group (`packages/ui/components/forms/Select.mesa:14`, `:105`); `MultiSelect.mesa` takes only a flat list (`packages/ui/components/forms/MultiSelect.mesa:28`).
  - **A** — MultiSelect takes Select's grouped shape, `{ group, options }[]`, and `controlFor` emits it for an enum list whose every member is `Model.verb`, splitting on the dot. No new prop: the grouping is derived from the members, and the options shape is one across both controls.
  - **B** — a `groupBy` prop on MultiSelect (a separator or a function), and `controlFor` passes `groupBy: '.'`. Cost: a second way to say grouping beside Select's shape, and a prop every hand-written caller must know.
  - **C** — a capability-matrix control: models as rows, verbs as columns, a checkbox per member that exists, chosen by `controlFor` for a `Model.verb` list. Reads best at 78 members, but coins a control and a `task` with one caller.
  - **D** — the flat list stays, and an app that wants groups passes its own options. Cost: every `@@capabilities` app writes the same split, and the generated form stays unbroken.
  - **Recommend A** — Select is the sibling that already decided this shape, so A adds no convention and the grouping is derived rather than restated. C is the reopen path if a grouped dropdown still scans badly at basecamp's size. Confidence 3: Mantine also gives Select and MultiSelect one grouped shape.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Mantine's Select and MultiSelect both take grouped data as `{ group, items }[]`, one shape across single and multi ([Select](https://mantine.dev/core/select/), [MultiSelect](https://mantine.dev/core/multi-select/)). MUI Autocomplete groups by a `groupBy` function over a flat option list ([docs](https://mui.com/material-ui/react-autocomplete/)), which is B's shape, and its grouped demo is single-select. Two of three precedents share one shape across both controls.

- **FJS-D1342 — Does a client subscription under a service's filename spelling hear the canonical name's events: the client folds the name by `FJS-D159`'s rule, a subscription under an alias is refused by name, or it stays deaf and documented?** `FJS-3124`: `deriveName` folds `log-chunks` to `logChunks` and mounts the stem as an alias (`packages/junction/src/core/loader.ts:219`), so a CALL under either spelling reaches one service and announces as `logChunks created`. The client keys a frame by the name it was handed — `this._services.get(serviceName)?._receive(method, data)` (`packages/junction/src/client/index.ts:2507`) — so `service('log-chunks').on('created')` received 0 of 56 frames while `service('logChunks')` received all 56. The client holds no manifest, so it cannot ask the server which name is canonical.
  - **A** — the client folds: `client.service(name)` keys its proxy by `camel(name)` from `@frontierjs/toolbelt/inflect` (which already answers `log-chunks` → `logChunks`, `product_variants` → `productVariants`), and `deriveName` uses the same `camel` so the rule has one owner. `service('log-chunks')` and `service('logChunks')` are then one proxy, and the alias covers subscriptions as D159 says it covers calls. Cost: a service an app registers by hand under a name with a dash is reachable for calls but heard under the folded name only.
  - **B** — the client refuses: `service(name)` throws on a name that `camel()` changes, naming the canonical spelling. Nothing is deaf, but every call that works today under the filename spelling stops, which takes back D159's alias on the client.
  - **C** — it stays as it is, and the client's docs say to subscribe under the canonical name. The deaf listener stays silent, which is the defect.
  - **Recommend A** — D159 already ruled one canonical spelling derived by a rule, and A applies that rule where the name enters the client instead of a second spelling living beside it. B moves every caller; C leaves a wrong answer nothing reports. Confidence 3: Feathers also normalizes a service path where it enters `app.service()`.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Feathers folds the name at the single entry point: `app.service(location)` runs `stripSlashes(location)` before looking up the service, so spellings that differ by slashes reach one service and one event stream ([source](https://github.com/feathersjs/feathers/blob/dove/packages/feathers/src/application.ts)). It normalizes only slashes, not case or dashes. No product found refuses an alternate spelling or leaves it deaf. Normalizing at entry is A's shape.

- **FJS-D1343 — Do the five blessed words with few prose hits — Vector, Fault, Gesture, Pivot, Adversary — stay blessed, or are the ones with no identifier in the tree demoted to open?** `FJS-2456` counted capitalized prose hits outside the doctrine files: Vector 3, Fault 1, Gesture 8, Pivot 5, Adversary 5. A lowercase and table sweep finds four in use: Vector as `served-path-vectors.json` and `audit-directives-vectors.test.ts` in junction's suite, Pivot in `packages/cli/core/deploy-plan.js:238` and `packages/cli/db/deploy.lite`, Adversary as the row key of `THREATS.md:9`, Gesture in `ARCHITECT.md:75` with `IDEAS/mesa-ir.md`'s open question *Is a handler's event name a DOM event or a Gesture?* waiting on it. Fault is ruled (`FJS-D655`) and its definition cites `FAULT_KINDS` in `@frontierjs/toolbelt/fault`, which does not exist yet: `IDEAS/failure-handling.md:36` records it as absent and owes the build. The row's other half, `fli ws:terms` misreporting Run, Method and Migration as `definedUnseen`, is a defect and stays the row's.
  - **A** — all five stay blessed. Each is a ruled word (`FJS-D385`, `FJS-D636`, `FJS-D655`, `FJS-D656`), four are in use under a spelling the case-sensitive count did not see, and Fault's code is owed by a paper. `FJS-2456` narrows to the `ws:terms` misreport.
  - **B** — Fault and Vector go to `open` until an identifier carries them: Fault's definition names a constant nobody can import, and Vector lives only in test fixture names. Cost: reopens two rulings, and Fault comes back the day `toolbelt/fault` lands.
  - **C** — a threshold: any blessed word under ten prose hits with no source identifier goes to `open`, graded by `fli ws:terms`. Cost: a rule that demotes a word for being precise and rarely needed, which Adversary and Pivot are by design.
  - **Recommend A** — the low counts measure capitalization, not use, and every word has a ruling behind it, so A moves nothing and reopens nothing. B is the reopen path for Fault if `IDEAS/failure-handling.md` is withdrawn rather than built. Confidence 2: no outside analogue, this is FrontierJS's own vocabulary list.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — No outside analogue for a blessed-word count. The nearest idea is Evans' ubiquitous language, which Fowler describes as "based on the Domain Model used in the software" and as evolving with the team's understanding ([bliki](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/UbiquitousLanguage.html)). Neither source sets a usage threshold or says a term must appear as an identifier *(inference)*.

- **FJS-D1344 — Does example's payroll take a measured hour: a third basis whose lines are the gross, a Payslip drafted from lines rather than a window, or does example stay a contract payroll and the workforce shape stay the prototype's?** `FJS-1246`: `PayWindowRow.basis` is `'salary' | 'hourly'` (`example/api/src/domain/payroll/employment.ts:93`), `annualGross` is `rate * hoursPerWeek * 52` (`employment.ts:312`), and `draftPayslip` takes one window and emits one `basicPay` line (`example/api/src/domain/payroll/payslip.ts:98`, `:116`). A connectteam timesheet pushed through it came out €12.00 wrong per fortnight from rounding 3.5 h/week to an `Int`, lost its per-line `rateSnapshot`, and had nowhere to put overtime. The row says itself that nothing here is a defect in what `example` ships.
  - **A** — a third basis, `measured`: `PayWindowRow` carries `lines: { onDate, kind, minutes, rate }[]`, `annualGross` is not called for it, and `draftPayslip` emits one earning line per `kind`, so overtime gets a line outside `arrears.ts`. Cost: every reader of `basis` grows a branch.
  - **B** — `draftPayslip` gains a second entry that takes lines and no window, and `PayWindow` stays the contract path. Cost: two ways in to one Payslip, and the window's period arithmetic restated for lines.
  - **C** — example stays a contract payroll. A workforce product's measured pay is the prototype's own domain (`fjs-prototypes/connectteam`), and `FJS-1246` closes as out of scope for example, the measurement recorded in the connectteam PLAN where it was taken.
  - **Recommend C** — no framework package is touched by any option, so this is app modelling, and C moves no caller and adds no shape to the kitchen sink for a product it is not. B is the reopen path when a second stressor needs measured pay through example's payroll. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — No outside analogue: whether the kitchen-sink example takes a measured-hour payroll is a scope call about FrontierJS's own example app. No product or library faces the same choice, so none was searched.

- **FJS-D1346 — Are the two orphaned npm names, `@frontierjs/utils` and `@frontierjs/datetime-kit`, deprecated with a pointer to toolbelt, also unpublished, or left as they are?** `FJS-274`: both folded into `@frontierjs/toolbelt` on 2026-08-15 and their source is gone, so `@frontierjs/utils@0.1.0` and `@frontierjs/datetime-kit@0.0.10` stay installable and point nowhere; `IDEAS/datetime-kit.md` found the latter's `extend()` and `add(d, '1 day')` throwing on every call. npm's unpublish policy (https://docs.npmjs.com/policies/unpublish) allows removing a package older than 72 hours only when nothing on the registry depends on it, it had under 300 downloads in the last week, and it has a single owner; a removed `name@version` can never be published again. `npm deprecate` (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/commands/npm-deprecate) leaves the tarball and prints the message at every install, and is undone with an empty message.
  - **A** — `npm deprecate` both, the message naming `@frontierjs/toolbelt` and the subpath (`/datetime` for datetime-kit). Reversible, and every install shows the pointer the row asks for.
  - **B** — A, then unpublish both where npm's policy allows it, so the broken datetime-kit cannot be installed at all. Irreversible, and refused outright if either has a dependent.
  - **C** — leave both as they are. A reader finds a FrontierJS name that throws, with no pointer.
  - **Recommend A** — it is what npm offers for exactly this case, it is undoable, and it gives the pointer the row wants; B adds only the removal of a version nobody is told to install, at an irreversible cost. Running it is an outward-facing action, so the ruling also authorizes it. Confidence 3.

- **FJS-D1347 — Where do fli's project commands live, now that `cli/src/routes/` is also the app CLI surface's hand-written routes?** `FJS-1352`: `routesDir` defaults to `cli/src/routes` (`packages/cli/core/config.js:18`, and `fli new` writes it into `.fli.json`, `packages/cli/commands/project/new.md:357`), while `FJS-D397` made `cli/` a surface whose `src/routes/` holds the app's own commands (`routeFiles`, `packages/mcp/src/client/routes.ts:83`). Each program reads the other's files as noise only because one takes `.md` and the other `.js`/`.mjs`/`.ts`. The fli files that would move today: `cli/src/routes/shortcut/` at this root (4), `example/cli/src/routes/shortcut/go-time.md` and `packages/cli/cli/src/routes/hello/`. Basecamp's `cli/src/routes/servers/status.js` is the surface's.
  - **A** — fli's default moves to `.fli/commands/` at the project root, beside `.fli.json` and in the same shape as the `~/.fli/` fli already keeps per user (`packages/cli/core/registry.js:33`). The existing project commands move in the same change, and `fli new` and `fli make:command` write the new path. The `cli/` surface keeps `src/routes/`.
  - **B** — the surface moves instead, to `cli/src/commands/`, and fli keeps `cli/src/routes/`. Cost: the surface loses the rule its own header states, *the path is the name, the way a file under `web/src/routes/` is a page*, and the surface is the newer, ruled one.
  - **C** — one directory with two readers split by extension, written down. One path still spells two commands in two programs, each seeing half.
  - **Recommend A** — fli's project commands are tooling, not a surface, and a dot directory at the root is where tooling lives (`.fli.json`, `~/.fli/`, `.claude/commands/`). It also takes a fli-only directory out of Invariant 3's surface layout. GitHub and Cargo both keep project tooling definitions in a dot directory at the root. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — GitHub Actions defines workflows in `.github/workflows` ([docs](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/concepts/workflows-and-actions/workflows)). Cargo reads project config, including `[alias]` command definitions, from `.cargo/config.toml` in the project directory and its parents ([docs](https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/reference/config.html)). Tool-owned project commands and config live in a dot directory, never in the app's own source tree, which is option A.

- **FJS-D1348 — Does Mesa accept a CDATA section inside an `<svg>` or `<math>` subtree?** `FJS-966`: the compiler recognizes `<![CDATA[` only to refuse it by name and say what to write (`packages/mesa/src/compiler.js:1991`). The HTML parsing spec accepts a CDATA section in foreign content only, where it becomes a text node, and Vue's compiler follows it: `@vue/compiler-core` has a parse error `CDATA_IN_HTML_CONTENT` for one outside foreign content. Svelte has no handling at all, per the row.
  - **A** — accept it in a foreign subtree as LITERAL text: no `{` interpolation, the content escaped into one text node. Outside foreign content it is refused as today. This is a literal-text escape with a rule of its own, and so a language feature.
  - **B** — accept it in a foreign subtree and interpolate `{` as in ordinary text. Smallest parser change, but the wrapper's whole meaning is *literal*, so a pasted `{` inside one becomes an expression, which is the surprise A avoids.
  - **C** — keep refusing. The refusal already names the construct and says what to write instead. The cost is one paste and one edit, and `FJS-966` closes as answered.
  - **Recommend C** — the cost of not having it is one error message that already tells you the fix, and A adds a language rule with one source (an exporter's paste) that only DOM backends would give a meaning (`FJS-D38`). A is the reopen path if a second case appears where pasted SVG has to stay byte-for-byte. The HTML spec and Vue accept it only in foreign content, so A is the shape if it ever lands. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — The HTML parsing spec reports `cdata-in-html-content` for a CDATA section outside SVG or MathML and treats it as a comment ([spec](https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/parsing.html#markup-declaration-open-state)). Vue's compiler has the parse error `CDATA_IN_HTML_CONTENT`, "CDATA section is allowed only in XML context" ([source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vuejs/core/main/packages/compiler-core/src/errors.ts)). So accepting it in foreign content only is the standard shape. Nothing found says whether to spend a language rule on it.

- **FJS-D1349 — Does `ServiceContextLocals` keep its index signature?** `FJS-774`: the interface declares `paginate` and `readCall` and then `[key: string]: unknown` (`packages/junction/src/core/context.ts:394-403`), so `$.locals.brandng` compiles and answers `unknown`, where `app.brandng` is an error. Junction itself writes one key the interface does not declare, `ctx.locals.tenantId` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts:5252`, `:5393`). TypeScript's `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature` (https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature.html) makes a dot read of an index-signature key an error (TS4111) and leaves bracket access legal.
  - **A** — drop the index signature. Junction declares every key it writes, `tenantId` included, and an app declares its own by augmenting `ServiceContextLocals`, the pattern Invariant 5 prescribes for `app.own()`. A typo is an error on read and on write. Cost: an app writing an ad-hoc key today must add a declaration first.
  - **B** — keep the signature, and `@frontierjs/config`'s tsconfig sets `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`, so a declared key reads by dot and an undeclared one only as `$.locals['x']`. Cost: the flag is app-wide and changes how every `Record` in the app is read, to fix one interface.
  - **C** — leave it as it is, and document that a misspelled read is silent.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 5 already chose augmentation as the way an app types its own key, and nothing has shipped, so breaking an unaugmented ad-hoc key is the evolution policy rather than a cost. What A moves is measured: basecamp writes `workspaceId` (`packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts:105`, `:186`, and three services), `mintedToken` and `statusFilter` (`api-keys.service.ts:184`, `:207`), `apiKeyId` (`domain/api-key/scopes.ts:151`) and a `MEMBERSHIP` key (`core/hooks.ts:185`), which makes one augmentation block in that app; `example` writes none. B fixes one interface by changing the whole app. Confidence 3: Hono also types context variables by an augmentable map.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Hono types `c.set`/`c.get` keys through an augmentable `ContextVariableMap` or a `Variables` generic, with no documented catch-all key ([docs](https://hono.dev/docs/api/context)). Express types `res.locals` as `Record<string, any>` intersected with an empty, augmentable `Locals` interface, so an undeclared key is silently legal ([types](https://github.com/DefinitelyTyped/DefinitelyTyped/blob/master/types/express-serve-static-core/index.d.ts)). Both offer augmentation. Only Hono turns a typo into an error.

- **FJS-D1350 — How does a one-per-key worker queue its successor while it runs?** `FJS-2287`: `unique` is held among live jobs, `status IN ('pending', 'running')` (`jobs_unique_live`, `packages/caravan/src/db.ts:130`), so a re-dispatch from inside the running handler returns its own id and the chain dies. Serpgrid loops until drained and adds a one-minute cron watcher for a dispatch that lands between the last read and the end of the job. BullMQ answers the same case with `keepLastIfActive` (https://docs.bullmq.io/api/types/v6.DeduplicationOptions.html): a duplicate added while the job is active is kept, and a new job is created when the active one finishes, so at most one active and one waiting exist per id.
  - **A** — `unique` frees its key when the job STARTS (index over `pending` only). The successor queues, but it can then be claimed while its predecessor still runs, which breaks the one-per-key limit the worker exists for (serpgrid's vendor limit is per account).
  - **B** — a dispatch option keeps one follower: while the key's job is running, a dispatch under that key writes or refreshes one pending row, the claim query skips a pending row whose key has a running job, and the index becomes one pending plus one running per key. This is BullMQ's guarantee, and the option's spelling is settled when it is built.
  - **C** — no change. The loop plus a cron watcher is the documented pattern, and `FJS-2287` closes as by design.
  - **Recommend B** — it is the precedent's answer to exactly this case, it keeps the one-per-key limit A gives up, and it deletes a watcher every drained-key worker would otherwise write. It is an option, so `decision-rules` § adding an option applies when it is spelled. Graphile Worker also schedules a new job when the keyed one is locked. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — BullMQ `keepLastIfActive` keeps the latest add while the job is active and queues a replacement when it finishes, so one job per id runs at a time ([docs](https://docs.bullmq.io/guide/jobs/deduplication)). Graphile Worker `job_key` in the default `replace` mode schedules a new job when the keyed one is locked, and the old one loses its key ([docs](https://worker.graphile.org/docs/job-key)). Oban counts only states listed in `states` toward uniqueness *(inference: the docs do not state the executing case)* ([docs](https://oban.hexdocs.pm/Oban.Job.html)). Two products answer with B.

- **FJS-D1351 — How are realms and surfaces colored in dev output?** `FJS-1564` asks for one color table (realm and surface to hue, ANSI and hex) with one owner, never the only signal, degrading under `NO_COLOR` and 16-color terminals. It leaves three questions open: hue family against distinct hues, what cli gets, and whether Deployment and Testing get colors. A 16-color terminal has six chromatic hues (red, green, yellow, blue, magenta, cyan), each in normal and bright, and red is already the failure color across `fli`'s output. Prior art is thin and points the other way. Turborepo passes the task's own colors through and controls only whether the `<package>:<task>:` prefix appears (`--log-prefix`, https://turbo.build/repo/docs/reference/run), and process runners such as `concurrently` and `docker compose` hand out a color per process in order. That order-assigned coloring is exactly what the row replaces (`bun run --parallel`). No tool was found that colors by what a process IS.
  - **A** — one hue per realm, and the surface named in text beside it (`api`, `web`, `cli`). Five realms take five of the six hues; Testing stays neutral so red keeps meaning *failed*. This answers all three questions: cli is a surface and gets its name, and Deployment gets a hue.
  - **B** — a hue family per realm and a shade per surface. Six of the eight surfaces are UI, so this needs six distinguishable shades of one hue, which a 16-color terminal does not have and color-blind separation will not survive.
  - **C** — a distinct hue per surface, with realms uncolored. Eight surfaces are more than the six hues, so two share one, and red has to be one of them.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option that fits the 16-color constraint the row itself sets, and it keeps the label the row requires anyway as the thing that tells surfaces apart. The row reads as a feature rather than a defect, so the ruling can also move it to an `IDEAS/` paper. Git and NO_COLOR support semantic slots that degrade, not realm hues. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Git colors by what the output is, through named slots such as `color.diff.<slot>` and `color.status.<slot>`, each overridable, with `color.ui` as the fallback and `auto` resolving on whether stdout is a terminal ([docs](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-config)). The NO_COLOR convention says software adding ANSI color by default should check `NO_COLOR` and, when it is present and non-empty, add none ([spec](https://no-color.org/)). Both back a small named table that degrades. Neither colors by realm, so the realm-to-hue choice itself has no precedent.

- **FJS-D1359 — What signal lets `fli check` tell a `.mesa` screen that fills a list from `service.find()` from a kept one-row lookup, join or count?** `FJS-3346`: `FJS-D1318` ruled that a rule grades a route calling `.service.find(` to fill a table, and `FJS-2968` tried the text form (`find` feeding `<Table rows>`) which flagged two kept sites and missed every `{#each}` list. `.service.find(` appears in ~25 `.mesa` files today (basecamp settings, portal, activity, servers; haven Notices; example payroll, orders), and the call text is identical whether the result is a list, a one-row `.data?.[0]`, a `.total` or a join. The red assertion: a screen with `rows = (await x.service.find()).data` read by `{#each rows}` must grade, `total = (await x.service.find({}, { limit: 1 })).total` must not, and no text rule separates them. Existing precedent is the `service.get` rule in `packages/cli/core/checks.js` (bare assignment to an outer `let`, a warning that fails open).
  - **A** — the compiler gives it: Mesa exposes a script/template analysis (which top-level bindings are assigned from which call, which bindings `{#each}` or an attribute reads), and a `fli check` rule joins *assigned from `X.service.find(`* with *read by `{#each}` or `rows=`*. Exact for the list case, and a kept lookup reads `.data?.[0]` or `.total` so never reaches a list. Cost: a new compiler surface, and Mesa is the leaf with no notion of a resource, so the join lives in the rule.
  - **B** — the resource gives it: `service.find()` returns the envelope with the rows tagged in dev (a symbol on the array), and Sierra warns when a tagged array is iterated by a list block or handed to `<Table rows>`. Runtime-exact, but it is a dev warning rather than a check, grades only a route that runs, and adds a mark to the one door `FJS-D1318` keeps as the plain escape hatch.
  - **C** — the `service.get` shape: a text rule on bare assignment of `await X.service.find(` to an outer `let`, a warning that fails open, with `.data?.[0]`, `.total` and `{ composed: true }` reads exempt. Cheapest and no new surface; it still cannot see a list built through a local, which is the miss `FJS-2968` hit.
  - **Recommend A** — the signal is already in the file (a binding and its readers), only the compiler parses a `.mesa` reliably, and C's misses are exactly the `{#each}` lists the ruling wants graded. B grades by running, which is what `fli check` exists not to need. C is the sibling-precedent pick (`service.get`, same domain) and the cheaper one; it is the fallback if A's compiler surface proves larger than one exported analysis, and either way the rule is a warning that fails open. Vue and Svelte lint rules already get template expressions as AST from the framework's own parser. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — `vue-eslint-parser` builds a `<template>` AST and lets one rule visit template and script together through `defineTemplateBodyVisitor` ([README](https://github.com/vuejs/vue-eslint-parser)). `svelte-eslint-parser` ships its own AST and a generated `ScopeManager` for Svelte files ([README](https://github.com/sveltejs/svelte-eslint-parser)). Both put the parse in the framework's tooling and leave the rule to join bindings with readers *(inference: the binding-to-template-reference link is the scope manager's job, the READMEs do not say so)*. No tagged-runtime-array analogue was found for B. This supports A.

- **FJS-D1366 — Does a read retry on a transient failure client-side, or stay as today: no retry, the pending queue for writes, the reconnect refetch for reads?** Found reviewing fetchium against the junction client. A call is sent once (`_request`, `_wsCall`); the socket reconnect backs off 1.5x to 30s with no jitter; `reconnected` refetches every list and record with 0–2s jitter (`FJS-701`); a write goes queue-first and the queue drains on `connect`, stopping at the first `unreachable()` (no status, or 408). Prior art: fetchium and TanStack retry per attempt with backoff and a `shouldRetry(error, attempt, status)` hook; Apollo's `RetryLink` is opt-in and off by default.
  - **A** — stay. A transient read failure surfaces as the call's error; a socket drop is already answered by the reconnect refetch, and a write by the queue. Cost: a 502 from a deploy restart stays on the screen until the person acts or the socket cycles.
  - **B** — retry READS only, bounded: three attempts, backoff with jitter, on no status / 408 / 502 / 503 / 504, never a write (a write has its idempotency key and its queue). Fetchium's `shouldRetry` shape is the hook if a caller asks. Cost: a second definition of *transient* beside `pending.js`'s `unreachable()`, and a knob.
  - **C** — one owner: `unreachable()` becomes the one definition for both directions and a read waits on `connect` the way a write does. Cost: a read that waits is a screen that hangs on a drained queue.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — TanStack Query and SWR retry reads by default while Apollo and urql leave it opt-in, so precedent splits and `FJS-D297`/`FJS-D298` already split offline by owner and `load()` falls back to the device only on `unreachable`; a retry is a second answer to the same event. Nothing moves. B is the reopen path, and the trigger is a real release restart (basecamp) showing the error to people rather than to a drive.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — TanStack Query retries a failed query 3 times by default, with the delay doubling from 1000 ms up to 30 s ([docs](https://tanstack.com/query/latest/docs/framework/react/guides/query-retries)). SWR retries on error with exponential backoff, and `shouldRetryOnError: false` turns it off ([docs](https://swr.vercel.app/docs/error-handling)). Apollo's `RetryLink` is opt-in, as the question records. Both read-cache clients that retry do so for reads only. Precedent splits between default-on and opt-in, and leans B if a trigger appears.

- **FJS-D1367 — Does a memo or `$:` refuse a write to a signal it already read this run, as Signalium throws at the line, or keep the `_running` guard and the 100-run cycle cap?** Found reviewing Signalium against the runtime. A memo clears `dirty` before `fn` (`runtime.js` ~716), so a write to a signal it read is accepted and the memo re-runs; a `$:` that wakes itself is caught by `_running` (`FJS-2652`); a true cycle stops at the hundredth run, and the message names a count, not a line. Signalium throws *A signal was dirtied after it was consumed by the current function* at the write, in dev and prod.
  - **A** — keep both guards, and make the cap NAME the line: when it trips in dev, the message carries the memo's `_src` and the signal written (the graph already holds both for devtools, `_cause`). Cost: a dev message. Nothing refuses.
  - **B** — refuse in dev at the write: a write to a signal the running memo or `$:` already read this run throws naming both; prod keeps today's guards. Cost: a `$:` that normalizes its own input (a clamp, a trim) converges today and would throw; the 21-app corpus (`bun run corpus`) has to be tallied first.
  - **C** — refuse in prod too, Signalium's answer. Cost: an app that converges today fails at runtime.
  - **Recommend A** (confidence 2) — Vue's own answer for an effect is the same cap with a dev message naming the component, and Mesa's answer to a mistake it survives is a `[Mesa]` warning naming the line, not a refusal (`FJS-D372` catches, the drives fail on the warning); A moves no caller and B is the reopen path once the corpus says no component writes what it read.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Vue caps a re-queued job at `RECURSION_LIMIT = 100` and reports "Maximum recursive updates exceeded in component <Name>" in dev ([source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vuejs/core/main/packages/runtime-core/src/scheduler.ts)). That is A for effects. Svelte 5 splits by kind: an effect that loops hits `effect_update_depth_exceeded`, but writing state inside `$derived` throws `state_unsafe_mutation` ([docs](https://svelte.dev/docs/svelte/runtime-errors)). That is B or C for derived values. Signalium throws at the write, per the question. Precedent favors A for `$:` and refusal for memos.

- **FJS-D1369 — How does a route FILE carry route options — a per-route `maxBodySize` (and the `body: 'stream'` it also cannot reach) — when its exports are only the verbs?** `FJS-3132`: `autoloadRoutes` (`junction/src/core/loader.ts`) calls `app.post(url, mod.POST)` with no third argument, so `RouteOptions` (`FJS-D1159`'s `{ body: 'stream' }`) is reachable from `app.post` but never from a file under `routes/`, and `_handleRequest` parses every matched route with the one `http.maxBodySize`. Red test: a route registered with `{ maxBodySize: 2 MB }` under an app whose `http.maxBodySize` is 256 KB takes a 1 MB POST (today 413), while `/api/*` services and a WS frame stay at 256 KB. The transport half (`RouteOptions.maxBodySize` → `RouteDefinition` → the `parseBody` bound, Bun's `maxRequestBodySize` already at the max of the two) follows `FJS-D1159` and is not in question; the file form is.
  - **A** — one named export, `export const options = { maxBodySize: 16 * 1024 * 1024 }`, read by the loader and passed as the third argument to every verb in that file. One shape for every `RouteOptions` key (`body`, `middleware`, `maxBodySize`), no per-verb split; a file that wants one verb different splits into two URLs it already cannot share.
  - **B** — per-verb: `export const POST = Object.assign(handler, { maxBodySize })`, or `export const POST = route(handler, { maxBodySize })` with a `route()` helper. Exact per verb, but it coins a wrapper and the handler is no longer the bare `(ctx) => …` the README promises.
  - **C** — leave files alone: the app registers the large route by `app.post(path, handler, { maxBodySize })` in code. Adds no convention, but `FJS-D626` made the folder the place a route lives, so the one route that most needs a bound would be the one outside it.
  - **Recommend A** — it moves no caller, adds one export that is the existing `RouteOptions` verbatim (so the type is the doc), and fixes `body: 'stream'` being unreachable from a file in the same stroke; B is the reopen path if verbs in one file need different bounds. Next.js declares route options the same way. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Next.js route files configure behavior by exporting named variables beside the handlers, such as `runtime` and `maxDuration`, one value per file and not per verb ([Route Segment Config](https://nextjs.org/docs/app/api-reference/file-conventions/route-segment-config)). That is A's shape. Fastify takes route options like `bodyLimit` as a per-route object, the form `app.post(path, handler, options)` already has here ([Routes](https://fastify.dev/docs/latest/Reference/Routes/)).

- **FJS-D1370 — Does enabling deploy on push register the hook on the forge, and with whose credential, or is pasting the shown URL the design?** `FJS-3393`: `apps.enablePush` mints a secret and URL and the App's Config tab now shows them once, but `IGit.createWebhook(repo, url, events)` is still `StubGit`'s `{ id: 0 }` — no `IGit` implementation exists, it takes no secret to sign with, and an App's `source.repo` can name any host (the seed's is `forgejo.local`, a customer's is GitHub), so registering needs a token for THAT host that nothing in the tree stores.
  - **A** — a ForgejoGit behind `IGit` (the `TODO: if (cfg.git?.url)` in `providers/index.ts`): `createWebhook` gains the secret, `enablePush` registers on Basecamp's own forge when the repo URL is that host and `disablePush` deletes the hook. One host, one installation-level token; every other host still pastes.
  - **B** — a per-workspace forge connection (a `Secret` of kind `forge_token` per host, like `provider_key` for a compute account, `FJS-D558`): `enablePush` registers on whichever host `source.repo` names through a GitHub/Forgejo/Gitea dialect, `disablePush` removes it. Coins a noun and a screen to hold the token; covers customers' repos.
  - **C** — pasting is the design: the screen shows the URL, secret and token URL once (CapRover's and Cloudflare's deploy-hook shape, `FJS-D884`), and `createWebhook` leaves `IGit` since nothing calls it. No credential held, nothing to rotate on a forge.
  - **Recommend C** (confidence 3) — a token able to write hooks on a customer's repository is a bigger secret to hold than the one it saves a paste of, the token URL already serves a forge that signs nothing, and B can be added later as a convenience without moving anything built here. Coolify already ships paste-the-webhook as its fallback beside an automatic GitHub App path, the same layering as C now and B later.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Coolify registers nothing itself for public repositories and deploy-key repositories: you copy its webhook URL, set a webhook secret, and create the hook at the provider ([docs](https://coolify.io/docs/applications/ci-cd/github/auto-deploy)). Its GitHub App path configures events automatically. Vercel registers hooks through an installed GitHub App holding `Web Hooks` write permission ([docs](https://vercel.com/docs/git/vercel-for-github)). Automatic registration needs a per-forge app, which is B's cost, and paste stays the fallback everywhere.

- **FJS-D1371 — How does a broker framing tell conduit a refusal is permanent, and what does conduit do with it?** `FJS-3276`: in `BrokerTransport.connect()` (`packages/conduit/src/transports/broker.ts`) a handshake that throws, a socket that errors and a close all end in `lost()` → `scheduleReconnect()`, so a revoked token redials on the capped backoff forever and logs `handshake failed` each time; `BrokerReceiver` has `opened` and `gap` and nothing that says *stop and ask the person to relink*. The red assertion: a framing whose `handshake` rejects as a permanent refusal dials once, makes no second dial after the backoff, and calls the receiver once; today it dials again. Bun's `WebSocket` does not expose the upgrade response's status (a 401 arrives as `error` then `close`), so conduit cannot read a revoked token off the dial; only the framing, which reads the Provider's own answer in `handshake` or its close code, can. Prior art: Home Assistant's `ConfigEntryAuthFailed` stops retry for that entry and starts reauth, apart from `ConfigEntryNotReady` which retries; Discord's close codes 4004 and the like are `Reconnect=false`. `FJS-D1244` kept readiness off per-person sockets and says nothing about stopping.
  - **A** — the framing throws a typed refusal. Conduit exports `BrokerRefused` (an `Error` subclass); `handshake` throws it, and `dial()` may too (a ticket mint answered 401). `connect()` marks the subscription refused, stops redialing, and calls a new `BrokerReceiver.refused?(ctx: { reason: string })` once. One new noun and one new receiver option; the framing already owns the Provider's wire, so the classification sits where the Provider's answer is read, and a plain throw stays transient.
  - **B** — the framing classifies: a `BrokerFraming.refusal?(err, close)` that is asked on every failed handshake and every close (with the close code) and answers `true` for a permanent one. Reaches the close-code case (a Provider that opens, then closes 4004) without the handshake having to throw. Adds a framing method beside `handshake` and a second path to the same stop, and a connector that forgets it redials forever with nothing reporting it.
  - **C** — no conduit change: the receiver's `opened`/`gap` stay, and a mirror app that sees its handshake fail calls `app.conduit.unsubscribe(target)` itself. Adds nothing, but conduit has no `unsubscribe` and the app learns of a refusal only by a log line, so it needs the same signal as A to act.
  - **Recommend A** — the throw is how `handshake` already reports failure, so a typed error moves no connector that does not opt in, and the close-code case is a handshake the Provider answered by closing, which the framing can raise as the same error. B is the reopen path if a Provider refuses only after the handshake has been believed. Whichever stands, the stop leaves `health().connected` false and re-subscribing the target (a relinked person) is the restart, not a timer. Home Assistant and Discord both split a permanent auth refusal from a transient failure. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Home Assistant `ConfigEntryNotReady` retries with growing delay up to 10 minutes, while `ConfigEntryAuthFailed` stops retrying and starts a reauth flow ([docs](https://contributors.home-assistant.io/core/integrations/setup-failures/)). Discord marks gateway close codes 4004, 4010-4014 as Reconnect false, and 4004 is a bad token ([docs](https://docs.discord.com/developers/topics/opcodes-and-status-codes)). Both classify at the point that reads the provider's answer, and a plain failure stays transient. That matches a typed throw from the framing.

- **FJS-D1372 — Does Caravan hand a handler a child-process helper that kills its process group on abort, or stay signal-only and document that a handler that spawns owns its group?** `FJS-3081`: `cancel()` of a running job writes `cancelled` and aborts `ctx.signal`, but a handler that spawned `sh -c 'sleep 120 & sleep 120 & wait'` and did `child.kill('SIGTERM')` on abort loses the shell and leaks both grandchildren (reparented to init) with the row saying `cancelled`; only `spawn(..., { detached: true })` plus `process.kill(-pgid, 'SIGTERM')` empties the tree. Measured in the ci-runner stressor: group kill took 5–6ms same-instance and 288–511ms cross-instance (bound `pollInterval`), and cannot reach a child that called `setsid`.
  - **A** — signal-only, documented: `JobContext.signal` (`types.ts`) and the `FJS-1523` hazard in `packages/caravan/CLAUDE.md` say a signal reaches the handler and stops there, and that a handler that spawns owns its process group (`detached: true`, kill `-pgid` on abort, sweep on exit). No new surface; the helper stays an app concern, and Caravan stays free of `node:child_process`.
  - **B** — `ctx.spawn(cmd, args, opts)`: Caravan spawns in its own group, SIGTERMs `-pgid` on abort, SIGKILLs after a grace, sweeps on exit. Makes the row's word true for the common case, but adds a method to `JobContext`, a grace-period option, and a process supervisor to a queue; a `setsid` child still escapes, so the guarantee is partial.
  - **C** — an exported helper outside `ctx` (`spawnGroup(signal, cmd, args)`) that a handler opts into: same behavior as B with no `JobContext` change, documented beside A's hazard line. A second thing to learn, and still partial.
  - **Recommend A** — Caravan is a queue, not a process supervisor, and a helper cannot make "cancelled" mean "tree gone" anyway (`setsid`, containers need a cgroup); documenting the group-kill recipe moves no caller and adds no convention. B/C are the reopen path if a second app writes the same helper. BullMQ passes an `AbortSignal` and leaves cleanup to the handler, and Celery terminate signals only the task process. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — BullMQ `worker.cancelJob` aborts an `AbortSignal` handed to the processor, and the handler owns cleanup. It ships no child-process helper ([docs](https://docs.bullmq.io/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs)). Celery `revoke(terminate=True)` signals the worker child process running the task and warns against programmatic use. Its docs say nothing about the task's own subprocesses ([docs](https://docs.celeryq.dev/en/stable/userguide/workers.html)). Neither queue kills a handler's process tree, so signal-only with a documented recipe is the norm.

- **FJS-D1373 — What is conduit's outbound pace: an eighth `TargetResilience` number keyed by target or by credential, held or shed, counted in-process or shared?** `FJS-2286`: `max_concurrent` bounds calls in flight and sheds the excess as `overloaded` (`Resilience.admit`, [resilience.ts](packages/conduit/src/resilience.ts)); nothing bounds calls per minute, and a vendor's limit (DataForSEO, 2,000/min per account) is per CREDENTIAL, where a target-level number cannot say it. Red test: a target with `rate: { max: 5, per_ms: 1000 }` sent 12 calls at once through a stub transport starts at most 5 in the first second and all 12 succeed; today all 12 start at once. Two sub-questions ride on it. **Keyed by:** `Resilience` keys state by target id (or origin key, `FJS-D939`); a credential is `auth.ref`, or `{tenant, principal, grant}` for `per_caller` (`FJS-D848`), so the key must come from the send, not the descriptor. **Held or shed:** `FJS-685` ruled the concurrency cap sheds because a queue only moves the pile-up; a pace that sheds instead of waiting makes every caller write its own wait, which is the app code the row measured.
  - **A** — `resilience.rate: { max, per_ms }`, an eighth number that falls back field by field like the seven (`FJS-728`), keyed by target (and origin), process-local, a send HELD until a slot frees and bounded by the existing `deadline_ms`/`signal` (past it: `overloaded` with the wait stated). Cost: a per-credential vendor limit is not expressible, so serpgrid still needs its own pacer.
  - **B** — A, plus `rate.per: 'credential'`: the key becomes the target plus the resolved ref (and `who` for `per_caller`), each credential gets its own window. Fixes the measured case. Cost: one more field and the key is computed at send time, after admission today.
  - **C** — B with the window counted in the conduit store (`stores/sqlite.ts`), shared by every process on it. Fixes "a second process doubles the rate". Cost: a table written per send; `FJS-D1193` ruled rate-limit counters in-process for junction's limiter, and `FJS-D1021` is the same question for caravan, so C should wait for that ruling.
  - **Recommend B** — it is the only one that answers the measured per-credential limit; process-local matches `FJS-D1193`, with C the reopen path once `FJS-D1021` lands, and a held send (not a shed one) is what makes the number replace the app's pacer. Sidekiq Enterprise and Oban Pro both key the limit per credential and hold the work. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Sidekiq Enterprise limiters take a dynamic name such as `"stripe-#{user_id}"` for a per-credential limit, wait up to `wait_timeout`, then reschedule the job. They are shared across processes through Redis ([docs](https://github.com/sidekiq/sidekiq/wiki/Ent-Rate-Limiting)). Oban Pro `rate_limit` can partition by args keys such as `account_id` ([docs](https://oban.pro/docs/pro/Oban.Pro.Engines.Smart.html)) and leaves excess jobs queued *(inference)*. BullMQ 3.0 removed group-key limits and keeps one global limiter ([docs](https://docs.bullmq.io/guide/rate-limiting)). Both per-credential products hold rather than shed. Both count in a shared store, which is the C reopen path.

- **FJS-D1374 — What ends a parked receipt that will never resolve: who owns the clock, and what an expired park becomes?** `FJS-1806`: `land()` leaves a `parked` receipt unapplied (`applied_at: null`) and nothing reads it again, so a send whose response was lost parks its receipts forever and the text stays `failed` while the carrier delivered it. The red test is conduit-side: record a receipt, apply answers `parked`, advance the clock past the park's limit, and the receipt is still `applied_at: null` with no outcome (quo's pinned `telnyx.test.ts` gap is not in this checkout). Any fix adds a Receiver field and a state the store does not have.
  - **A** — `Receiver.parkFor?: number` (seconds) and a conduit-owned `sweep(now)` the plugin runs on its own interval: a receipt unapplied past `parkFor` becomes `lapsed` (a new `Receipt.lapsed_at`, one store method `lapse`), `apply` is called once more with `ev.lapsed` so the app can reconcile against the carrier, and a delivery of the same key after that is a duplicate. Conduit owns the clock; the app owns what a lapse means. Adds a field, a store column, an interval to the plugin.
  - **B** — `lapse?(ev, ctx)` on the Receiver instead of `parkFor` on a bare number: the receiver declares what to do (dead-letter, reconcile) and conduit calls it when a sweep finds a park older than a conduit-wide `parkWindow`. Same sweep and column as A; the expiry is conduit-wide, not per receiver, so Telnyx's minutes and a slower carrier's hours share one number.
  - **C** — no conduit change: `parked` stays a hold-and-redrive state, the app queries `conduit.unapplied(provider)` (a read, no clock) and schedules its own expiry on Caravan (quo's Q12). Smallest, but it is the unowned timer the row names, once per app.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D899` gave conduit the receipt and what a duplicate means, so a receipt that never ends is conduit's to end; a per-receiver number fits because carriers differ, and `lapsed` is a state, not a deletion, so the audit of what happened survives. C is the reopen path if a second app wants the expiry to be a job rather than a sweep. Temporal likewise has the server own the activity timeout clock and hand expiry to the workflow as an event. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Temporal does not trust a worker to report a lost activity. The Service enforces Start-To-Close, records `ActivityTaskTimedOut` and lets the workflow decide, with the timeout set per activity ([docs](https://docs.temporal.io/encyclopedia/detecting-activity-failures)). Stripe v1 prunes idempotency keys after 24 hours and says an unresolved outcome must be reconciled by the caller ([docs](https://docs.stripe.com/error-low-level)). The platform owns the clock and the app owns what an expiry means. That is A, with the number per receiver. The analogy is loose because neither has a `parked` receipt.

- ~~**FJS-D1375 — How does an app backup get asked for from a screen, run on a schedule, and restored without moving the archive by hand: does Outpost's restore root widen to the backup directory or a promote step copy across, and does the cron live on an `App` column or a `Job` row?**~~ **Answered 2026-11-09 (`FJS-D1521`): Built differently from A–D: the archive goes to a BackupDestination at take time and restoreVolume({ backupId }) hands Outpost a pre-signed GET (restoreSourceOf), so neither a second restore root nor a promote step exists; the cron is App.backupCron synced by job-schedule.ts (FJS-D1606); AppBackups.mesa has Back up now and Restore (FJS-4132).** `FJS-3179`: `app-backups.take` is on the API and `/mcp` only, `apps.restoreVolume` accepts a path only under `OUTPOST_RESTORE_DIR` (`FJS-D943`), and `take` records a path under `OUTPOST_BACKUP_DIR`. `/hub/backups` shows its own cron with the caveat that nothing registers it. The red test would assert: after `take` succeeds, `apps.restoreVolume({ backupId })` restores it; today `backupId` is refused as an undeclared key.
  - **A** — Screen and restore only, no schedule yet: an app-detail card lists `AppBackup` rows with Back up now and Restore (`restoreVolume` takes `backupId`, resolving `location` from the row; Outpost's `/volume/restore` accepts `OUTPOST_BACKUP_DIR` as a second root, `realpath`'d as the restore root is). Smallest; a schedule follows as its own row.
  - **B** — A, plus the clock on the App: `App.backupCron` (nullable) and a `backup:app` caravan job synced by the one rule `job-schedule.ts` uses, so an edit moves the clock (`FJS-328`). One more column on `App`, and a retention number is owed (`AppBackup` rows and archives otherwise grow without bound).
  - **C** — A, plus the clock as a `Job` row of a new kind `backup`: no schema column on `App`, the schedule screen exists, but `Job` is a command run on a machine and this one calls a service, so the kind needs its own dispatcher.
  - **D** — Outpost gains `POST /backup/promote { path }` copying an archive from the backup directory into the restore directory; the restore root stays single (`FJS-D943`'s confinement untouched). A second route and a second copy of a multi-GB file on the same disk.
  - **Recommend A, then B** (confidence 3) — one root widened by a second directory is the same `realpath` check twice rather than a new route, and a schedule without a restore button is a backup nobody has tried to use. Heroku restores by backup id, and Heroku and Coolify both keep the schedule and retention on the database itself.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Heroku restores by backup id (`pg:backups:restore b101 DATABASE_URL`), schedules per database attachment, and applies a retention policy ([docs](https://devcenter.heroku.com/articles/heroku-postgres-backups)). Coolify sets a cron expression on the database's own Backups page, with per-schedule retention by count, days and storage ([docs](https://coolify.io/docs/databases/backups)). Velero schedules take a cron and name each backup `<schedule>-<timestamp>` ([docs](https://velero.io/docs/main/backup-reference/)). None copies an archive between directories to restore it, which D would.

- **FJS-D1376 — What closes `FJS-053` (email-kit never opened in a real mail client): a human Outlook check, a rendering-matrix service, or a static conditional-comment lint with the gap documented?** `FJS-053` is not a code defect: 110/110 email-kit tests pass and assert the `<!--[if mso]>` / `<!--[if !mso]><!-->` shapes against the HTML string, but Outlook's Word renderer is the one thing a string assertion cannot stand in for, and a headless session has no mail client to open. No red test exists, so the row cannot close by a fix alone.
  - **A** — A person opens `WelcomeEmail` once in Outlook 2016/2019/365, Gmail and Apple Mail; the result goes in `docs/HTML_EMAIL_TRAPS.md` as a dated checklist, and the row closes on that note. Cost: one manual session; goes stale at the next template change.
  - **B** — A rendering-matrix service (Litmus / Email on Acid) driven from a `fli prove` drive. Needs a paid account, network and a secret in CI; the drive would be `skipped` without them.
  - **C** — Add a static lint to `email-kit.test.js` that every `<!--[if` has its `<![endif]-->` and nothing nests, across every template, and re-word the row as a standing caveat in `PROJECT_STATE.md` only (close the register row). Cheap and catches the FJS-928-class regressions, but never claims Outlook renders correctly.
  - **Recommend A, with C's lint** (confidence 3) — the unconfirmed claim is "renders in Outlook", which only a real client answers; the lint is a cheap regression guard in the meantime, and B's cost buys little for a kit with one template. Open-source email kits claim client support from a real-client or Litmus pass, not from a string check.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Cerberus says it is tested in the most popular email clients as reported by Litmus and by the author's own campaigns, and Litmus includes it in its Email Builder ([site](https://www.cerberusemail.com/), read through a search summary, not the README). The MJML README states no client matrix or test method ([repo](https://github.com/mjmlio/mjml)). The support claim in this space comes from a real-client pass, once, which is A, and a paid matrix in CI (B) was not found in either.

- **FJS-D1377 — What is an absent optional list with `@minItems(2)`, once the DDL stores it as `'[]'`, and which realm grades it?** Red test: `createClient({ schema: 'model Survey { id Int @id @default(autoincrement()) labels String[]? @minItems(2) }' }).survey.create({ data: {} })` returns `labels: []`, a list the schema refuses. `validate` (validate.js:796) grades only a list it was handed, and the DDL (`ddl.js:311,325`) makes every array column `NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'` — "empty array is the null state" — so `String[]?` has no absent state in storage. `FJS-3328` made junction's boundary leave the key absent, so below it the `[]` lands unchecked.
  - **A** — Litestone grades the fill: an absent key on an optional list with `@minItems > 0` is refused at create like a stated `[]`. Then `?` on such a list means nothing and `FJS-3328`'s "can be left out" is lost again (junction must put the `default: []` back).
  - **B** — `[]` is an optional list's *not stated* state: `@minItems` grades a non-empty list only, in `validate`, in `generateJsonSchema` (`minItems` stays, with `[]` allowed on an optional list) and in the boundary, so `@minItems(2)` on `String[]?` reads "none, or at least two" and the stored `[]` is honest. A required list keeps the full rule.
  - **C** — A real NULL: an optional list column becomes nullable in the DDL (migration for existing columns) and a reader meets `null`. Matches `?` exactly, moves every array reader and the `json_insert(coalesce(…))` ops (client.js:3209).
  - **Recommend B** — it matches the DDL's stated null state and `FJS-3328`'s outcome, moves one rule and no column, and A or C stay the reopen path. Django skips validators on an empty value, so `[]` on an optional list passes. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Django's `Field.run_validators` returns early when the value is in `empty_values`, so a blank list skips a length validator ([source](https://github.com/django/django/blob/main/django/db/models/fields/__init__.py)). Prisma's list docs steer away from NULL arrays, since a NULL array escapes the `NOT` and `isEmpty` filters, and suggest defaulting to `[]` ([docs](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/prisma-client/special-fields-and-types/working-with-scalar-lists-arrays)). Both treat the empty list as the not-stated state, which is B, and shy from C.

- **FJS-D1378 — How does `payments.start` stop its losing concurrent request leaving an unrecorded intent at the provider?** Red test: two parallel `start` calls on one order against the sink — the sink holds two intents (`pi_…`) while `Payment` names one; the loser's `createIntent` ran before its insert was refused (`FJS-3398`'s unique on open rows), and nothing ever pays or reconciles it. The row says the fix is claiming before the call, which `providerRef @unique` + `@length(3, 64)` and `start`'s own "provider FIRST" comment forbid today.
  - **A** — Claim first: `providerRef` becomes nullable (single-column `@unique` over NULL is legal), `start` inserts `pending` with no ref, then calls the provider and sets the ref; a failed call marks the claim `failed`. Costs the schema change, a `pending` row with no provider behind it, and reverses `start`'s documented order.
  - **B** — Collapse at the provider: `createIntent` states an `idempotency_key` of order reference + count of the order's earlier payments, so concurrent starts get the SAME intent back and the loser's insert names the winner's `providerRef`; a decline raises the count and a fresh intent follows. The sink already honors `Idempotency-Key` for refunds. Reverses `createIntent`'s "a key would be the wrong way" comment.
  - **C** — Leave it and say so: an unconfirmed intent moves no money and a real provider expires it; close the row as accepted, add the sentence to `start`'s comment. Costs the "never reconciled" tidiness only.
  - **Recommend B** — it closes the orphan with no schema column and no row that lies about a payment existing, and the key's attempt-count part keeps the retry-after-decline case the current comment guards. Stripe itself advises deriving the key from a cart ID, and a real provider may answer the loser 409, which the loser retries with the same key. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Stripe v1 caches the result of a keyed POST, errors included, so a retry with the same key replays a decline ([docs](https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests)). It suggests deriving the key from a user-attached object such as a shopping cart ID. A concurrent request with the same key gets a 409, and Stripe advises waiting and retrying with that key ([docs](https://docs.stripe.com/error-low-level)). That is B, including the attempt count, because a bare order key would replay the decline forever. Only the 409 differs from the sink.

- **FJS-D1429 — What text form do `array`, `pipe`, `let` and `match` take in the shared flow grammar, and does `match` stay in the `Expression` union when the resolver has no case for it?** Found fixing `FJS-2136`: `emit.ts` refuses all four by name and `text.ts` parses none, so a stored one shows as a document (6 pipes, 5 arrays, 3 lets in the repo's flows). The text is syntax in `@frontierjs/toolbelt/predicate` (`FJS-D271`), which `FJS-D513` grew for `template` and `object` in the flow dialect only. Choices: `[a, b]` already parses as a literal-only `list` (the right side of `in`, empty refused), so an array of expressions must not change what a list means; a pipe's step omits its first operand (`lower` is `lower($)`), which no existing syntax says; and `ExpressionResolver.resolve` (`packages/orion/src/engine/expression/index.ts`) has no `match` case, so a stored `match` resolves to `undefined` today.
  - **A** — Grow all four in the flow dialect: array `[e, ...]` read as an array when any member is not a literal (a list otherwise), pipe `a |> f(b) |> map(x => ...)`, let `let a = e, b = e in body`, match `match v { when e => e, else => e }`; a `.lite` policy refuses each by name as it does `template`. Also adds the missing resolver case for `match`.
  - **B** — Grow `array`, `pipe` and `let` (the forms the repo's flows use) and drop `match` from the union and from `PipeStep`'s neighbors, since nothing runs it; a `match` is a `cond` chain until a flow needs one.
  - **C** — Accept: these stay documents, the inspector keeps showing JSON, and `FJS-2136` closes as won't-fix. Keeps the shared grammar to predicates plus the two assembling forms.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 3) — the three forms are in real flows and the fourth is a stored shape no engine path executes, so syntax for it would be a grammar nobody can test against behavior. A is the reopen path if a flow needs a `match`. Mature expression languages (CEL, JSONata) carry list, binding or chain forms and none carries a `match`.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — CEL's spec allows list literals of any expressions (`[e1, e2, ..., eN]`), has no pipe and no match, and its only multi-way choice is the ternary ([spec](https://github.com/google/cel-spec/blob/master/doc/langdef.md)). JSONata has `:=` variable binding in blocks and the `~>` chain operator, and its conditionals are the ternary, with no match ([docs](https://docs.jsonata.org/programming)). Binding, chaining and arrays are common, a `match` is not.

- **FJS-D1430 — A job dispatched under a bearer grant: does it run as the app's system with the grant named in the trail, or re-enter the grant and run at its standing?** `FJS-3076`: `dispatch()` records `host.principal()?.id` (`caravan/src/index.ts` actorId), a grant has no id, so the row's `actor_id` is null and `worker.ts` runs it `runAs(null, { tenant })` — the app's system principal. The red test's assertion: a job dispatched inside a call that resolved `bearerClaim(...)` writes a row whose audit `actorType` is `'service'`/`'system'` where the direct path writes `'bearer'`, `actorId` the grant's id, `subjectId` its subject (`FJS-D342`). The grant lives in `ctx.locals[BEARER]` (`core/bearer.ts`), which `installLogContext` reads as `bearerId`/`bearerSubject`; nothing in the jobs row, `runAs` or `RequestMeta` carries it.
  - **A** — Attribution only: a host getter reads the call's grant, the jobs row gains `bearer_id` and `bearer_subject`, the worker passes `runAs(null, { tenant, bearer })`, `RequestMeta` carries it and `installLogContext` falls back to it. The trail matches the direct path; the job still runs with the app's full standing, which `FJS-D69` rejected by name ("SYSTEM by default"), and a revoked key's queued work still runs.
  - **B** — Re-enter the grant: the row records the grant's model and id; the worker re-reads that row at run time, a revoked or expired grant fails the job permanently (the `PRINCIPAL_MISSING` path and `onMissingActor`), and the handler runs at the grant's standing (STRANGER plus the claims `bearerClaim` states) with the trail naming it. Faithful to `FJS-D69` — revocation reaches queued work — and it needs `bearer.ts` to expose a re-resolve that runs without a request, and handlers that wrote past the grant's row policy as system are refused.
  - **C** — Carry it as data: `JobContext` gains the grant (`ctx.bearer`) and the app writes the key into its own rows; the trail keeps naming `system`. Smallest, and leaves the stolen-key search `FJS-3076` names to every app.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — A files the right name over the wrong authority, which is the `FJS-D69` hazard exactly and for exactly the caller a public key is; A is B's first half (the row records the grant either way), so it is the reopen path if re-entry proves too heavy. Precedent splits: Rails re-resolves at run time, Hasura snapshots; `FJS-D69` decides it.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Rails ActiveJob serializes a record as a GlobalID and re-fetches it at run time, raising `ActiveJob::DeserializationError` if it was deleted ([guide](https://guides.rubyonrails.org/active_job_basics.html)). That is B's shape. Hasura event triggers carry a snapshot of `session_variables`, including `x-hasura-role`, in the payload with no re-check at delivery ([payload docs](https://hasura.io/docs/2.0/event-triggers/payload/)). That is A's shape. Neither runs the job at the grant's standing.

- **FJS-D1431 — Does a custom method on a row-tenant-scoped service get a per-method opt-out of the tenant guard, or is a platform-wide operation a model-less service (`FJS-D49`)?** `FJS-3091`: under `tenancy { strategy row }`, `tenantClaimGuard` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`, mounted `before: all` in `core/app.ts`) refuses every call on a scoped service when the request names no tenant, a custom method included, so an administrator's `POST /api/payouts` with `x-service-method: run` and no `X-Shop-Id` answers 400 though `run` reads nothing through the caller. The red test: a custom method on a row-scoped service called by a signed-in principal carrying no tenant claim answers 200 where it answers 400 today. `FJS-D49` ruled a tier above every tenant is a SEPARATE service, and rejected widening existing services by a caller-chosen switch; it did not consider a switch the schema's author declares on the method. `scope:` already names an API-key scope on a method (`FJS-D916`).
  - **A** — Keep `FJS-D49`: the platform-wide operation is a model-less service (`model: null`), which is what the etsy stressor's `platform.service.ts` already is. Close `FJS-3091` as accepted, add an `api-hazards` entry saying a method across tenants lives on its own service. The administrator is graded by hand there, since a model-less service has no gate floor.
  - **B** — A boolean on the custom method, `tenant: false`, read by `tenantClaimGuard` to skip the refusal for that method only. Never on a CRUD verb, so every read stays one tenant's, and the method's model read-gate floor (`FJS-826`) still grades the caller. `manifest()` and `svc.describe()` carry it, so the methods spanning tenants are listable. The method reads through `asSystem()` since a caller-scoped client with no claim matches no row.
  - **C** — `scope: 'platform'` on the method, as the row words it. One word with two owners: `scope:` is already the API-key scope a key must hold (`FJS-D916`), so a method naming it would be read as a key's standing rather than a tenancy exemption.
  - **Recommend B** (confidence 2) — the caller cannot widen anything (the author states it on the method, greppable like `FJS-D49`'s hub service), the gate floor grades the administrator instead of a hand-written check, and C collides with `FJS-D916`; A is the reopen path if a second method wants it and the extra service per operation proves the lesser cost. No outside analogue declares it on a method: acts_as_tenant exempts by author-written call site, which B matches.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — acts_as_tenant has no per-action opt-out. Cross-tenant code is wrapped by its author in `ActsAsTenant.without_tenant`, and `require_tenant` can exempt a path or a model, never a caller ([README](https://github.com/ErwinM/acts_as_tenant)). In both cases the exemption is written by the developer in code, which is B's property, and none of them is a separate service. Nothing else read declares the flag on a method.

- **FJS-D1432 — Does the webhooks plugin deliver through `app.conduit` when it is present (bare fetch otherwise), or keep its own fetch and gain only a metrics line?** `FJS-2924`: `attemptDelivery` (`packages/junction/src/plugins/webhooks/index.ts`) grades with `gradePublicUrl`, pins the address, fetches with `redirect: 'manual'` and a 10s `AbortSignal`, and records the result on its own `deliveries` row and six-rung ladder. No breaker per origin, no observer, no `/metrics` line, where conduit's `address_from: 'request'` target does all three per send. Junction may not import Conduit (Invariant 1), so a conduit path is a structural member on `AppConduit` (`FJS-D219`'s shape), and the ladder is the unsettled part: conduit's own `retry_limit` would multiply the plugin's six attempts.
