  - **A** — Structural send: `AppConduit` gains the one member `attemptDelivery` calls; with conduit present the plugin sends through an `address_from: 'request'` target with `retry_limit: 0`, so the plugin's persisted ladder stays the only retry and conduit adds breaker, observer and metrics. Without conduit the bare fetch stays, as IMail's default does. Two paths to keep equal; signing stays `signRequest` with the `X-Webhook` prefix on both.
  - **B** — Keep the fetch and close the gap in the plugin: `app.registerMetricsSource('webhooks', …)` over the deliveries table, and a per-origin breaker the plugin keeps itself. One path, but it is a second breaker beside the package that owns the ring, which is what the row objects to.
  - **C** — Conduit required for delivery: the plugin refuses to register without `app.conduit` and drops its fetch. One path and no ladder doubling to reason about, but a webhooks-only app must install a package it never configures, and every webhooks test needs a conduit.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the row's own candidate and the only option where the ring's breaker, observer and metrics reach the framework's one shipped outbound integration without making the package mandatory; `retry_limit: 0` settles the ladder question in one number. The bare-fetch path logs once at register so a missing ring is not silent, and a test holds the two paths to the same redirect, address and signature refusals. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Svix, a hosted webhook sender, owns its whole delivery stack. It uses an 8-attempt schedule (immediate, 5s, 5m, 30m, 2h, 5h, 10h twice), a 30s success window, and disables an endpoint after 5 days of failures ([docs](https://docs.svix.com/retries)). It has no pluggable outbound client, so it does not test the question of delegating one hop to a shared client. No other webhook sender was found that delegates. This is internal to FrontierJS, and the sibling `FJS-D219` shape decides it.

- **FJS-D1433 — Where does `@phone`'s national-number metadata live, and how much of a region's rules does it carry?** `FJS-D874` = B rules `@phone(region: "US")` reads `(555) 123-4567` as `+15551234567`, which needs region → calling code, the trunk prefix to drop (`0` in the UK, `1` in the US) and some notion of a valid length; `@frontierjs/toolbelt` has zero dependencies (`FJS-D26`) and holds none of it. The `+` half (strip separators, require 8–15 digits, store `+<digits>`) needs no metadata. Red test: `create` with `'(555) 123-4567'` then `'+15551234567'` under `@unique` — the second must be refused as a duplicate; `'-------'` and `'+0000000'` must be refused as malformed.
  - **A** — A hand table in toolbelt: region → `{ code, trunk, lengths }` for a stated set of regions (the ~20 an app is likely to name), an unlisted region refused at parse by name. Zero deps kept and the diff stays reviewable; a number valid by a region's real plan but outside the stated lengths is refused, and adding a region is an edit here.
  - **B** — `libphonenumber-js` (its `min` metadata) as toolbelt's first dependency. Every region and real per-region validity for free; toolbelt stops being zero-dep, the transform runs in the browser bundle (~80 KB), and `FJS-D26`'s import license no longer comes free.
  - **C** — A hand table of region → `{ code, trunk }` only (all ISO regions, ~250 rows of two fields) with the one E.164 length bound (8–15 digits total) for validity. Every region nameable, no per-region plan; accepts a number a region's real plan would refuse, which a carrier then catches, as before this row.
  - **D** — Ship the `+` half now (normalize, refuse `'-------'`) and rule `region:` off until an app needs it; a national number is refused by a message naming the missing `+`. Closes the unique-per-spelling hole for every app that collects E.164; leaves `FJS-D874`'s `region:` unbuilt.
  - **Recommend C** — it keeps toolbelt zero-dep (`FJS-D26`) and names every region, and the only validity it forgoes is the carrier's own; if an app shows a number C admits that matters, A's per-region lengths grow out of C's rows without changing what a field means.

- **FJS-D1434 — Does the derived instant keep the word `@zoned` beside `FJS-D512`'s reader, and what does a nonexistent or repeated wall clock resolve to?** Found building `FJS-3381` (`FJS-D963` = A, `startsAt DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)`). Today `@zoned(path)` is legal on a `String @date`/`@time` only and says "this wall clock is READ in the zone at path" (`parser.js` `parseZoned`, validated near `parser.js` 8049, emitted as `x-zoned`, read by `due()` per `commitment.js`); `parseZoned` takes ONE path and the validator refuses `@zoned` on anything that is not a `String @date`/`@time`. The ruled form is a three-argument attribute on a `DateTime` that WRITES the column. Red test: a schema with `startsAt DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)` parses; a create with `date '2026-03-08'`, `startLocal '02:30'` under `America/New_York` stores the `compatible` instant (`2026-03-08T07:30:00.000Z`, the gap resolved to the later side); an update of `site.timeZone` re-writes the children's `startsAt` in the same transaction; an app write of `startsAt` is refused by name. Toolbelt's `fromWall(fields, zone, { disambiguation })` already answers Temporal's four modes, so the instant itself is not owed.
  - **A** — One word, one meaning: `@zoned`'s LAST argument is always the zone path, and the field's type says the rest. `String @date @zoned(site.timeZone)` is read in that zone; `DateTime @zoned(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)` is derived from two columns and that zone. The parser dispatches on argument count, the validator on the field's type, and the catalog gains the second form under the one `field:zoned` row. Matches `FJS-D963` as ruled and needs no rename; the cost is that one attribute does two jobs (a reader that annotates, a writer that stores) and a one-argument `@zoned(site.timeZone)` on a `DateTime` is refused by name rather than meaning anything.
  - **B** — Keep `@zoned(path)` for the reader alone and spell the derivation with its own word: `startsAt DateTime @instant(date, startLocal, site.timeZone)`. Two words for two jobs, so knowing one predicts nothing false about the other, and `@zoned`'s validator and `x-zoned` stay as they are. Overrides `FJS-D963`'s spelling and adds a `.lite` word (catalog row, tier, axis) and a doc page.
  - **C** — Rename the reader, keep the ruled spelling for the writer: `endsOn String @date @inZone(timeZone)` and `startsAt DateTime @zoned(...)`. Every `@zoned(path)` in the tree (`FJS-D512`, `x-zoned`, `commitment.js`, the example and prototype schemas) moves in one change with no alias; the one `@zoned` then means *derived from a zone*, at the price of the largest churn and a second ruling reopened.
  - **Disambiguation, whichever of A–C** — a wall clock in a spring-forward gap resolves with `fromWall`'s `compatible` (the later side: `02:30` on the gap day becomes `03:30`) and a repeated hour with its earlier instant, fixed in the attribute with no option; a caller that must refuse a gap states it as a `@check` over the wall clock. **Alternative** — `reject`, a nonexistent time refused at write by name: stricter (a shift booked into the gap is a typo or a stale form) but a zone write then refuses a child whose stored clock became nonexistent under the NEW zone, which blocks a legitimate correction.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D963` already spelled it and the last-argument-is-the-zone reading is one sentence, so the three-argument form is the same declaration (*this column is tied to the zone at the end of its path*) rather than a second one; with `compatible` fixed, because a zone change cannot be allowed to fail a child write. Temporal, `java.time` and RFC 5545 all resolve a gap to the later side and an overlap to the earlier. Confidence 3 — the disambiguation has precedent, and B is the stronger name if a reader meets both forms in one model and misreads which one writes.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Temporal's default `compatible` mode takes the later side of a gap and the earlier of an overlap ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Global_Objects/Temporal/ZonedDateTime)). `java.time` shifts a gap time forward by the gap and keeps the earlier offset in an overlap ([ZonedDateTime](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/api/java.base/java/time/ZonedDateTime.html)). RFC 5545 reads a gap time with the offset before the gap and an overlap as the first occurrence ([§3.3.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5545#section-3.3.5)). All three give `02:30` on the gap day as `03:30` local. No outside analogue exists for the `@zoned` spelling.

- **FJS-D1435 — How does `claimIdempotency` learn the bearer grant's id, when the resolver that parks it runs inside the data hook, after the claim?** Found fixing `FJS-2151`. `claimIdempotency` runs in `callService` ahead of the pipeline (`FJS-D451`: a replay runs no hook), and `principalOf` reads `ctx.user?.id`. A `bearerClaim` caller has `ctx.user === null`; the grant row is parked at `ctx.locals[BEARER].id` by the resolver, which `withLitestoneDb` runs through `applyClaims` inside the around hook (`core/litestone.ts` `resolveAppClaims`) — so at claim time `ctx.locals[BEARER]` is empty. Red test: a service behind `principal: bearerClaim({ from: header('x-api-key'), ... })`, one POST twice under one `Idempotency-Key` with a live key — the second must replay the first's answer and the method must have run once; today it runs twice and the server warns *no principal*.
  - **A** — Resolve ahead of the claim: when a key arrives, the caller has no `ctx.user` and the app states a `principal` (held under `PRINCIPAL_RESOLVER`), `callService` runs the resolver first and keys on `locals[BEARER].id`. The grant id is the namespace `FJS-D342` already names. Cost: the resolver runs twice on a keyed, userless write (a read plus the windowed `lastUsedAt` stamp), and a dead key is refused 401 before the claim, the same answer the hook gives.
  - **B** — Key on a digest of the credential as presented (the `bearerClaim`'s `from` header value), read in the transport before any resolver. No double resolve and no new ordering; but the key is the secret's digest rather than the grant id, so a rotated key starts a fresh namespace, and the transport must learn each resolver's `from` — a second owner of *how a request names its grant*.
  - **C** — Move the claim inside the pipeline, after the data hook. The grant is resolved once and any principal works; but a replay then runs the hooks before it answers, which `FJS-D451` rules out, and the gate-skipping price `FJS-D1309` ruled final changes shape.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the row's own wanted shape (scope to the grant id) and `FJS-D451` stays whole; the second read is bounded to keyed, userless writes, which are rare next to every other call.

- **FJS-D1436 — How does a `header()` or `cookie()` bearer's socket get graded and revoked: what carries its token on the upgrade, who asks the app's `principal` for a recipient, and what closes a revoked grant's socket?** Found fixing `FJS-1955`. Three seams, one per symptom. (1) A browser sets no header on an upgrade, and the subprotocol slot `FJS-D486` gave the session is read whole by `extractToken` (`http.ts` ~1891) as a session, so a grant rides `/ws?visitor=<token>` in the URL. (2) A broadcast has no frame, so `bearerClaim` (which reads `ctx.headers`/`meta.headers` through `requestHeaders`, `bearer.ts:53`) is never asked for a recipient: `gradeRecipients` (`channels.ts:591`) grades `conn.user == null` as a stranger unless the app hand-writes `channels({ claims })`, three apps now (chatwoot `visitor.ts`, maidtech `socketClaims`) copying `bearerClaim`'s lookup and `isLive` rule. (3) `disconnectSession` (`channels.ts:1183`) matches `conn.user?.sessionId`, and a bearer's `conn.user` is null; `reverifySeconds` re-asks only a session credential (`http.ts` `_wsReverify`). Red test, if it is wanted as a test: a `channels()` app with `principal: bearerClaim({ from: header('x-visitor') ... })`, a socket upgraded with the grant, a `messages` row the grant's policy admits, and the broadcast arrives; today it is refused for want of a claim, and revoking the grant row leaves the socket open.
  - **A** — The framework asks the resolver. `channels()` with no `claims` option defaults to running the app's `principal` list (the `PRINCIPAL_RESOLVER` held on the app) over a synthetic context built from the upgrade's `conn.data.headers`, per frame per principal as `FJS-D472` already asks `claims`, and its `PrincipalClaims` answer is the claim set. A hand-written `claims` still wins. The token for a browser rides a new subprotocol entry, `fjs.grant.<token>`, that `extractToken` does NOT read as a session and that the socket's headers expose to the resolver under the bearer source's declared `headerName` (the source already declares it for CORS and the frame allow-list). A revoked grant is closed by the same per-frame ask: a resolver that answers 401 for a dead grant ends the socket (`4001`), and `reverifySeconds` re-asks the principal for a grant-only socket as it does a session. One liveness rule, owned by `bearerClaim`; `disconnectSession` stays session-only.
  - **B** — Keep the grading app-written but export the pieces. Export `ChannelClaimsFn` (done, `FJS-2535`) and add `bearerClaim(...).socketClaims()`, returning a ready `ChannelClaimsFn` that reads the grant by digest per frame with `isLive`, plus `app.channels.disconnectBearer(model, grantId)` that closes sockets whose last resolved grant is that row. The token still rides an app-chosen carrier (query string, or a `fjs.grant.` subprotocol the app reads in `onConnect`). Smaller blast radius and no new default, but every app still installs two calls and the carrier stays unspecified, so the next stressor repeats the chatwoot `/ws?visitor=` URL.
  - **C** — A first-frame auth. The socket upgrades anonymous and the client's first frame is `{ type: 'auth', credential }`, resolved by the same `principal` list a call frame uses, with the result parked on the connection as `conn.user` for a session or `conn.grant` for a bearer. One carrier for both kinds of credential and nothing in the URL or the subprotocol (`IDEAS/pen-testing.md` option C); a revoked grant closes at the next frame or `reverifySeconds`. It moves the handshake, so `FJS-D486`'s subprotocol stays only as the session fast path, and a frame before auth is a stranger's.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — it is the row's own wanted shape ("the request's resolver asked for a recipient by the framework") and Invariant 4's one owner for the lookup and liveness rule: `bearerClaim` already knows how to read a grant and say it is dead, and A calls it rather than copying it. The subprotocol carrier follows `FJS-D486`'s reasoning that a credential never rides the URL, and is the smallest widening of it; Kubernetes uses the same carrier for browser tokens. The browser client half (`client/index.ts` `_connect` opening at a fixed `/ws` with only the session) rides the same carrier: a `grant` option naming the token, offered as `fjs.grant.<token>`. Add Phoenix's explicit disconnect-by-id if per-frame re-asking is too slow to revoke.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Kubernetes authenticates browser WebSockets with a token in a subprotocol, `base64url.bearer.authorization.k8s.io.<token>`, because browsers cannot set headers on an upgrade ([PR #47740](https://github.com/kubernetes/kubernetes/pull/47740)). That is A's carrier. Phoenix closes a user's sockets by broadcasting `"disconnect"` to the socket's `id/1` topic ([Phoenix.Socket](https://phoenix.hexdocs.pm/Phoenix.Socket.html)), the push form of revocation B's `disconnectBearer` sketches. Ably notes subprotocol tokens may reach server logs *(secondary source)* ([guide](https://ably.com/blog/websocket-authentication)).

- **FJS-D1437 — Who turns a written method's null single-row write into a refusal?** The served `@@transitions` moves are fixed (`declaredTransition` asks the caller's own read: 404 for a hidden row, 403 for a readable one, `test/declared-moves.test.ts`). A WRITTEN method is not: `return $.db.order.transition($.id, 'ship')` or `$.db.app.update({ where: { id }, data: { port }, system: ['port'] })` on a row the caller cannot read, or can read and not update, gets litestone's `null` and answers 200 with `null`; the second sighting lost a write (App.port never stored) while reporting success. Red test: a service with a written `ship()` returning `$.db.todo.transition($.id, 'complete')`, called as `stranger` (expects 404) and as `staff` (expects 403); both answer 200/null today. `test/declared-moves.test.ts` § *a written method that lifts it with { system: true }* pins "a written method answers the null as it likes" for the stranger, and `FJS-3129` pins a bare `null` at the Data tier.
  - **A** — Junction, in the call: a single-row `update`/`transition` on `$.db` that answers `null` marks the call; if the method then returns `null`, the envelope runs the same `missOrRefused` read the CRUD verbs use (404 hidden, 403 readable). One owner for the translation (Invariant 4), the Data contract unchanged. Cost: a heuristic on the method's return value; a method that swallows the null and returns something else is not caught.
  - **B** — Litestone: `update`/`transition` by id throws `NOT_PERMITTED` when the caller can read the row and the write matched nothing, and stays `null` for a hidden or missing row (the FJS-1093 oracle argument holds only there). Every caller, written method and script alike, gets the refusal with no junction rule. Cost: `update` becomes a second read on the miss path and `FJS-3129`'s "bare null at the Data tier" is reversed; `missOrRefused` in junction then reduces to the 404 case.
  - **C** — Nobody: the written method's author tests the result (`?? throw new NotFound`), and AGENTS.md § Services says so beside the `transition` example. Cost: the writer who followed the documented shape was the one bitten, twice.
  - **Recommend B** — the null is ambiguous at the one place that can disambiguate cheaply, and that is the owner of the policy; A leaves `$.db` calls outside a method (jobs, hooks) still silent. Confidence 2: Prisma's single-row `update` also throws on a miss, but no outside product splits hidden from forbidden, so that half is FrontierJS's own.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Prisma's `update` throws `PrismaClientKnownRequestError` P2025 when the record does not exist, instead of returning `null`, and `updateMany` returns a count ([reference](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/reference/prisma-client-reference)). That supports a refusal at the data tier, as B has it. PostgREST's zero-row PATCH behavior was not confirmed from its docs. No product read separates a hidden row (404) from a readable but forbidden one (403) at this layer.

- **FJS-D1439 — How does a client save a parent with its edited children as one atomic write: nested-write ops carried by `save()`, a transactional batch of calls, or an app-written service method?** Found fixing `FJS-2742`. `_changed` drops every composed key on a patch (`FJS-1576`), so `resource.save({ id, lines: [...] })` sends the document and loses the lines; ela's editor then makes N+1 calls (remove, patch, create lines, patch document) and can stop half-written. Litestone already writes a nested `create`/`connect` in one transaction (`FJS-638`), so the missing piece is the client verb and the wire shape, not atomicity at the Data boundary. The red test's assertion: after `resource.save({ id, lines: [edited, added], … })` over a parent whose baseline holds lines, ONE request leaves, and a refusal on any line leaves the parent unwritten.
  - **A** — `save()` diffs a child list against the baseline and sends nested-write ops (`lines: { create, update, delete }`) in the parent's own patch; one call, one litestone transaction, each child still graded by its own gate. Needs litestone's nested `update`/`delete` ops on a relation, which `FJS-3002`'s batching ruling (`FJS-D1106`) also touches.
  - **B** — A transport-level batch, `client.batch([call, call, …])` (and `resource.batch`), run in one `$transaction` server-side; every call keeps its full service pipeline, so the app orders writes and nothing is nested in a payload. Coins a noun and a route, and a call's result may feed the next (a created id), which the shape must answer.
  - **C** — Neither: the app declares a custom service method (`document.saveWithLines`) that opens `$transaction` and runs the writes; the framework adds a `resource.call` that carries it. Cheapest; every app re-writes the validate-then-order-then-write loop ela already wrote.
  - **Recommend A** — a child list is already a composed key the schema knows, so the diff has a baseline to compare against and the gate stays per-row. B is the larger noun and C leaves the half-written case to each app. Rails does A in core, and JSON:API's Atomic Operations extension is the standard form of B. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Rails `accepts_nested_attributes_for` takes child hashes that create (no `id`), update (matching `id`) or destroy (`_destroy`), all saved atomically inside the parent's save transaction ([docs](https://api.rubyonrails.org/classes/ActiveRecord/NestedAttributes/ClassMethods.html)). Prisma documents a nested update of a user and its posts in one call ([reference](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/reference/prisma-client-reference)). JSON:API's Atomic Operations extension is the batch form, with `lid` to reference earlier creates ([spec](https://jsonapi.org/ext/atomic/)).

- **FJS-D1440 — How does a URL-state list say 'no filter' when it started on filters — what spelling does an explicitly empty query have in the address?** `tethered({ query })` or a resource's `tetheredQuery.query` is what the list reads while the URL carries no filter (`tethered.js` `currentQuery()`, tested in `resource-tethered.test.js` "yield to the URL's"), and `buildUrl` drops an empty bag, so `list.apply({})` writes the address the list was already on. Red test: `tethered({ query: { completedAt: { equals: null } } })` on `/tasks/`, then `list.apply({})` — the load after it still carries `completedAt[equals]=null`; it should carry no filter and `list.query` should be `{}`. The address has no way to say "the filters were cleared on purpose", and that is the missing noun.
  - **A** — A directive `$filter=none` in the `@frontierjs/toolbelt/directives` table (Invariant 10's one place), written by `commit` when the next query is empty and the start is not, read by `currentQuery()` as `{}`, and never sent to the server. The default view keeps its clean address and a shared link of the cleared view says so. Cost: one new directive that is list state, not transport.
  - **B** — The list writes its start filters into the address on first load (a `replace`), so the URL always holds the whole filter state and an empty bag means empty. Nothing new in the table; `currentQuery()` loses its fallback. Cost: every default view's address grows filters, a link shared before a default changes keeps the old one, and `tetheredQuery` stops being "what a list starts on beneath the URL".
  - **C** — No new spelling: a start filter is the view's default and stays; "no filter" is stated as a neutral filter the app picks, or the filter moves out of `query` into app state. Document it in `AGENTS.md`, close the row as designed. Cost: the `{ id: { gte: 1 } }` workaround becomes the documented way.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2, precedent splits)** — it is the only option that keeps the URL as the state and the default view's address clean, and `$`-syntax belongs in the toolbelt table already. Separately of the answer, the row's second half is a doc gap: `list.reload()` is the re-read `list.apply(list.query, list.directives)` was reaching for, and `AGENTS.md` does not name it.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — nuqs drops a value equal to its default from the URL (`clearOnDefault`, default `true`), and setting `null` removes the key, so cleared and default collapse. `clearOnDefault: false` keeps the value in the URL, which is `B` ([docs](https://nuqs.dev/docs/options)). GitHub's Issues page starts on `is:issue is:open` and clearing the search box gives an empty `q=`, an explicit empty marker *(inference from user reports)* ([search](https://docs.github.com/en/search-github/searching-on-github/searching-issues-and-pull-requests)). Precedent splits between `A` and `B`.

- **FJS-D1441 — Does `columns()` omit a column a create policy fills with `auth().id` (`x-determined`), as it omits the tenancy stamp, or only when the read is owner-scoped?** `columnList` (`field-rules.js`) omits `x-litestone-kind: tenancy` because the boundary scoped the read, so the value repeats in every row; `x-determined` is the CREATE policy and says nothing of the read. A scaffold over `ownerId String @default(auth().id)` with an owner-only read draws Owner as the reader's own uuid in every row, with an Owner select in the filter bar (`filters()` ranks over the same list); an admin table over the same model sees many owners and wants the column. Red test: `columnList` over a schema carrying `x-determined: { ownerId: 'auth().id' }` returns `ownerId` in `columns` today.
  - **A** — Omit every determined column from the default ranking, reason in `omitted`, `only` names it back — the tenancy rule verbatim, one filter line beside it. Cost: an operator's table (`admin:generate`) loses Owner unless its generator passes `only`.
  - **B** — Omit only where the read is scoped to the caller: litestone emits what the read policy pins (a read-side twin of `x-determined`), `columnList` omits where it agrees with the create claim. Exact for both tables; cost: a new schema keyword and a policy analysis, which a plain `@@allow read all` model never has.
  - **C** — Leave `columns()` alone and let the scaffold pass `except` for the determined column: the generated page of a person's model drops it, the ranking stays a pure read of the read schema. Cost: every caller of `columns()` repeats the rule.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — it is the tenancy precedent one line wide, `omitted` names the reason so nothing vanishes silently, and `only` is the already-documented way back for the operator's table; the autoincrement `Id` and its range filter are a separate question and stay out of this ruling. No outside analogue for the read-side half.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Hasura column presets fill a column from a session variable and remove it from what that role may insert or update, with no word on whether the column is shown on read ([docs](https://hasura.io/docs/latest/auth/authorization/permissions/column-presets/)). I found no tool that omits a determined column from a generated table's default columns *(inference)*. This is FrontierJS's own scaffold question, so the tenancy rule is the only precedent.

- **FJS-D1443 — Does Harbor's port carry app-level messages, or is the port Junction-only and the docs say so?** `routeMessage` (`packages/jetty/src/define/harbor.js`) handles `init`, `service:call` and `channel:*`; its `default` branch only logs. An extension with no Junction behind it (intake) reaches the harbor over `runtime.sendMessage` with its own `onMessage`, so the port has no door for it. The red test would post `{ type: 'app:ping' }` on a port and expect a reply.
  - **A** — `defineHarbor({ onMessage(msg, { port, respond }) })`: the `default` branch hands an unknown type to the app hook and answers its return value under `response:<_requestId>`.
  - **B** — the port is framework-only. Docs say so, and say that an app message arrives by `chrome.runtime.onMessage` registered in `run()`, which already executes inside the worker.
  - **C** — `runCtx.on(type, handler)` registers a handler per type on the port router, so `run()` owns the door and `defineHarbor` takes no new option.
  - **Recommend B (confidence 2)** — intake's case is `runtime.sendMessage`, which never touches a port, so A and C would add a second door beside one `run()` can already open; the gap is that nothing says so. Chrome itself treats one-time requests and ports as two separate listeners. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Chrome extensions keep two doors. One-time requests use `runtime.sendMessage` and arrive at `runtime.onMessage`. Long-lived ports use `runtime.connect` and arrive at `runtime.onConnect`, and a service worker registers one listener for each ([Chrome message passing](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/develop/concepts/messaging)). The platform does not route one into the other. This supports B: the app registers `onMessage` itself and the port stays framework-owned.

- **FJS-D1452 — How does site-kit learn which prose tag draws a photo and which attributes carry its address and words, so an owner's swap is let past `FJS-D1250`'s no-new-tag guard?** Found fixing `FJS-3436`. `codeAdded` (`packages/site-kit/src/editable.js`) refuses any tag in a new body that the old body lacks byte for byte, so `<Image source="/a.jpg" text="x" />` rewritten to `source="/b.jpg" text="y"` is refused as new code; the red test is `applyEdit(body with that tag, { body: swapped })` returning `{ text }` where it returns `{ refused }` today. `source` and `text` are ksite's attribute names (`Image.mesa`), and the preset returns only `sierra, plugins, shell, rules` (`config/preset.js` `KEYS`).
  - **A** — A new preset key `photos: { tag: 'Image', src: 'source', alt: 'text' }`: `codeAdded` lets a tag through when its name matches, every attribute but those two is byte-identical to a tag the old body held, and the new `src` passes `photoOf` and the `text` is non-blank. Cost: a sixth contract on `KEYS`, and `editable.js` (which basecamp's save and the model-diff grade call) needs the preset passed in, which it takes from no one today.
  - **B** — The same rule with the names fixed in site-kit (`Image`, `source`, `text`), no preset key. Cost: ksite's spelling becomes the engine's, which `FJS-D648` split out; a second preset spelling it `src`/`alt` gets no swap.
  - **C** — Leave prose tags to the developer and close `FJS-3436` as designed: an owner swaps a photo only where front matter holds it, and a page that needs a swap moves the tag's photo into a front-matter key. Cost: Luminous Heart's 29 tags stay unswappable.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the attribute names are the preset's vocabulary and `FJS-D1407` already read B as "a preset contract worth its own question"; the guard stays exact for every tag the preset does not name. Keystatic declares such components the same way, by name and field schema in config. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Keystatic declares embedded content components in the field's config: a name keyed to a `schema` of fields, such as `Playlist: block({ schema: { id: fields.text() } })`, which writes `<PlayList id="…" />` ([docs](https://keystatic.com/docs/content-components)). Decap CMS registers editor components with a `pattern` and `fromBlock`/`toBlock` mapping in config ([docs](https://decapcms.org/docs/custom-widgets/)). Both let the project, not the engine, name the tag and its attributes. TinaCMS takes a `templates` list on its rich-text field ([docs](https://tina.io/docs/reference/types/rich-text)). This supports A.

- **FJS-D1453 — How does a trial lane's copied register mint ids that cannot collide with main's or another lane's?** `fix:loop --trial` gives each lane a COPY of the register (`syncLane(lane, { linked: false })`), so `nextId` in `packages/cli/core/file.js` reads the same max in every lane: two lanes filing in one run mint the same `FJS-###` / `FJS-D###`, and a kept patch's ids are taken in main by the time it is applied (`FJS-2777`). A non-trial lane is safe already — the register is linked and `withRegisterGuard` serializes the writers.
  - **A** — A reserved floor per lane: `laneEnv` sets one variable on a trial lane, `nextId` and `nextDecisionNumber` mint above `max(register, floor)`, and the floor is main's high mark plus a block per lane (`registerHigh`). Cost: one new variable read by the id owner, and a patch still needs main's high mark to stay below the floor.
  - **B** — Renumber when a kept patch is applied: a trial patch's new ids are rewritten to the next free ones in main as it lands, citations in the same patch with them. Cost: a rewriter of ids inside prose, and nothing applies a trial patch today but a person with `git apply`.
  - **C** — No change: a trial's patches compare models and are read, never applied together; the ids are scratch. Cost: `FJS-1286`/`FJS-2650` style collisions stay for anyone who does apply two, and the row closes as designed with a sentence in `lanes.mjs`.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — the collision is minted at one place, the id owner (Invariant 4), and a floor there stops it for every kind of id at once instead of repairing prose afterwards. Postgres sequences hand each session a block for the same reason.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Postgres `CACHE n` lets each session preallocate a block of sequence values, guaranteeing distinct values across sessions at the price of gaps and out-of-order ids ([CREATE SEQUENCE](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createsequence.html)). Rails 2.1 replaced sequential migration numbers with UTC timestamps because numbers clashed between branches and developers ([commit](https://github.com/rails/rails/commit/c00de99f69358b58ca2bd6bc732e2de1b667800e)). Timestamps would break human-numbered `FJS-###` ids cited in prose *(inference)*, so the block-per-owner shape is the fit.

- **FJS-D1454 — What does a cron handler learn about the fire it was dispatched for, and where does the cron dispatch put it?** Found fixing `FJS-3078`. `index.ts` dispatches a cron fire as `caravan.dispatch(name, {}, { id: occurrenceKey('cron', name, fireMinute) })`, and the worker builds `JobContext` from the row (`worker.ts` `run`), which holds no minute, so a handler that must write the row it was OWED parses `ctx.id`. The red test is a `* * * * *` job whose handler records `ctx.firedFor` after the scheduler's tick: it is `undefined` today. The number the scheduler hands over is an instant for a wildcard schedule (epoch minute) and a naive wall-clock count for a fixed-time one (`wallMinute`, `FJS-D144`), so what the field MEANS is part of the question.
  - **A** — `ctx.firedFor: Date | null` is an instant, stored in a new nullable `jobs.fired_for` column (migrated on open). A wildcard fire is `minute * 60_000`; a fixed-time fire resolves its wall minute to the first instant that showed it in the schedule's zone, and a spring-gap minute (no instant) resolves to the instant the gap ends. Null for a hand run, so a handler writes `ctx.firedFor ?? now`. Cost: a column and the wall→instant resolution, which exists nowhere in `cron.ts`.
  - **B** — The same column, but it stores what the scheduler hands over untouched and `ctx.fireMinute: number | null` returns it, documented as epoch minutes for a wildcard schedule and a wall-clock count for a fixed-time one. Cost: the handler needs `isFixedTime(cron)` to read it, which is the trap the row names, moved from the id into the type.
  - **C** — No column: the cron dispatch passes the instant in `data` (`{ firedFor: <ms> }`), so a handler reads `ctx.data.firedFor`. Cost: the payload of every cron job stops being `{}`, `data` becomes a place the framework writes into, and a fixed-time schedule has the same wall-versus-instant question with nowhere to answer it.
  - **Recommend A** — a handler wants the instant it was owed (`FJS-3060` is the same wish from litestone's side), the ambiguity belongs where `wallMinute` lives rather than in every handler, and a context field keeps `data` the caller's. Quartz and Temporal both give the handler the scheduled instant, not the wall-clock fire. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Quartz `JobExecutionContext.getScheduledFireTime()` returns a `Date` for the time the trigger was scheduled to fire, apart from `getFireTime()`, the actual time ([docs](https://www.quartz-scheduler.org/api/2.3.0/org/quartz/JobExecutionContext.html)). Temporal appends the Action's timestamp to the workflow id and sets `TemporalScheduledStartTime` to it ([docs](https://docs.temporal.io/schedule)). Airflow calls `logical_date` an identifier with no semantics and points readers to `data_interval_start` and `data_interval_end` ([docs](https://airflow.apache.org/docs/apache-airflow/stable/templates-ref.html)). Instants win.

- **FJS-D1455 — Where does the notifications bell/list component live, and how does an app take it?** Found fixing `FJS-3451`. The package ships `.ts` and a `files` list of `*.ts`, `db`, `drivers`; its only UI is `examples/Notification.mesa`, a data half an app copies, and Basecamp's `_module.mesa` hand-writes the bell (a tether, `DropdownMenu`, a `Badge`, `markRead` and `markAllRead`). `FJS-D630` names the destination, "growing a real `resource/`", but no `fli add` exists to eject one, so there is no path from this package to an app's `src/resources/`. The red test is a file the package ships that an app can import or copy: `test -f packages/notifications/<the component>` fails today.
  - **A** — the package exports `./resource/*.mesa` (as `@frontierjs/ui` exports `./components/*.mesa`): `NotificationBell.mesa` and `NotificationList.mesa` take the app's `notifications` resource as a prop, so `markRead` stays the app's `mutate` and the component holds no tether of its own. Restyle by wrapping; no installer needed. Cost: `peerDependencies` on `@frontierjs/mesa` and `@frontierjs/ui`, and a `.ts` package now ships Mesa source that its own typecheck must skip.
  - **B** — a `resource/` folder in the package, copied into the app by hand now and by `fli add` later (`IDEAS/rigs.md` § Link vs eject: a bell is always restyled). Nothing is imported, so no peer dependency. Cost: every app holds a copy that upgrades never reach, which is the state this row complains about, one step better.
  - **C** — a generic `NotificationBell` in `@frontierjs/ui` fed `rows`, `onOpen`, `onMarkAll`. No dependency from notifications to ui, but ui learns a domain noun, and the tether wiring stays in every app.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — a prop-fed component keeps the write-through-`mutate` rule (`FJS-2111`) in the app and the markup in the package; B is where `FJS-D630` ends up once `fli add` exists, and A's files move there unchanged. Notification vendors ship the bell as an importable package component too. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Knock ships the bell and feed as pre-built components in `@knocklabs/react` (`NotificationIconButton`, `NotificationFeedPopover`), with hooks for a custom UI ([docs](https://docs.knock.app/in-app-ui/react/overview)). Novu ships `<Inbox />` with a `Bell` sub-component in `@novu/react`, restyled by overriding styles or replacing sub-components ([docs](https://docs.novu.co/platform/inbox/react/components)). Both import the component from a package and customize in place. Neither copies source into the app. This supports A over B.

- **FJS-D1456 — How does a queued job's payload answer a subject request?** Found fixing `FJS-2100`. `jobs.data` holds whatever the app dispatched until `cleanupAfter` (7 d) sweeps the terminal row, and no column names whom a job is about: `actor_id` is who asked, `correlation_id` is which request, `unique_key` is a live-work lock (`DispatchOptions`, `types.ts`). The red test would be `dispatch('mail', { to }, { subject: 'candidate:42' })` then `purgeSubject('candidate:42')` leaving `list()` with no row and no `to` in `jobs.db`; today neither the option nor the verb exists. `IDEAS/forgetting.md` § 5 left the queue "not built here" and "ids, not addresses" as the nearest help.
  - **A** — a stated `subject?: string` on `DispatchOptions`, stored in a new nullable `jobs.subject` column (migrated on open, partial index), and one verb `caravan.purgeSubject(subject, { actor, reason })` that DELETES every non-running row carrying it, running rows refused by name, recorded as an operator event like `purge`. Cost: a noun and a column; the app states the subject, so a dispatch that forgets to is still unreachable.
  - **B** — a scrub, not a verb: a job definition declares `payload: 'ids'` or `scrubOnFinish: true` and caravan nulls `data` the moment the job is terminal. Closes the 7 d window for done jobs and leaves a pending or retrying job holding the value; no subject noun.
  - **C** — no framework change: the contract is a payload of ids that mints the value at send time (jazzhr Phase 3), written into the `api-hazards` jobs entry, plus the `advise` rule `forgetting.md` § 5 names. Cost: the guarantee is a convention, so the next app measures this row again.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option that deletes a value already queued, and it matches the sibling shape `correlationId`/`findByCorrelation` (an optional stated tag plus a verb over it); B leaves pending work holding the value, and C is the workaround the row was measured against. Oban and Sidekiq delete by querying payloads, so precedent backs the verb but not the stated tag. Confidence 2.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Oban `delete_all_jobs` takes an Ecto query over the job table and deletes only jobs that are not executing ([docs](https://oban.hexdocs.pm/Oban.html)). Sidekiq has no delete-by-subject. You iterate a queue, filter on `job.args` and call `delete`. The wiki warns against bulk deletion except when something has gone wrong ([docs](https://github.com/sidekiq/sidekiq/wiki/API)). Both delete non-running work by matching payloads, with no stated subject tag. That backs the verb and the running-row refusal. It does not settle the tag against a payload query.

- **FJS-D1458 — How does an island ask to be injected on the toolbar click (activeTab) instead of registered by matches?** Found fixing `FJS-3386`. `buildRegistration` (`packages/jetty/src/island/registration.js`) throws `island "<id>": matches[] is required`, `manifest.js` unions every island's `matches` into `host_permissions` and into the island's `web_accessible_resources`, and `defineHarbor` registers all islands on every wake. A clipper that runs only when asked therefore has to declare `<all_urls>` to exist at all; `activeTab` plus `chrome.scripting.executeScript` on the action click needs no host permission. The red test: a config `islands: { clip: { on: 'action' } }` builds a manifest with no `host_permissions` entry for it, no `registerContentScripts` call for it, and `permissions` carrying `activeTab`; today `buildRegistration` throws.
  - **A** — one new island key, `inject: 'action'` (default `'matches'`): `matches` becomes optional for it, the manifest adds `activeTab` and skips the island's host permissions, Harbor skips registration and instead listens on `action.onClicked` and calls `scripting.executeScript({ target: { tabId }, files: ['islands/<id>.js'], world })`. Cost: Dock's `default_popup` suppresses `onClicked`, so an extension with both a Dock and an action island needs the island to run from a Dock/Harbor call instead; the build refuses that pair by name.
  - **B** — no config key: Harbor gets a verb `islands.inject(id, tabId)` and the app writes its own `action.onClicked` listener; the manifest adds `activeTab` whenever an island has no `matches`. Cost: the gesture wiring is hand-written in every clipper, and `web_accessible_resources` for the island still needs a rule.
  - **C** — close as out of scope: a clipper declares `hostPermissions: ['<all_urls>']` and `optional_host_permissions` is the narrower route later. Cost: the store question the row names stays unanswered by the framework.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — the island file is already discovered and built, `runAt`/`world`/`css` map one-to-one onto `executeScript`/`insertCSS`, and the manifest already derives permissions from the island config, so one key keeps the declaration where `matches` already lives; B moves the same wiring into every app.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — WXT gives a content script entrypoint `registration: "runtime"`, which keeps it out of the manifest and lets `matches` be omitted. The background then calls `scripting.executeScript` on `action.onClicked` with `activeTab` ([WXT PR 2288](https://github.com/wxt-dev/wxt/pull/2288), [scripting guide](https://wxt.dev/guide/essentials/scripting.html)). Chrome says `activeTab` "essentially grants host permission temporarily" with no install warning ([docs](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/develop/concepts/activeTab)). So a per-entry config key is the precedent, as in A.

- **FJS-D1650 — How does Basecamp move an enrolled machine's Outpost pin to `OUTPOST_VERSION`?** Found fixing FJS-3533: the unit's `ExecStart` carries `@frontierjs/outpost@<version at install>` (`enrollment.ts` unit heredoc) and nothing rewrites it, so yuma ran 0.1.5 after 0.1.8 shipped. Basecamp already holds both numbers (`Server.outpostVersion` off the heartbeat, `OUTPOST_VERSION`) and the signed `/exec` channel with the certificate pinned (`FJS-D557`). The red test: after the action, a machine enrolled at an older pin has `ExecStart` naming `OUTPOST_VERSION`, its `outpostCert` and env file untouched. The restart ends the Outpost serving the call, so whichever option is chosen detaches it (`systemd-run`, as `docker.js` does for recipes). `/servers/` marking a machine behind `OUTPOST_VERSION` is the same in every option.
  - **A** — a Server action `upgradeOutpost` sends one `/exec` script: `sed` the pin in `/etc/systemd/system/outpost.service`, `daemon-reload`, detached `systemctl restart outpost`; a `fli basecamp:server:upgrade` command calls it. Works on every machine already enrolled, yuma included, with no install change. Cost: the script restates the unit's `ExecStart` line, so a change to that line in `enrollment.ts` must keep the `sed` matching (a test holds the two together).
  - **B** — the install writes `/usr/local/sbin/outpost-upgrade <version>` beside `outpost-uninstall`, and the action runs it over `/exec`. The edit lives next to the unit it edits and a person at a shell can run it. Cost: a machine enrolled before this has no script, so yuma still takes one hand edit, and every machine carries a second Basecamp-written file to keep honest.
  - **C** — unpin the unit (`@frontierjs/outpost@latest` or a dist-tag) so a restart upgrades. No action, no gap. Cost: two machines provisioned a week apart stop running the same thing (the pin exists for that, `enrollment.ts` header), an unpublished or broken release reaches every machine on its next restart, and `FJS-3534` widens from new enrollments to the whole fleet.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — it is the only option that reaches machines already enrolled, which is the row's own failing case, and the pin stays so a release still reaches a machine only when Basecamp says. B is A plus a file, and the file's only extra reader is a person at a shell, who has `sed`. The reopen path is a second place that edits the unit, which would make the script worth lifting into the install (B). Two conditions on building A: `enrollment.ts` exports the `ExecStart` line (or its matcher) so the heredoc and the script share one copy, and the action checks `outpostUnpublished()` first, since a bad pin leaves `Restart=always` looping on the only channel that could repair it; the script is idempotent and `grep`s the pin after the `sed`, exiting non-zero when it did not change.

- **FJS-D1651 — How does an agent (Ace, Charles) walk into a Haven that takes only a Frontier member's machine?** Found fixing FJS-3471: `POST /haven/login` with no `links` is a 401 (`login.ts` § `body.links === undefined`, `FJS-D1477`), and `agents.js` signs in with a key of its own under `~/.fli/haven/agents/<nick>/` through `machineKeys` + `signIn`, so `bun run agents` is refused at its first login and the live halves of `agents.test.js` are `describe.skip`. The red assertion: an agent's `signIn` against a Haven with `HAVEN_POST_KEY` set returns a token and a member whose nick is the agent's.
  - **A** — A Haven-listed agent key: `HAVEN_AGENTS=ace:<pubkey>,charles:<pubkey>` read by `loginRoute` beside `mods`, as Matrix appservice registration. A bare `{ publicKey }` login whose key is listed gets the listed nick and no chain; every other key stays refused. The agents keep their own keys and `machineKeys` stays for them. Cost: a second door in `login.ts` (one more way to make a member), and the operator pastes two public keys into the Haven's env.
  - **B** — Staked membership: each agent's key is a machine in a Frontier chain (`fli frontier:stake` once per agent, signed by the post). Login stays one shape and `login.ts` gains nothing. Cost: an agent is a Frontier member, which costs a stake per agent and mints a post row for something that is not a person.
  - **C** — In-process: `agents.js` runs inside the Haven API and writes through `db.asSystem()` and the room's own calls, with no login and no key. Cost: agents leave the process boundary they have now (one `claude` per soul, `ask-claude.js`), `machineKeys` goes with no remaining caller, and the socket path the agents are tested through stops being exercised.
  - **D** — A link in the owner's chain: the owner links each agent key into their own chain (`fli frontier:link`), so an agent logs in as a machine of its owner. Cost: the Haven sees one member with several machines, and the nick is the owner's, so Ace and Charles cannot be told apart in a room.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — it is the only option that keeps both the one-login-shape refusal for strangers (the list is the Haven's, not the caller's) and a distinct nick per agent without minting Frontier members; B is the cleaner door but pays a stake for a non-person, and C undoes the agents' process boundary.

- **FJS-D1652 — Is `FJS-3525`'s part (3) — the declared inputs arriving in the App's create body, so a first `--dry` has nothing to list — wanted, now that `fli basecamp:app` makes the App bare and `basecamp:deploy` completes it on every run?** Found fixing `FJS-3525`: parts (1) and (2) are built and applied on every run (`FJS-D1599`, `releasePlan` in `packages/cli/core/release-preflight.js`). `basecamp:app` ([app.md](packages/cli/commands/basecamp/app.md)) POSTs `/projects`, `/environments` and `/apps` and is bare on purpose. So a fresh App's first `releaseFindings` is the reconcile's to-do list (3 errors, 7 warnings), not a defect.
  - **A** — close (3) as delivered: bare create plus reconcile derives each setting once from `releasePlan`. Cost: a first `--dry` on a fresh App still lists the whole setup as findings.
  - **B** — `basecamp:app` imports `releasePlan` and sends the plan's `app` patch and Variables in its create body. Cost: `app.md` needs the Domain question and the secret minting `deploy.md` has, and the plan is applied in two commands.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — one owner per translation (Invariant 4): `releasePlan` plus `applyPlan` own the settings and `basecamp:app` owns the rows; B puts the plan's output in a second command to save one `--dry` listing. Fly's bare create works the same way. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — `fly apps create` registers only the app name and org. It "won't generate a fly.toml configuration file", and settings come from a separate step ([docs](https://docs.fly.io/flyctl/apps-create)). *(inference: that `fly deploy` then applies `fly.toml` is the documented flow, but the reference page for it returned 404.)* One bare-create precedent was found, and no product was found that pushes its full config in the create call. This supports A.

- **FJS-D1659 — Outside row tenancy, who hears a gate-mode frame (the `FJS-D597` regrade, a bulk count): every reader of the model's gate, only readers of the written row's images, or only readers of a named workspace claim?** `gradeRecipients`' gate branch (`transport/channels.ts`) narrows by tenant only under `strategy row` with a claims resolver (`FJS-D1038`); in an app that separates workspaces by `check(org)` it admits on `readGateLevel` alone, so quo's dan (another org) hears `calls changed`, `callLegs changed` and four `agentTurns changed` as `{ operation: 'regrade', count: null }`. The red test asserts that a second-workspace socket receives no frame after a write to the first workspace's call (quo `ring.test.ts`, `agent.test.ts` § who hears).
  - **A** — admit a gate-mode recipient outside row tenancy only when `$readAs` reads the WRITTEN row's before- or after-image for them, so `gate` mode carries the row (or both images) like `row` mode does. Cost: a revoke whose Share the grantee cannot read never heals their screen, the stale-screen case `FJS-D597` turned down; the tap threads two images to the gate branch. The ruling must also say which model grades the image (a `D597` regrade counts a DEPENDENT model, so the written Share is graded under the SOURCE model's accessor, not the dependent's) and what a bulk write threads (`FJS-D1038` B was turned down for materializing many-row purges: one image per row, or a cap).
  - **B** — leave the gate branch as is and have the boot warning (`warnRefusedAll`'s sibling) name a policy-separated app, documenting that call cadence crosses workspaces. Cost: the leak stays and every such app owns it.
  - **C** — let the app name the workspace claim its `check(org)` reads (the claims resolver's key), and grade a gate frame the way `FJS-D1038` does under row tenancy, by the tenants the write touched. Cost: a new option, and Litestone must answer which workspaces a write touched for a policy-scoped model, which it answers today only through `@@tenant`.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the cadence of another workspace's calls is a leak and a stale revoke is a delay, but `FJS-D597` chose fail-closed over exactly this, so the ruling should say the heal case (a Share the grantee cannot read) is paid by the app announcing it. Supabase Realtime pays the same price. Confidence 3.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Supabase Realtime Postgres Changes "authorizes every event against each subscriber", so 100 subscribers cost 100 checks. It does not apply RLS to `DELETE`, because the deleted row can no longer be checked, and it points large audiences to Broadcast ([docs](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/realtime/postgres-changes)). Hasura multiplexes live queries only among subscriptions with the same role, which keeps one role's result from reaching another ([docs](https://hasura.io/docs/2.0/subscriptions/postgres/livequery/execution/)). Both check per recipient and accept lost deletes or higher cost. This supports A.

- **FJS-D1662 — How does the App picker label tell apart two projects' same-named environments?** Found fixing FJS-3639: `App` declares `@@label([name, environment.name])`, one hop (`schema.lite` § model App); `project` is two hops away, so `worker · Production` repeats across projects. `FJS-D674` refuses a two-hop `@@label` at parse by name, and a `@generated` column cannot read another table.
  - **A** — widen `FJS-D674` to allow two to-one hops (`@@label([name, environment.project.name, environment.name])`): parser, `x-label-field`, and sierra `options()` populate/search follow the longer path. Cost: reopens a ruling made for the one-hop cases measured, and every consumer of the list (fts title, ⌘K, relation filter) gains a depth.
  - **B** — give `App` a `projectId` own column with a `project` relation, so `project.name` is one hop (`App` already carries `workspaceId` the same way, denormalized). Cost: a schema change on a frozen migration lock (`FJS-D1660`), and the column must stay equal to `environment.project` on every write.
  - **C** — give `Environment` an own `label` column holding `project · name`, kept by a hook, and label `App` by `[name, environment.label]`. Cost: a derived column written by a hook, which Invariant 6 prefers declared in the schema; renaming a project must rewrite every environment's copy.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the repeat is a label problem and A fixes it where labels live, with no second copy of the project to keep equal; B and C each add a denormalized column a rename can leave stale. Precedent splits between reading through the relation and storing a copy.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Odoo's `display_name` is a computed field that can read across relations, and for partners it stores a separate `complete_name` because the computed form is costly to search ([forum](https://www.odoo.com/forum/help-1/what-is-the-logic-behind-display-name-field-of-product-product-178452), read through a search summary). Directus v8 templates reach through a junction with `{{movie.name}}` ([docs](https://v8.docs.directus.io/guides/relationships.html), also a search summary). One product reads through, one stores a copy.

- **FJS-D1663 — How does a route file (`FJS-D626`) reach the app: a second argument, `ctx.app`, or an ambient import?** `autoloadRoutes` (`packages/junction/src/core/loader.ts`) registers `mod[v]` as the handler, so `export const GET = (ctx) => …` sees only the `TransportContext`, which has no `app` (`ServiceContext` does, `context.ts:211`). Basecamp's `GET /backup-archive/{id}` needs `app.db.asSystem()`, `app.sqlite.filename` and `app.logger`, so it stays a plugin and `app-layout` holds a ceiling of 1 (`FJS-3596`). Red test: a route file `export const GET = (ctx) => ({ hasLogger: !!ctx.app?.logger })`, or `(ctx, app) => …`, answers `hasLogger: false` today.
  - **A** — `autoloadRoutes` binds the app as a second argument: `register(url, ctx => mod[v](ctx, app))`, so `GET(ctx, app)`. Cost: a handler signature the plain `app.get` form lacks; the one place that knows both is the loader, so no transport changes.
  - **B** — `ctx.app` on `TransportContext`, parallel to `ServiceContext.app`. Cost: the HTTP and WS transports build contexts apart from the app (`http.ts` `_buildContext`, `bridge.internal()`), so each must be handed the app, `context-contract` and the testing parity runner gain a field, and every middleware and plugin handler sees it too.
  - **C** — an ambient `app` export from `@frontierjs/junction` set at boot, like `$`. Cost: a process-global that a test mounting two apps cannot hold, and a second owner of `app` beside `createApp`'s result (Invariant 5).
  - **Recommend A (confidence 2)** — the loader already holds the app and is the only code that registers route files, so it is the one-owner fix with no transport or contract-test surface; B is the wider design and waits until a non-route handler needs the app too. Precedent leans B: Koa and Fastify put the app on the request object, so B is the live alternative.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Koa exposes the application on the context, `ctx.app`, "Application instance reference" ([Koa](https://koajs.com/#context)). Fastify's request carries `request.server`, "the Fastify server instance, scoped to the current encapsulation context" ([Request](https://fastify.dev/docs/latest/Reference/Request/)). Both put the app on the per-request object, which is B. Neither passes it as a second handler argument, as A does.

- **FJS-D1664 — A stand-in a person runs beside the dev pair binds a port, and `test-port-literal` can only prescribe `portFor()`, which has no category for it: exempt it, give it a category, or only reword the message?** Found fixing FJS-2525: maidtech's `api/test/standins/crm.ts` (8794 under `import.meta.main`) and ELA's four stand-ins (8790–8793, `SA_URL` documented as `localhost:8790`) are tools a person starts by hand and types the URL of from memory. `portFor(root, 'tooling', { env: 'dev' })` answers 8500 for every scaffolded app (`tooling` is not in `SLOTTED`, [ports.js](packages/cli/core/ports.js)), `{ env: 'test' }` answers 7500–7503 for every one, and `'be'` is pinned to the app's slot. The red assertion: a file `api/test/standins/crm.ts` holding `listen(8794)` yields a `test-port-literal` finding today (`checks/app.js` `driveFiles(root, /^tests?$/)` takes every script under `test/`). Both apps carry it in `check-baseline.json` naming FJS-2525.
  - **A** — the rule skips a `standins/` directory under `test/`: a stand-in is a tool, its port is documented, and a branch checkout of it is not meant to move. Cost: a directory-name convention the rule reads, and a stand-in written elsewhere stays graded; two apps' stand-ins can still collide if both pick the same literal, which `fli ports` would not see.
  - **B** — give stand-ins a port category (a `SLOTTED` entry or a tenth CAT digit) so `portFor(…, 'standin', { service })` hands each app its own and a branch moves it. Cost: the CAT digits 0–8 are used and `ports.js` header says the schema is closed at nine, so this adds a tenth noun (`the-nine-stay-nine` pressure) and the URL stops being typeable from memory in a branch.
  - **C** — keep the rule and reword its message to name the existing per-file `allow` with a reason, the way both apps already use it. Cost: the finding still fires on a thing that is not a defect, and every app with a stand-in carries an allow row.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — the rule exists so a *test* follows a branch's offset; a stand-in a person runs is not a test and must not move, and the directory is already where both apps put theirs. Firebase and LocalStack both give a hand-started tool a fixed, documented port, which is what A preserves.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Firebase's emulators each bind a preferred default port (Firestore 8080, Auth 9099, UI 4000), changed only by editing `firebase.json` ([docs](https://firebase.google.com/docs/emulator-suite/install_and_configure)). LocalStack's gateway listens on 4566 unless `GATEWAY_LISTEN` is set ([docs](https://docs.localstack.cloud/references/configuration/)). Tools a person types a URL for keep a fixed documented port. No outside lint rule with a stand-in exemption was found *(inference: not searched for)*.

- **FJS-D1665 — A memo chain past the engine's stack depth throws a bare `RangeError` that names no node: guard the depth, pull known deps iteratively, or document the ceiling?** `read` → `_recompute` → `read` (`packages/mesa/src/runtime.js` `createMemo`) recurses once per link. Probed under vitest on node 22: a 2000-link chain of `createMemo(() => p() + 1)` reads 2000; 5000 and 20000 throw `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`, on first read and after a write alike. The ceiling is the engine's, so node and bun differ (`FJS-907`). The red assertion: a 20000-link chain read either returns its value or throws an error naming a memo.
  - **A** — a depth counter in `_recompute`: past a fixed limit (say 1000) throw a Mesa error naming the memo and the limit, the same on every runtime. Cost: one module-level counter on the hottest path, and it turns a chain bun holds today into an error; a limit chosen by this package is a number to defend.
  - **B** — pull iteratively: on a dirty read, walk the memo's previous `_deps` that are dirty memos with an explicit stack and recompute leaves first, so the recursion never exceeds one link. Cost: fixes only chains that have computed once, since deps are unknown before `fn()` first runs, so a first read of 20000 links still overflows; a second pull path beside `read` (Invariant 4 wants one owner).
  - **C** — leave it and write the ceiling into `docs/hazards.md` as engine-bound. Cost: the overflow still names no node, and a shared app keeps a different limit per runtime.
  - **Recommend C (confidence 2)** — nothing anyone has written reaches the limit, and Solid's maintainer closed the same overflow as by-design rather than pay for a guard. Write the ceiling into `docs/hazards.md` as engine-bound, and if the unnamed `RangeError` hurts, catch it once at the outermost read and rename it for the memo, which needs no counter on the hot path.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — Solid 2.0 hit this exact overflow with a memo chain of about 8,000 ([issue 4587](https://github.com/solidjs/solid/issues/4587)). A fix that walked the chain iteratively was written and not merged ([PR 4599](https://github.com/solidjs/solid/pull/4599)): the maintainer closed the issue as not planned, because about 39 B on every app was too much for a depth real apps do not reach, and told users to flatten the chain. I found no framework that guards depth. This favors C.

- **FJS-D1666 — How does a server render stop waiting on an `{#await}` whose promise never settles: a timeout option, an abort signal, or nothing?** `_render`/`_renderString` (`packages/mesa/src/render.js`) `await settleAwaits()` / `settle(out)` with no bound, so `renderToHTML(Comp)` over `{#await new Promise(() => {})}` never returns, and its `_queue` tail never resolves either, so every later render in the process hangs behind it, naming no file. The label a failure already carries is `options.label` (`renderFailure`). The red assertion: `renderToHTML(Hang, {}, { label: 'Hang.mesa', <bound> })` rejects with a message naming `Hang.mesa`; today it never settles.
  - **A** — `options.timeout` (ms) on `renderToHTML`: past it the render rejects through `renderFailure` as `await did not settle in Nms`, disposes the root, and releases the queue. No default, so a slow legitimate fetch is never cut. Cost: a new option on the public renderer; the number is the caller's to pick; a `setTimeout` raced against the wait in both paths.
  - **B** — `options.signal` (an `AbortSignal`) instead, React's shape: the caller owns the clock and can share one abort across a whole prerender (`AbortSignal.timeout(n)` gives A for free). Cost: the same plumbing as A, plus callers must build the signal, so the build's own prerender loop (`render-component.js` `executeComponent`) has to choose a number anyway.
  - **C** — a finite default bound (say 30 s) on `executeComponent` only, with `renderToHTML` left unbounded, Svelte's shape. Cost: no new public option, but an unbounded direct caller still hangs and the default is a number this package defends.
  - **Recommend B (confidence 3)** — `AbortSignal.timeout` spells A in one call, and a signal lets a build abort every in-flight page at once; the refusal names `options.label` so the hang finally has an address.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — React's `renderToReadableStream` takes `signal`, an AbortSignal, and its docs show `setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), 10000)` to make the server give up on slow content ([docs](https://react.dev/reference/react-dom/server/renderToReadableStream)). That is B. Next.js bounds static page generation with a default `staticPageGenerationTimeout` of 60 seconds and restarts the worker ([docs](https://nextjs.org/docs/messages/static-page-generation-timeout)). That is C at the build layer. So the public renderer takes a signal and the build chooses the number.

- **FJS-D1667 — How does a Basecamp blueprint param mint a valid ntfy sender token, when `ParamGenerator` is a hex-only enum and the token must name a user in a sibling param?** `paramValues` (`packages/basecamp/api/src/services/apps/apps.service.ts`) calls `mint(generator)` for one param, and `mint` accepts only `random_hex_N`; `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` needs `name:tk_<exactly 29 letters or digits>:label` with `name` a row of `NTFY_AUTH_USERS`, whose `name:hash:role` carries a bcrypt hash nothing here can mint. Red probe: no `ParamGenerator` value yields a string `firstSenderToken` (`api/src/push.ts`) accepts. Adding a value widens the enum and its `CHECK` in `20260801000000_initial_schema.sql`, which `FJS-D1660` freezes, so the change is a new appended migration, not an edit.
  - **A** — a generator `ntfy_token` that mints the whole `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` value with a fixed sender name (`frontier:tk_<29>:Frontier`). Cost: enum change plus an appended migration; the operator still types a matching `frontier:<hash>:user` into `NTFY_AUTH_USERS` by hand, so half the restart-loop is still possible, and the generator names a user the blueprint does not own.
  - **B** — a generator that mints only the `tk_<29>` secret into a new generated param `NTFY_SENDER_TOKEN`, and the ntfy container's `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` is composed from it at install. Cost: a derived-value concept (a param built from other params) the blueprint model has no word for, and `firstSenderToken` reads a different variable.
  - **C** — no generator; keep the hand-typed value and the hint and notes FJS-3595 added (`ntfy token generate` makes a valid one). Close FJS-3657 as not worth a schema change. Cost: the first install still needs a docker run before the form submits.
  - **D** — refuse a malformed or unprovisioned `NTFY_AUTH_TOKENS` at install, naming the field, instead of letting the container restart-loop (no enum change, no migration); file as its own row if taken.
  - **Recommend C** — a generator can only make half of a pair whose other half is a bcrypt hash it cannot make, so it moves the typing rather than removing it, and it costs an enum change and a migration after the freeze. `decision-rules` agrees; D is the cheap follow-up if first-install friction hurts.

- **FJS-D1675 — How do junction's README examples that grade a hostname run offline: an injectable resolver, a prelude that stubs DNS and fetch, or stay fragment?** `app.webhooks.register('https://partner.com/…')` (`README.md` § Registering a subscriber) reaches `gradePublicUrl` (`src/core/public-url.ts:155`), whose `resolve` is a third parameter no plugin option reaches and whose `lookup` is a named import of `node:dns/promises`, so a doc-runner prelude cannot swap it. The conduit blocks (`api.hetzner.cloud`, `outpost:srv-abc`) dial a real address. Red assertion: with the network down, `DOC_EXAMPLES_ONLY=README.md:1962` fails at grading with a lookup error, so the fence cannot drop `fragment`. The `FRAGMENT_CEILING` in `test/doc-examples.test.ts` falls by one per block completed.
  - **A** — `PublicUrlOptions.resolve`: the existing third parameter MOVES into the option (no second spelling; the three `webhook-targets.test.ts` call sites become `{ resolve }`), and the prelude passes a stub resolver. Cost: a new public option on the SSRF guard, shared by webhooks, conduit `destinations`, basecamp and webpush, that exists only for tests, next to `allowPrivate`; it is never fed from a caller-supplied value. It completes the `register` block alone, so the ceiling falls by one, not three: the conduit blocks still need the stub transport README already documents.
  - **B** — prelude only: preload that replaces `globalThis.fetch` and `Bun.dns`/`node:dns` before the block, no source change. Cost: `lookup` binds at import, so this needs a bun plugin rewriting the import in the run's tmpdir; the block then proves a stub, not the guard.
  - **C** — leave the 3 network blocks `fragment` and record why next to the ceiling; complete only the surface ones that can run. Cost: three examples the suite never runs, which Invariant 16 names a bug.
  - **Recommend A (confidence 3)** — the third parameter already exists for exactly this, `allowPrivate` sets the precedent of a policy key a test turns on and says so, and B proves a stub where A proves the guard's real code path. Ruby's `ssrf_filter` gem takes the same `resolver:` option.
  - **Prior art** (read 2026-11-01) — The Ruby `ssrf_filter` gem takes a `resolver:` proc per call that maps a hostname to IP addresses, defaulting to `Resolv`, and its README shows a static resolver in use ([README](https://github.com/arkadiyt/ssrf_filter)). The SSRF guard runs its real check over a stubbed lookup, which is A. No product read stubs DNS by rewriting an import, as B would.

- **FJS-D1706 — Does a declared move take hook-written `ctx.data`, or does the move stay data-less until `FJS-D1699`'s engine-written WHO lands?** Found fixing `FJS-3731`. Probed: `hooks.before.<move>` runs on `invoke(move, id)` and may set `ctx.data = { movedBy }`, but `declaredTransition` calls `table.transition(id, move)`, which takes no data, so the column stays null (`src/core/litestone.ts` `transitionMethod`). The red test asserts `movedBy === 'u1'` after the move; it fails today.
  - **A** — `transitionMethod` writes the hook's `ctx.data` (honoring `ctx.system`) inside the same transaction as the move, so a move is a patch that also names its state. Gives every app a stamp seam, but it is a second app-side route to a protected column beside the one `FJS-D1699` removes, and a Litestone `transition(id, move, { data })` shape to rule on.
  - **B** — nothing in junction: the move stays data-less, and the `hooks.after` + `asSystem` workaround in the app stands until `FJS-D1699`'s `@stamps(by:)` build makes the move write its actor, which retires the hook and `FJS-3693` with it.
  - **C** — run `hooks.before.patch` on a move too, so one hook covers every door. Makes a move a patch in the hook pipeline, which the pipeline's per-method naming does not say.
  - **Recommend B** — `FJS-D1699` already ruled that the access rule's inputs leave hooks for the schema (Invariant 6); building A or C hands the hook a seam the ruling is deleting.

- **FJS-D1707 — What does a REFUSED access leave in an audit trail, and may the entry name the id it tried?** Found fixing `FJS-3708`. Today `emitLogs('read', …)` runs only `if (tableHasLogWork && rows.length > 0)` (`src/core/client.js` findMany/findFirst/findUnique), and the row policy is folded into the WHERE, so a policy-refused read is an empty result and logs nothing. A refused write throws before `emitLogs`. Red test: `@@trail(audit, reads: true)` model, outsider `findUnique({ where: { id } })` → null, `audit_trail` count unchanged (want +1); a signed-Note `update` → `SealedDocumentError`, count unchanged. An entry needs an `operation` value the trail does not have today (`read | create | update | delete`), and for a read the entry cannot say whether the id exists (the policy hid it), so naming it is the Invariant 7 question.
  - **A** — one new operation, `denied`, for reads AND writes: an entry per refused call, `records: []`, with the attempted verb and the `where` KEYS (never values) in a `note`. No id ever, so the entry leaks nothing about the row and nothing needs redacting. Cost: a privacy officer sees "someone tried a chart" but not which.
  - **B** — `denied` carries the id the caller NAMED (`findUnique`/`update` `where` on the id field), as supplied by the caller, never read from storage. The caller already holds that id, so it discloses nothing new; it answers the privacy officer. Lists and `where` filters log no ids. Risk: an unbounded caller-chosen string lands in the trail, so it is length-capped and logged as an id only when it parses as the id field's type.
  - **C** — as B, plus system callers: a `@@require`/state-guard refusal under `asSystem()` that today answers `null` / `{count: 0}` also writes a `denied` row (actor = system), because a job that tried to delete a signed note is evidence too. A and B leave system silent.
  - **D** — leave reads unlogged and log only refused WRITES (a refusal there is a thrown error, so the call site is unambiguous). Cost: the 404-vs-empty read, the case a privacy officer most needs, stays dark.
  - **Recommend B** — the id is the caller's own input, so it is not a new disclosure under Invariant 7 and it is the whole value of the entry; C's system half is a separate defect (`@@require` answering null silently) and should be its own row after this ruling.
- **FJS-D1730 — How does a client filter name the caller: a caller token in a where, a per-request projection of the caller's claims, or keep the client reading its own row?** Found fixing `FJS-3725`. The approver inbox (`submitted` and not mine, or `approved1` by someone else) is sayable only with the caller's Employee id as a literal: `ne` is refused (`not`/`notIn` exist) and nothing in a filter names the caller. The id lives in `claim employeeId from Employee(userId)` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts` § Claims resolved per request), which is resolved per request onto the principal and never reaches the session or the client. `sessionFields` cannot carry it: it runs at sign-in with the user row only (`packages/auth/auth.ts:268`), and Employee is a different row. The red test's assertion: `GET /claims?$or[0][submittedById][not]=<token>` for a caller whose claim is 7 returns the rows `not: 7` returns; today the token is a literal string that matches nothing.
  - **A** — a caller token as a filter VALUE, spelled like the sibling `auth().field` (`@default`, `@createdBy`) rather than a second caller noun, e.g. `[not]=auth().employeeId` or `@me.employeeId`, substituted from the request's principal claims at the Data boundary. The filter language gains one value form; it needs a name that is not `$`-prefixed (Invariant 10) and a refusal for an unknown claim. The client never learns the id, and the same token works in a service-built query.
  - **B** — project declared claims to the client: the session/whoami answer carries the claims the schema names (`claim employeeId …`), resolved per request, so a screen reads `session.employeeId` and builds the literal. No filter-grammar change, but the id is now client data (a leak surface for a claim an app meant to keep server-side) and `claim` needs an opt-in word to say which are projected.
  - **C** — keep it: the screen reads its own Employee row first and the filter stays advisory (policies refuse the wrong approver, Invariant 6). Document it in the junction hazards; nothing to build, one extra read per inbox screen.
  - **Recommend A** — the id never leaves the server, one token serves both client and service queries, and Invariant 6 is untouched since it narrows a read rather than granting one. Prior art to check before ruling: Hasura's `X-Hasura-User-Id` session variables in permission filters and PostgREST's `request.jwt.claims` are server-side only, and Directus's `$CURRENT_USER` is a filter token — all three put the caller token in the filter, none projects it for the client to echo.

- **FJS-D1731 — How is the engine-kept last-mover column (FJS-D1699 § B, FJS-3694) declared?** `@stamps(…, by: col)` is built (parser `parseTransitionStamps`, `moveStampSets` in client.js) and writes `ctx.auth.id` into ONE column per move. D1699 owes "an engine-kept `lastMovedById`" so rule 3 (`@@deny(update, (status == 'submitted' || status == 'approved1') && lastMovedById == auth().id)`) grades the last MOVER, not the last writer (`@updatedBy`), but ruled no spelling. Today `by:` refuses a second column (`@stamps names a by: column twice`), so a move cannot write both `approvedBy1Id` and `lastMovedById`. The red test: a model with a machine and `lastMovedById`; a plain non-move PATCH by user B after user A's move must leave `lastMovedById == A`, and a move by B must set it to B. Both fail today (no way to declare it).
  - **A** — A named argument of the machine: `@@transitions(status, lastMover: lastMovedById, submit: draft -> submitted @stamps(submittedAt, by: submittedById), …)`. One declaration per machine, every move of it writes `ctx.auth.id` into that column in the move's own UPDATE (same `@system`/`@guarded` requirement and payload refusal as `by:`; no principal leaves it as it was). Sits beside `declaredBy:`/`scope:`, the existing named arguments; no new attribute.
