  - **B** — A field attribute on the column: `lastMovedById String? @system @lastMover(status)`. Same behavior; the declaration lives with the column a policy names, a new attribute noun in the catalog.
  - **C** — No new spelling: let `by:` take several columns (`@stamps(approvedAt, by: approvedBy1Id, lastMovedById)`) and have each move name `lastMovedById` itself. Zero new surface, but a move that omits it leaves the column stale and rule 3 silently permissive (a gate that fails open).
  - **Recommend A** — it declares once per machine so no move can forget the column (C fails open), and reuses the named-argument slot of `@@transitions` instead of coining an attribute (B).

- **FJS-D1742 — What names a local File store's signing key so URLs survive a restart and a second api process?** `mountLocalFiles` sets `store.config.signingKey ??= randomBytes(32)`; nothing else can set one (`FILES_ENV` has no signing row), so a URL printed before a restart, or by a second api process behind one origin, fails `store.verify` with 403. Red test: two stores mounted over one directory, a URL from `a.sign(key)` fails `b.verify(key, …)`; after the fix it passes.
  - **A** — A `signingKey: 'FILES_SIGNING_KEY'` row in `FILES_ENV` (`packages/litestone/src/storage/file-storage.js`), beside `FILES_PUBLIC_BASE`. One new env name an operator sets and a deploy writes; every app with a local File column needs it for a restart-stable URL, and unset keeps today's random key.
  - **B** — Derive it: HKDF-SHA256 over `ENCRYPTION_KEY` with a versioned label (`files signing key v1`), as `FJS-D1646` derived Basecamp's hub pair. No new name; an app with no `ENCRYPTION_KEY` keeps the random key, and rotating `ENCRYPTION_KEY` ends every printed URL (they expire within the hour anyway).
  - **C** — Both: `FILES_SIGNING_KEY` wins, else the derivation, else random. Covers the app with no encryption key, at the price of two spellings for one thing.
  - **Recommend A** — `FILES_ENV` already owns every File-store env name and `ENCRYPTION_KEY` is optional (`env.ts` grades it only when declared), so B leaves a File app with no encrypted field unfixed and puts a second reader of the key in junction; `FJS-D1646` derived because Basecamp always holds one. B stays the reopen path as a fallback under the label `files signing key v1`. Owed with A: a production boot warning when the random key is in use.

- **FJS-D1744 — What shape lets a service filter trail reads per chart: one entry per record id, an indexed column naming the record, or the id array kept and a side table beside it?** Found fixing `FJS-3784`. `emitLogs` (`trail-entries.js:186`) writes ONE entry per read with `records: ids`, a `Json` array (`audit-log.js:45`, `AuditTrail.lite:52`), so `@@allow(read, …)` on the trail cannot anti-join `CareTeam` against a chart id: a policy compares a column, and an array is not one. The only per-subject column, `subjectId`, is support mode's principal (`audit-log.js:235`), and `records` has no index. Red test: a trail with reads of Patient 1 and Patient 2 by one actor; a reader policy `recordId in careTeam(auth())` returns both or neither, never the one chart.
  - **A** — One entry per id on a read (and a bulk write): `records` stays a one-element array or becomes a scalar `recordId` column with `@@index([model, recordId])`. A policy and a service filter then name one column. Cost: a 200-row list read writes 200 entries (`FJS-2317` measured 100–170 µs per audited write), the trail grows by the page size, and a write's `correlationId` is what ties a bulk write back together.
  - **B** — Keep one entry per operation and add an indexed `recordId String?` for the single-row case only, null on a multi-row entry. Cheap and indexed, but a list read stays invisible to a per-chart reader — the privacy officer's commonest question ("who looked at this chart") misses every list that included it, a gate that fails closed on the wrong rows.
  - **C** — Keep `records` as the array and let the engine fan out a side model `AuditTrailRecord(entryId, model, recordId)` with `@@index([model, recordId])`, written in the same statement batch. One entry per operation stays one, a policy anti-joins the side table, and the trail entry's shape is unchanged. Cost: a second model in the trait, a second append-only gate (Invariant 6), and a writer that must keep both in step.
  - **Recommend A** — the question a reader asks is per chart, and one row per (operation, record) is the only shape both a row policy and an `EXPLAIN` over an index can answer without a join; a read of a page is what `FJS-3708`/`FJS-3714` already log row by row for the field entries. Prior art to check before ruling: Postgres `pgaudit` object-level logging and FHIR `AuditEvent.entity` (an array on one event) keep the array, while Rails `paper_trail` and Django `simple_history` write one row per record — none of them filters by policy.

- **FJS-D1745 — Does an audit-trail entry with no tenant on the call take the tenant of the row it describes, or stay null?** `audit-log.js:244` stamps `tenant: from?.tenant ?? null`, and `from` is `logContext`'s answer, which junction fills from the call's `locals.tenantId` or `meta.tenant`. A seed, a system write and a FJS-3714 engine read run with neither, so the clinical stressor measured 85 of 527 rows with a tenant and a `@@allow(read, tenant == auth().clinicId)` reader never sees the other 442. The red test: a `@@tenant` model written under `asSystem()` with a `tenant`-column row leaves `auditTrail.tenant` null; the assertion that fails is `entry.tenant === row.<tenantField>`.
  - **A** — Fall back to the described row's own tenant column (`after ?? before` image, the model's `@@tenant` field) when the call carries none, in `audit-log.js` beside the `from?.tenant` line. Names the tenant the data belongs to, which is what a tenant's reader asks; a model with `@@tenant(none)` and a write with no row stay null. Changes the column's meaning from "the caller's tenant" (FJS-D774) to "the data's tenant when the caller has none".
  - **B** — Leave null and keep the documented limit (`trail.md` § reader). Costs a review queue silently omitting system and seed rows, which the guide already tells the host to read as `asSystem()`.
  - **C** — Stamp the data's tenant always, even when the call carries a different one, so a support write across tenants files under the tenant it touched. Wider than the row asks and loses the caller's tenant.
  - **Recommend A** — it closes the 442 rows without changing any entry that already has a tenant, and the model already knows its tenant field.
  - **Under A, settle before building** — a support write across tenants keeps the caller's tenant, so the data's own tenant reader still misses it (reopen path: C, or an `actorTenant` column); and probe that an Invariant-7-redacted image still yields the tenant scalar. A also makes `tenant` read "the caller's tenant, else the data's", so `trail.md` § reader loses its limit bullet in the same change.

- **FJS-D1746 — How does a plain-JS framework subpath get types for an app installed from the package host?** Found fixing `FJS-3720`. 22 of toolbelt's `exports` (`./inflect`, `./markdown`, `./search`, …) and css's `./themes.js` and `./vocabulary.js` are bare strings with no `types` condition, so `tsc` in an app installed from tarballs reports TS7016 (`link:` hid it by following the symlink and reading the JS). Red assertion: an app importing `@frontierjs/toolbelt/markdown` from a packed tarball typechecks with 0 TS7016. `maxNodeModuleJsDepth: 1` is ruled out (adds nine errors from junction's shipped .ts). `FJS-D1028` kept `.ts` packages source-only; these are JS kits, and toolbelt already ships hand-written `src/<kit>/<kit>.d.ts` for 15 others.
  - **A** — Hand-write `.d.ts` beside each untyped kit and add the `types` condition, the toolbelt precedent. Precise types, 24 files to write now; a hand `.d.ts` can drift from its `.js` unless a check compares them.
  - **B** — Generate them: `tsc --allowJs --declaration --emitDeclarationOnly` over the JS kits, committed beside the source, with a snapshot check that fails when the emit differs. Mostly `any` where the JS carries no JSDoc, but never wrong and no hand upkeep; a second artifact per kit.
  - **C** — One shorthand `declare module '@frontierjs/toolbelt/inflect'` list shipped in `@frontierjs/config`. One file, no per-kit work, but every import is `any`, and a shorthand wins over a real `.d.ts` so a kit that later gains types stays `any` unless it leaves the list.
  - **Recommend B** — it follows the toolbelt `.d.ts`-beside-the-kit shape without 24 hand files that can lie, and the snapshot check is the drift guard `FJS-D1028` named; hand-tighten the few kits an app leans on later.

- **FJS-D1739 — How does a correction to a `@personal` column keep its original?** Found fixing `FJS-3715`. `trailMarksOf` (`packages/litestone/src/core/trail-entries.js:24`) marks a `@personal` field `[personal]` in both `before` and `after` (Invariant 7, `FJS-D657`: the trail outlives the row, so an erased person's value must not sit in it). Red assertion: `update({ dateOfBirth })` on a `@personal(demographic)` column under `@@trail(audit)` files an entry with `before.dateOfBirth === '[personal]'` and `after.dateOfBirth === '[personal]'`, and the old date is in neither the row nor the trail. A clinical record wants "the original never changes; a correction is appended" (clinical `PLAN` Q3), which `@seals`/`@immutable` give a Note and no word gives a person's own column. Any answer that keeps the old value keeps a personal value past the row's life, so it must meet `forget()` (`FJS-D663`) and the freed-page scrub (`FJS-2099`).
  - **A** — No framework change: the app models the correction as an appended row (`PatientCorrection`, `@@seals` or `@immutable`, `@personal` on its value so `forget()` reaches it) and the policy reads the derived current value. Document the shape in `data-hazards`. Zero new nouns and erasure keeps one path, but every app rebuilds the append-and-derive pair, and the live column still re-dates grants the instant it is overwritten.
  - **B** — Digest mark: `@personal` logs `[personal:<digest>]` as `@opaque` does (`FJS-D1158`), so an entry says THAT the value moved and whether two entries agree. Small, but a date of birth has ~40k plausible values, so a digest of it is brute-forceable and defeats the redaction; it also still keeps no original. Does not answer the row.
  - **C** — A word for a column whose prior values are kept (`@personal(…, history)` or `@@history(col)`): an erasable side table that `forget()` and the scrub clear with the row. Answers the row inside the schema, but coins a noun and a second place personal values live.
  - **Recommend A** — the original-kept rule is a record-keeping policy of the clinical app (Q3 already says so), not a property of every `@personal` column, and A keeps `forget()` the single erasure path; revisit C if a second stressor wants the same pair.

- **FJS-D1740 — Should a `$audit()` event inside a transaction be held to commit like a `@@trail` write line, or file at call time?** FJS-3712 holds a write's trail line to the outermost COMMIT (`tx.queueLog`, `trail-entries.js` `emitLog`). `auditWith` (`client.js` ~7265) still does `await table.create(...)` at call time, so `sys.$transaction(async tx => { await tx.$audit({ operation: 'x', model: 'Note', records: [] }); throw new Error('refused') })` leaves the `x` line in the trail (red assertion: trail is `[]` after the rollback; today it holds `x`). But `$audit` records an EVENT, not a write: its headline use is `login.failed`, whose caller usually throws right after, and the data-hazards skill says outright "`$audit()` events are filed at call time and are not covered". FJS-D435 also fixes that it awaits and throws, which a commit-time fire cannot do.
  - **A** — Hold it to commit via `tx.queueLog`, as a write's line is. One rule for everything in the trail; but an event recorded inside a transaction that then aborts is lost (a failed login inside a refused transaction leaves no trace), the call returns the built values rather than the written row, and a commit-time write failure can no longer throw to the caller (breaks D435).
  - **B** — Keep call-time filing, ruled as intended: an event is true whether or not the transaction around it survives. Close FJS-3786 as not-a-defect and make the hazard skill and README say why `$audit` differs from a write line. Smallest; D435 stays whole.
  - **C** — Call-time by default, `$audit(entry, { heldToCommit: true })` for an event that describes a write in the same transaction. Answers both uses, but adds an option (and a noun) to a call that has had none.
  - **Recommend B** — the trail's two writers differ on purpose (write line = consequence of a write, event = the point), and holding events would lose the failed-attempt rows the call exists for; revisit C if a caller shows an event that must follow its transaction.

- **FJS-D1741 — Who is the trail actor of a conduit Receiver's apply write, and who names it: conduit automatically, or the Receiver?** FJS-3710: the card receiver's `apply` does `db.asSystem().charge.create()` and the trail row reads `actorType: 'system'` with `actorId`, `source`, `meta` null. Junction's `installLogContext` already files `requestMeta().operator` as the actor (`app.runAs(null, { operator })`, `FJS-D1534`), but `runAs(null, …)` also swaps in the app's own principal, which an `apply` that calls a service would inherit — and `FJS-D899` says conduit mints no principal. The red assertion: after a delivery of provider `card` with dedupe key `ch_x`, the `charge` trail row has `actorId 'card'`, a non-null `actorType` other than `system`, and `source` naming `ch_x`.
  - **A** — Conduit's plugin names it, no option: it wraps each route receiver's `apply` in a request-meta-only seam (operator `{ id: provider, type: 'conduit' }`, principal untouched) and a `source` of `<provider>:<dedupe key>`. Every receiver gets it free; the trail names provider and delivery. It cannot say "on behalf of the cardholder" (no subject).
  - **B** — The Receiver declares it: `actor?: (ev, { owner }) => { id, type, subject? }`, applied through the same seam. The app can file card network → cardholder; every app that omits it stays anonymous, which is today's bug.
  - **C** — Nothing new: the app's own `apply` calls `app.runAs(null, { operator: { id: 'card', type: 'conduit' } }, …)`. Actor works today, but the principal swap, no delivery id, and every receiver must remember it.
  - **Recommend A** — the provider and the delivery are facts conduit holds on every receiver and the row's harm is the anonymous default; a subject slot waits for an app that asks (then B's `actor` adds only `subject`).

- **FJS-D1743 — How does a backup/replicate destination get declared for a placed tenant's files?** Found fixing `FJS-3723`: `FJS-D1729` made `tenant-files-<place>` a copy target beside `tenant-files` (`cli.js` `tenantStorage`), so `backup` and `replicate` carry the EU place's bytes to the one default root/target, and `--zip` makes them one archive. The row's acceptance (*skipped unless the destination is declared for it; export/studio name it*) needs a way to say which destination a place may go to, and no ruling gives one. Red test: a fleet with `tenancy { place eu "…" }` and a tenant `meta.place='eu'`; `litestone backup` today writes `backups/<stamp>/tenant-files-eu/<id>.db`; it must not. Row strategy (`clinical`, one `app.db`) has no place, so it stays out of reach under every option.
  - **A** — Skipped by default, opted in per run: `backup`, `replicate` and the default `restore` leave every `tenant-files-<place>` (and its trail, `FJS-D1727`) out; `backup --place eu <dest>` carries only that place to the `dest` the operator names, `replicate --place eu` to the target in that run's config. No schema keyword; the declaration is the operator's flag, so nothing stops `--place eu` from naming a US bucket. `export` and `studio` print the tenant's place.
  - **B** — Declared beside the place in the seed: `tenancy { place eu "data/eu" backup "backups/eu" }` (a local dir or a bucket name). The default backup/replicate skip the place; a place's own destination receives only its files, and `--zip` is refused across places. Invariant 6 (boundaries live in the schema) and the acceptance's word *declared*, at the price of one more keyword and a destination noun per place (`FJS-D1488` already names a BackupDestination).
  - **C** — No enforcement: keep the copy paths as they are, document that a place is a directory, and have `backup`/`replicate`/`export`/`studio` print the place of every tenant they touch. Nothing to build beyond the notice; residency stays the operator's.
  - **Recommend A** — it ends the silent carry at the price of two flags and no schema word; B is the stricter shape and is added later by making `--place`'s dest a declared one, once an app asks for the guarantee (price against the simpler version first).

- **FJS-D1767 — How does a policy compare a `String @date` column to the clock?** FJS-3705: probed on `Patient { dob String @date, @@allow(read, dob > now() - 16yr) }`. It is accepted and compares the day text against the instant text, so it is right a day away from the boundary and wrong ON it (`'2010-11-01' >= '2010-11-01T09:…'` is false, `<` true; the day answer is the reverse). `today()` is a parse error (`Expected RPAREN`). The app therefore keeps `dateOfBirth` a `DateTime` against `FJS-D288`. Red test: `dob >= now() - 16yr` over a row whose dob is the cutoff's day reads false where the day reading is true, SQL and JS halves alike.
  - **A** — A day-valued `today()` (and `today() - 16yr`, shifting in calendar years/months/days) in the policy language, bound as `YYYY-MM-DD` in UTC unless the client names a `timeZone`; `now()` against a `@date` column is refused by name, pointing at `today()`. One new noun, the compare stays honest, and the zone is stated at the one crossing `FJS-D288` names.
  - **B** — No new word: when `now()`/a shift meets a `String @date` column the binder truncates the instant to its UTC day (SQL and JS halves both). Nothing to learn, but the zone crossing is silent and fixed at UTC, which is the `FJS-1234` hazard in a smaller place.
  - **C** — Refuse the compare by name (`@date` column against an instant, pointing at a `DateTime`), and keep a birthday an instant column. Smallest build, and it ratifies the app's workaround against `FJS-D288`.
  - **Recommend A** — a policy that compares a day should say it is comparing a day and which zone made it; B hides the crossing and C leaves D288's own column kind unusable in the one place a birthday rule lives.

- **FJS-D1768 — How does the schema answer the `litestone advise` rules baselined at 7 basecamp, 1 haven and 2 example (FJS-3816)?** Each is a design call, not an omission; `fli check` ceilings fall only when they are answered. Four sub-questions, ruled together so the schema edits land once:
  - **(1) `Invitation.token`** is `@secret @hashed` (`FJS-D1608`) and required, so no create below level 8 can supply it (advise: error). A `@default` is out: the service must hold the plaintext to put in the accept link, and a generated default is never seen. **A** — `@system`, and `invitations.create`/`resend` name `system: ['token']` on the caller's own client instead of `sys().invitation.create`; keeps the `@@gate(5)` and row policies and the audit actor, drops the `asSystem()` that today wraps the whole create. **B** — leave `asSystem()` as it is and mark the rule accepted (advise has no suppression; the baseline holds it). **C** — make `token` optional; wrong, a row with no token is an invitation nobody can accept.
  - **(2) The `role` columns** under a per-model gate: `WorkspaceMember.role` (gate 5, advise error), `Invitation.role` (5), `Server.role` (4), `AppEditor.role` (5), haven `Member.role` (3, error). `WorkspaceMember` already has `@@deny(update, userId == auth().id)` and `refuseGrantAboveOwn` in `core/hooks.ts`; a FIELD `@allow(write, …)` is evaluated against the payload, not the stored row (`FJS-433`), so it can only compare the caller's level to a constant. **A** — `@allow(write, auth().level >= N)` on each column, N = the level that may hand out that role today (owner, 6, for WorkspaceMember/Invitation role; the gate for Server/AppEditor); one more door closed at the Data boundary. **B** — `A` for the two errors (WorkspaceMember, Member) only, and for the three `warn` ones declare `@@auth` on the identity model so advise can decide whether the column grades a ladder at all. **C** — leave the columns; the hook plus `@@deny` hold the door.
  - **(3) `Flow.workspaceId`** (the orion `extend model Flow`) is an unindexed foreign key. **A** — `@@index([workspaceId])` in the extend, one migration across basecamp and example. No design in this one; it waits here only because the row bundled it.
  - **(4) `Variable.environmentId` picker** and **`ProductVariant.id`** (example, two rules). Variable's create `@@allow` reads `environment.isProtected` while `Environment` has no read `@@allow`, so a picker lists rows the write refuses: **A** — an `optionsQuery where: isProtected == false || auth().level >= 5` on the field; **B** — a read `@@allow` on `Environment`, which hides protected environments from developers who deploy to them (wrong). ProductVariant: **A** — `id String @id @default(uuid())` so an offline `InventoryMovement` can name it; **B** — keep the server id (stock is minted online only).
  - **A** — every sub-recommendation, as one bundle: (1) A, `token` `@system` and the `asSystem()` dropped; (2) B, `@allow(write, auth().level >= N)` on `WorkspaceMember.role` and haven `Member.role`, and `@@auth` on the identity model for the three warns; (3) A, the index; (4) A and A, the `optionsQuery` and the client-minted `uuid`. One schema pass across the three apps.
  - **B** — the hooks stay: (1) B and (2) C, so `asSystem()`, `refuseGrantAboveOwn` and `@@deny` keep holding those doors, and only (3) A and (4) A and A land. The baseline keeps the accepted rules, since advise has no suppression.
  - **Recommend A** — each moves a check from a hook or a convention into the schema (Invariant 6) at the smallest edit, and (1)(2) shrink the baselines from 7/1/2 toward 1/0/1 without a suppression mechanism. A sub-question ruled apart from the bundle is an Other answer naming it. Confidence 2: (2)'s `@@auth` half is the least probed.
- **FJS-D1131 — Is a foreign wire format the plugin's alone, or may the schema point at it?** Measured in vaultwarden Phase 1 (`fjs-prototypes/vaultwarden`, `bw` 2026.10.0 logging in against the real app): the Bitwarden client needs `Type: 2`, `RevisionDate` and `Object: "cipher"`, names and values fixed by a client somebody else wrote. `@map` renames the column and nothing a caller touches, `@label` is a human title, and an enum member carries no value. Nine raw routes in one app plugin served it with no service touched. Of the four workarounds, the request scope (`FJS-3104`) and TLS (`FJS-3105`) are fixed; what is left is the OAuth error body (`invalid_grant` with `error_description`, 400), which the plugin writes in a `try/catch` around `auth.login`, a second thrown-value-to-HTTP translator (Invariant 4). Probed 2026-11-02: a throw from any route handler already reaches `errorResponse` (`packages/junction/src/transport/bridge.ts:646`), which writes `fe.toJSON()`, and `sanitizeError` passes a status under 500 unchanged, so a `FrameworkError` subclass with its own `toJSON()` comes out as written.
  - **A** — the plugin's alone, and its `catch` stays: the plugin's codec owns names and values, and Invariant 4 names a raw route speaking a foreign protocol as the exception. Cost: a written exception to an invariant.
  - **B** — the plugin's alone, through the one owner: the codec owns names and values, and the plugin turns `auth.login`'s refusal into its own `OAuthError extends BadRequest` whose `toJSON()` is the OAuth body, thrown for `errorResponse` to write. No new seam, no exception, and nothing in the schema. Cost: the docs teach a `toJSON()` override as the way a plugin speaks a foreign error dialect.
  - **C** — the schema points at it: a column and enum-member attribute naming the foreign name and value (`@wire('RevisionDate')`, `Login @wire(2)`) read by a generated serializer, plus B for errors. Cost: one vendor's format in the origin every audience reads, and a column can speak only one foreign dialect.
  - **Recommend B** — the schema stays the origin of the app's own shape, and the foreign format is a translation at the edge. That is how the protocol's own servers do it: vaultwarden's `Cipher` stores `atype` and `updated_at` and its `to_json` writes `"type"` and `"revisionDate"` ([cipher.rs](https://github.com/dani-garcia/vaultwarden/blob/main/src/db/models/cipher.rs), read 2026-11-02), and Bitwarden's own server answers through response models apart from its entities (`CipherResponseModel`, [bitwarden/server](https://github.com/bitwarden/server)). B keeps Invariant 4 at one owner with what Junction already does. Reopen C when a second foreign-protocol plugin restates the same columns. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1856 — Does a raw or stream route resolve `createApp({ principal })`, and if so where does the resolved claim set and scoped client live?** Found fixing FJS-3977: `resolveAppClaims` runs only in `withLitestoneDb` (`core/litestone.ts:4125`), an around hook of `callService`; `transport/http.ts` builds a `TransportContext` with `user` from `resolvePrincipal` (session/credential) and never touches the app `principal`. A device key in `Authorization: Bearer` on `PUT /api/probe/stream` and `/plain` left `ctx.user` null, `ctx.locals` undefined, and answered 200 for a dead key. The guide (`AGENTS.md` § Who the caller is) says claims resolve "on every request"; § raw route says it runs no hook pipeline and no gate. Red test: a `bearerClaim` app, revoked key on a raw `app.put` route; today the handler runs, the assertion that fails is a 401.
  - **A** — the transport resolves `principal` for a raw route before the handler: it builds the callDb/locals the resolver reads, refuses (401) on a thrown resolver, and hands the claims to the handler as a new `ctx.claims` (and optionally a scoped client). Closes the silent pass, but adds a `TransportContext` field and a second caller of `resolveAppClaims` beside `withLitestoneDb` (Invariant 4: one owner), and runs a DB read on every raw route including webhooks that carry no bearer.
  - **B** — a raw route opts in with one exported helper (`app.principalFor(ctx)` / `resolveCaller(ctx)`) that builds the hand-made context the row's workaround builds, returns `{ claims, db }`, and throws 401 for a dead key. The owner stays single (the helper calls `resolveAppClaims`), raw routes that need no claims pay nothing; the cost is that forgetting to call it still passes silently, as today.
  - **C** — docs only: AGENTS.md says a raw route resolves no principal and tells the handler to call a service for anything touching a model (the service pipeline resolves the bearer there), and "on every request" is narrowed to "on every service call". Smallest edit; a stream route that must authenticate before reading a body still has no way short of the hand-built context.
  - **Recommend B** — a raw route is by contract outside the pipeline, so A silently changes what every webhook pays, while C leaves the stream-upload case (auth before the body is read) with only a workaround; B gives one named owner call and the guide sentence narrows to say a raw route opts in. `decision-rules` agrees: the export plugin's `resolveCaller` (`plugins/export/index.ts:163`) is already this hand-built copy (fake service context, `PRINCIPAL_RESOLVER`/`TENANT_REGISTRY` off the app, `withLitestoneDb`/`withTenantDb`), so B lifts it beside `resolveAppClaims` in `core/litestone.ts` (returning `{ client, principal }`), points the exporter at it, and adds a test that a raw route and a service see the same rows under a claim (precedent `FJS-D230`). A fails the concept budget (new `TransportContext` field, reverses "a raw route has no pipeline"); reopen as a lazy getter over B if a third caller wants `ctx.claims`.

- **FJS-D1857 — When a kind tier crowds a required belongsTo out of an unaided `columns()` list, does the ranking rank the parent ahead of the state tier, or does the list pin its columns?** Found fixing FJS-3974: the uncommitted `Deployment.swap` enum (`FJS-D1846`) joined `columnList`'s state tier (`packages/sierra/src/resource/field-rules.js` 1261), so basecamp's unaided `/deployments/` list ranks `status, trigger, swap, queuedAt, startedAt, finishedAt` into its limit of 6 and omits `appId` as "beyond the 6-column limit (ranked rest)". The row has no `td[data-col="appId"]`, and verify.mjs:2526 (`…and the app cell still names the app after the push`) reads `""` every run while the API's find carries `app`. The page's own header says taking the ranking unaided is its point.
  - **A** — Pin the list: `columns: { only: [...] }` on `Deployment.mesa` (Invoice's shape, `example/web/src/resources/Invoice.mesa:38`) or on the page. One caller moves, no convention; basecamp loses its one unaided table, and a later column stays invisible until someone edits `only`.
  - **B** — Amend `FJS-D243`: a required belongsTo ranks in a *parent* tier between identify and state, read off the `relation` kind `tierOf` already owns. Every unaided list (crud-templates' pages, home-template, basecamp's apps list) names its parent; proved by a `packages/sierra/test/column-list.test.js` case and verify.mjs:2526 unchanged.
  - **C** — Leave the list unaided and move the drive's push-keeps-include check elsewhere. Fails: no relation cell survives the ranking (`triggeredBy` is optional and ranks rest), so the list shows releases with no app and the include check guards nothing.
  - **Recommend B** — a child row is identified by its parent, and Deployment would be the second pin of the same crowding Invoice already pinned around; `decision-rules` passes A and B and fails C on derivation and enforcement.

- **FJS-D1923 — How does a row declare that it waits on an upstream release, so fix:loop passes over it rather than ranking it ready?** Found fixing FJS-4028: its fix is Caddy 2.12's `stream_detached` (PR #7649, milestone v2.12.0), and the latest release is v2.11.9 with no v2.12 tag or prerelease (probed twice on 2026-11-05). Its links cell said "Blocked on the Caddy release" in prose, which `BLOCKED_BY` (`packages/cli/core/next.js:92`) rightly ignores, so `fli next --json` ranked it under `ready` and `fix:loop` (`scripts/fix-loop.mjs` reads `.ready` only) paid a session to re-probe it each run. No other open row waits on an outside release. For now it waits on this ruling by `blocked by`.
  - **A** — the existing `by hand` segment. No new marker, and `fix:loop` passes over it. But `by hand` means "a person can finish it now" (a long run, an owner-only check), so the row stays in `fli next`'s ready list and sends a reader to a row with nothing to build.
  - **B** — a new links-cell segment `waits on: caddy v2.12`, read in `rankNext` beside `BLOCKED_BY`/`ENV_SEGMENT`: a row carrying it goes under `blocked`, and `blockedLine` prints it as `waits on caddy v2.12`. One regex and a case in the next test. Nothing notices when the release ships: unlike `blocked by FJS-###`, an outside blocker never closes itself, so someone has to remove the segment.
  - **C** — leave the prose. Fails: the loop re-picks the row every run.
  - **Recommend B** — the row really is blocked, not owner-only, and the house pattern is a declared segment, not prose (next.js § Blocking is declared). `decision-rules` passes B and fails A on predictability. Fall back to A if a second outside-release row never appears.

- **FJS-D1932 — Does caravan learn job dependencies (dispatch names a predecessor), or does a staged pipeline stay the app's own handler chain?** Found fixing FJS-3993: `DispatchOptions` (`packages/caravan/src/types.ts:295–345`) takes queue, delay, priority, unique, actor, tenant, id and nothing naming a predecessor, and `claimNext` (`db.ts:526`) picks any `pending` row with `run_at <= now`. Immich's per-asset stages (metadata, then thumbnail, preview, transcode) are therefore one `derive` job with an `only` payload, so caravan's retry reruns the whole pipeline and the queue reports one job. A red test would dispatch B `after: A`, fail A to dead, and assert B never ran; it needs a column and a claim predicate that do not exist, so it is not written until the shape is ruled.
  - **A** — `dispatch(job, data, { after: jobId })`: a nullable `after_id` column (migration like `unique_key`), and `claimNext` skips a row whose predecessor is not `done`. If the predecessor goes `dead` or `cancelled`, the dependent is cancelled with the reason `predecessor <id> <status>`, so `queue.state()` reports the stage that broke. Smallest surface, one option; a fan-in (wait on two) is not expressible.
  - **B** — a flow primitive: `queue.flow([a, b, c])` dispatches the stages in one transaction, each waiting on the one before, and returns the ids. The same column underneath, but the option is not public, so an app cannot build a cycle or name an id that never existed. A new noun (`flow`) for a shape that is one option wide.
  - **C** — nothing in caravan: the app's `derive` handler dispatches the next stage as its last act (a stage's `done` is the trigger), which is the immich workaround one stage per job instead of one job for all. No schema change; each stage retries and reports on its own, and a deterministic `dispatch({ id })` for the next stage is a no-op on replay (`FJS-D35`), so a crash reruns the stage rather than losing the chain (read, not probed: probe it before ruling). The order lives in handler code, a broken stage leaves later stages nonexistent rather than `cancelled`, and a request cannot enqueue the whole pipeline atomically.
  - **A's obligations if chosen** (`decision-rules`): `dispatch` refuses an unknown `after` id by name (so no cycle, which removes B's one advantage); a purged or cleaned-up predecessor counts as unsatisfied, not done; cleanup spares a `done` predecessor with live dependents; dead/cancelled cascades transitively in the same transaction as `markFailed`/`cancel`/`purge`; `retry` of the predecessor does not revive cancelled dependents; `anyPending` (`db.ts:648`) agrees with the claim predicate; a dependent waits on a predecessor in a paused queue. Fan-out is expressible, fan-in (two predecessors) is not and would need a join table.
  - **Recommend C, reopen as A** — `decision-rules` (confidence 2): immich is the only named consumer and its stated harm (retry reruns the whole pipeline) is solved by one job per stage; document the pattern (deterministic `id` per stage, dispatch the successor as the last act) in `packages/caravan/CLAUDE.md`. Reopen as A when a second app needs to see or cancel a pipeline from the queue. B loses either way.

- **FJS-D1933 — When a local FileStorage's publicBase names no path under the API origin, does junction refuse it, rewrite it to the mount, or serve under it?** Found fixing FJS-4006: `mountLocalFiles` (`packages/junction/src/transport/local-files.ts:40`) does `store.config.publicBase ??= <origin><apiPrefix>/files`, so a base the app or `FILES_PUBLIC_BASE` named wins, and `https://api.example` mints `https://api.example/Doc/resume/<key>.pdf?exp=…&sig=…`, which the router answers 405 (the route is `GET <apiPrefix>/files/*`). The red test: `appWith('/api')`, `process.env.FILES_PUBLIC_BASE = 'https://app.example'`, create a Doc, read it, and the URL's path must start `/api/files/` (it starts `/Doc/`). Not written until ruled, since A and B assert opposite things. The comment above `mountLocalFiles` promises a named base is kept, because a proxy or CDN mapping `https://cdn.example/*` onto the mount is a legitimate deploy the signed URL still reaches.
  - **A** — refuse at boot: a base whose path is empty (`/`) throws naming `FILES_PUBLIC_BASE` and the mount path. Loud, and the proxy deploy writes the mount path into its base (`https://cdn.example/api/files`) at the cost of a proxy rule it may not have.
  - **B** — rewrite: a bare-origin base becomes `<base><apiPrefix>/files`, the same as no base. Silent and fixes immich's line, but a proxy-at-root deploy gets its URLs changed under it, and the 405 returns the moment the proxy strips the prefix.
  - **C** — serve under the minted base: also mount `GET <base path>/*` when the base's path differs from the mount. A bare origin gives `/*`, which captures every GET, so the route would need a `<Model>/<field>/` shape match; the most code and a second public route.
  - **Recommend A** — a bare origin is a misconfiguration far more often than a root proxy (immich carried it from the docs' old spelling), and A fails at boot where B hides it. A proxy-at-root deploy can write the mount path into its base. Confidence 2.

- **FJS-D1971 — Should a workspace role manage Apps and Domains without Servers, or does the admin-minted scoped key (FJS-D1896) stay the only way?** Found fixing FJS-4024: no `WorkspaceRole` manages Apps and Domains in its own workspace while refused Servers. `developer` cannot delete an App (`@@gate(2.4.4.5)`, `schema.lite:1557`) or write a Domain (`@@gate(2.5)`, `schema.lite:1803`), yet `ROLE_GRANTS` gives `developer` `Server.create`/`update`/`reboot` (`core/capabilities.ts:52`), and `admin` gets Servers too. `FJS-D1896` = A routes the charter-to-live run through a scoped key, so nothing waits on this; what is left is whether the roles themselves should change for every workspace.
  - **A** — change nothing: the scoped key (`apps:*` + `domains:*`, refused `/servers` by `domain/api-key/scopes.ts`) is the answer. Scope is enforced at the call, not the Data boundary (Invariant 6), but only keys carry it; a human user in the workspace still cannot hold "Apps and Domains, no Servers". Close `FJS-4024` by this ruling.
  - **B** — lower App delete to `2.4.4.4`, Domain writes to `2.4.4.4`, and take `Server.create`/`update`/`reboot` off `developer` in `ROLE_GRANTS`. `developer` then manages Apps and Domains and no machines, enforced at the Data boundary for users and keys alike. Cost: a schema gate change is a deploy question (`data-hazards` migrations-and-release), and it narrows what `developer` can do today — a workspace that provisions Servers as `developer` loses it.
  - **C** — add a new `WorkspaceRole` between `developer` and `admin` that carries Apps and Domains without Servers. Fits the run exactly, but coins a noun and a rung for one consumer the scoped key already serves.
  - **Recommend A** — the one consumer (the charter run) is served by D1896's key; no human has asked for "Apps without Servers", and B narrows `developer` for every workspace on a single probe. Reopen as B when a real workspace needs it.

- **FJS-D1972 — Where does the byte-capped body read live now that conduit's `readBytes` and junction's `readCapped` are one loop, and does conduit's hop-walk stay its own?** Found fixing FJS-4045: `readBytes` (`packages/conduit/src/transports/http.ts:950`) and `readCapped` (`packages/junction/src/core/public-url.ts:254`) are the same stream loop, differing in the error built (`ResponseTooLargeError` vs `PublicFetchError`, a 400) and a `content-length` pre-check only junction has. The hop-walks are NOT copies: `fetchPublicJson` follows any public host graded per hop, GET only, JSON only, 64 KiB/5 s/3 hops; conduit's `dispatch` follows same-origin only, opt-in, credentialed, any method (a 301/302/303 rewrite is refused), any content type, 10 MiB/5 hops. Routing conduit through `fetchPublicJson` would take a flag per row of that table. The red test: a toolbelt (or junction) test of `readCapped(res, limit, onOver)` with an oversized chunked body and an oversized `content-length`, both calling `onOver`; it is not written until the owner is ruled.
  - **A** — lift `readCapped(res, limit, onOver)` into toolbelt (a new kit, e.g. `/bounded`); both callers pass their own error builder. Toolbelt is already a dependency of conduit and junction. Cost: the package's license is "every export is a pure function, no I/O" (`FJS-D26`), and this one drains a caller's socket stream; the kits that touch a stream (`/share`, `/frontier`) only read an in-memory `Blob`.
  - **B** — export `readCapped` from `@frontierjs/junction/public-url` and let conduit import it dynamically beside `gradePublicUrl`, where it already loads that module for `address_from: 'request'` targets. No purity question and one owner beside the bounded fetch (`FJS-D1947`). Cost: conduit's `readBytes` runs on every response, so a junction import on every send breaks "a conduit with no such target never imports junction" (`http.ts:428`), and `peerDependencies` junction is not optional.
  - **C** — leave both loops; close `FJS-4045` as "different nouns by design" and add the `dispatch` comment saying why it does not call `fetchPublicJson`. Cost: a second cap loop (~30 lines) that must be fixed twice, and conduit keeps no `content-length` pre-check.
  - **Hop-walk, under any of A–C** — stays in conduit. Calling `fetchPublicJson` for it is refused: it would need method, body, headers, content type, same-origin and credential flags, which is a second transport inside the first.
  - **Recommend A** — `decision-rules` (confidence 2) picked a lift to toolbelt, but did not weigh the purity rule; if the owner holds toolbelt to it strictly, take C over B, since B costs every conduit send a junction import for ~30 lines. Reopen as A or B when a third caller of the loop appears (`junction/src/transport/body.ts:183` already has one).

- **FJS-D2011 — Does `basecamp:key` take a `--workspace` flag to name the workspace when one holder owns several, or stay interactive?** `holders()` answers one row per owner/admin seat, and `mint` needs the seat's `workspaceId` (`--tenant`), so one email owning chartered and ela gives two picks under `--as`; with no terminal `pickHolder` refuses (`Several could hold it — pass --as`) and `--as` cannot say which. The red test: a holder with two seats and `process.stdin.isTTY` false returns `{ error }` today. FJS-4085's second half, the menu label cut at `cols()` (`core/tty.js` `footerText` truncates the whole one-line prompt), rides on the same ruling: with the email constant, the label is the workspace alone.
  - **A** — `--workspace <name>`: filters the picks after `--as`/ME, a name matching none lists those that exist, and the menu shows `role of workspace` with the email dropped when every pick shares it. One flag, same shape as `--as`.
  - **B** — `--as` takes `email` or `email:workspace`. No new flag, but a second grammar inside one string, and an email with a colon-free workspace name is the only case it handles.
  - **C** — No flag; non-interactive stays a refusal and the error names the workspaces. Leaves a scripted mint on a multi-workspace box impossible, which is the case the row was found in.
  - **Recommend A** — the picked thing is a seat, a seat is (email, workspace), and a flag per axis is how `--as` already reads; B hides the second axis in a string.

- **FJS-D2046 — Where does a refused `/replicate` send leave its finding on the Server?** Found fixing `FJS-4127`: Outpost's refusal ("still replicates a, b, and this list would stop all of it") reaches Basecamp only as the thrown message of a failed `replicate:sync` job (`replicate-sync.job.ts` `syncReplicas`, `reply.error`). Nothing on the Server keeps it, and `Server.replication` cannot, because every heartbeat rewrites it. The red test's assertion: after `syncReplicas` against an executor answering `{ error }` for a list missing an entry the machine holds, the Server carries a finding naming that entry, and a later accepted send clears it.
  - **A** — a `@system` `Server.replicaRefusal Json?` (`{ message, entries, at }`), written by the job on a refusal and nulled on the next accepted send, graded by `posture.ts` as a control (`backup.replica-list`, high) beside `backup.replica-lag`. One column and one migration; the finding is a row the `/hub/security/` screen and `fli basecamp:posture` already draw.
  - **B** — a notification (`ReplicaListRefused`, as `AppBackupFailed` is) sent from the job's failure path, nothing stored. No schema change, but the finding is a message and not a row, so it does not clear when the list is fixed and the posture screen stays green.
  - **C** — an `Alert` through a built-in `AlertRule` with a new `AlertSubject`. Reuses fan-out and resolve, but a rule's condition is columns evaluated on a sweep (`alerting.ts`), and a refusal is an event with no series to evaluate.
  - **Recommend A** — the plan says every finding gets a row and the refusal's cause persists until a send is accepted, which is exactly a column plus a control; B cannot clear and C has no condition to hold. The other two halves of the row (a `unique`-dropped person's `clear`, and a throwing send losing its `clear` on retry) are independent of this answer and go in the same change. Grade it in `server-postures.service.ts`'s per-beat list beside `gradeReplication`, not the six-hourly report, so a standing refusal never ages into `unknown`; confirm Outpost's refusal text carries no credential before storing it verbatim (Invariant 7), and record once rather than let the job retry a deterministic refusal five times.

- **FJS-D2047 — Who frees a branch port block when all five are held by worktrees that are checked out but idle — the lane, the claimant, or nobody?** Found fixing `FJS-3804` (sibling `FJS-3270`): `freeBranch` (`packages/cli/core/ports.js`) already drops an entry whose worktree has no `.git`, and `MAX_BRANCH = 5` is a deliberate ceiling (a sixth puts 8120 at 68120). A fix-loop lane (`~/.fli/lanes/frontierjs-N`) is a worktree that outlives its row, so each one keeps its block while no server runs, and with agent worktrees and hand-made ones the five go. The register line cannot go red today (the lock holds 2 live branch entries); the assertion a test would make is that `portFor` from a sixth live-but-idle worktree returns a block instead of throwing "All 5 branch port blocks".
  - **A** — the lane frees its own: `scripts/lanes.mjs` calls `releaseSession(<lane path>)` when a row ends, so a lane holds a block only while a drive runs. The number is recomputed on the next claim; no change to `ports.js`.
  - **B** — the claimant evicts: when `freeBranch` finds all five held, it takes the block of an entry whose recorded `pid` is dead (`isProcessAlive`), and that worktree is given a new number at its next claim. Covers agent and hand-made worktrees too, but a worktree's number then changes between runs.
  - **C** — nobody: the refusal stays, its message names the holders, and `fli ports:status --clean` is the remedy.
  - **Recommend A** — the lane is the one holder the loop itself creates and knows the end of, so it releases what it claimed (the owner of the claim is the owner of the release, as `fli dev` does); B moves a number under a worktree that did nothing wrong.
